Archie screwed the cups back onto Bev's thermos, set it down by the wall, and went to the back of the hall, because he needed, for a few minutes, to not be looking at the trumpet.

Chief Dale Pinkham was standing by the door to the stairs in his uniform jacket, with his hands in his pockets and the expression of a man who had been told he could stay if he was quiet.

"Archie," he said reverently. "The riverboat."

So they talked about the riverboat. The chief's theory involved a second, identical riverboat and the Army Corps of Engineers, and it was wrong in every particular and so lovingly reasoned that Archie couldn't bring himself to say so. While they talked, he watched the stage from a long way off.

Up there in the dimness a dozen people were doing a dozen things. Hollis, in a pair of the crew's black stage gloves, lit the candles; the town's open-flame permit was in her name. Kev was taking pictures of the table with his phone until Teddy's voice came out of the wing — *Phones away, please, sir, that's in your contract* — and Kev pocketed it and went to stand by the radiator, looking guilty. Warren Gilley went past Archie and the chief and down the stairs to the men's room, very fast, holding his stomach. "Nerves," said the chief, with sympathy. A boy from the crew knelt to tape down a cable. Hollis went round the circle with a tray, setting a glass of water at every place, and when Teddy called from the wing for her to slide the horn away from the candle, she slid it over on her way off. Vivienne was at her chair, arranging a black shawl over the back of it with great care. Shannon sat alone at her place in the candlelight, turning her ring.

At seven fifty-five, Archie left the chief to his riverboat and went up to his mother.

"You don't have to do this," he said quietly.

Vivienne looked up at him from her chair. In the candlelight, in her black velvet and pearls, she looked beautiful, and seventy-three.

"Neither do you, darling," she said.

He sat down beside her. After a moment she put her hand over his on the table, and he turned his hand over and held hers.

At seven fifty-eight, Lorelei Crane came back up the stairs from the green room with her daughter behind her. She'd redone her mouth; it was darker and glossier than before, almost black in the candlelight. She smelled of menthol. She took her place at the head of the table, upstage, facing the empty dark of the hall and the little glowing window of the light booth high at the back, and she smiled around the circle at each of them in turn.

"Hands, please, everybody," she said softly. "Left hand under, right hand over. Don't let go, no matter what you feel. The spirits don't like a broken circle."

Archie took his mother's hand on one side. On the other, he took Kev's, which was damp and trembling, and which had, a moment before, been fumbling with something in the pouch pocket of his hoodie. Across the table Shannon put her hand in Lorelei's like someone putting her hand into a fire. Ivy took her mother's left without looking at her.

"Thank you, Hollis," Lorelei called into the dark. "Whenever you're ready, my dear."

High at the back of the hall, in the booth, a small shape lifted a hand.

One by one the candles were pinched out, until there was only a single dim red bulb glowing on a stand beside the table, like the light in a darkroom.

Then the house lights went out.


---

# Chapter 8

## The Other Side

The dark in the Merrow Opera House was not like other darks.

Archie had sat in a lot of them. Dark was his business. He had been sealed in boxes and safes and coffins and once, memorably, a hollowed-out Steinway, and he knew that most darkness had a kind of grain to it — a little gray seeping under a door, a phone glowing in somebody's pocket three rows back, the ghost of an exit sign. Darkness was usually just light that hadn't been invited in.

This was the real thing. Marchand Media's drapes had swallowed the windows, and Hollis had killed every light in the hall, and for a moment after the house lights went the only thing left in the world was the small red bulb on its stand by the table, glowing like a coal. It lit nothing. It made a little red smear in the air and gave the dark something to press against.

He could hear everything. The radiators, knocking. The old building creaking in the wind off the harbor. Kev Duchesne breathing through his mouth beside him, fast and shallow. His mother's rings against his knuckles. Somewhere out in the hall, very faint, the electric hum of the cameras.

"Let us join our hearts," said Lorelei Crane.

Her voice came out of the dark across the table, low and warm and rich as a cello, and Archie felt the whole circle lean toward it.

She prayed. It wasn't a prayer to anything in particular — it was to *those who have gone before*, and *the loving ones on the other side*, and *anyone who has a message for those gathered here tonight*, and it went on long enough that Archie's eyes stopped straining for light and gave up and let the dark in. That was the purpose of it. He'd done a whole segment once on the opening prayer: two minutes to let the sitters' eyes adjust to nothing, and their hearts adjust to hope.

Then the red bulb went out, and Lorelei's voice changed.

It dropped half an octave and slowed down and grew a faint, unplaceable accent — Welsh, maybe, or Bible. "*Good evening, my children.*"

Kev's hand jumped in his.

"Brother Amos is with us," Lorelei said, in her own voice again, softly delighted. "He's my guide. He'll be helping tonight. Brother Amos, there's a lot of love in this room."

"*There is a great deal of love,*" agreed Brother Amos, "*and a great deal of doubt.*"

That got a nervous laugh from somebody — Warren, Archie thought. He didn't laugh. He was counting. *Opening prayer. Control spirit. Now the raps.*

The raps came on cue: three sharp knocks, seemingly from inside the table itself, then three more. *Knee,* thought Archie. *Or toe against the table leg. Or a little clicker in her shoe.* He listened for the direction and couldn't pin it down, and was pleased with her for that, professionally.

Then, beside him, Vivienne gasped.

The hand bell had begun to ring.

It rang softly at first, a single silver note, and then again, and then it was moving — he could hear it travel across the tabletop, ringing as it went, and rise, ringing, into the air above them, and circle there.

*Somebody has a hand free,* he thought. *Two hands, probably. One of them is hers.*

He could feel the circle all the way around through his own two hands — his mother's dry, cool fingers gripping hard on his left; Kev's hot, damp ones on his right. He couldn't feel past them. Nobody could. That was the beauty of the circle. Everybody was absolutely certain of the two hands they held, and nobody knew a thing about anybody else's.

The bell dropped to the table with a clank. Something cold and wet and soft brushed across Archie's forehead and was gone.

Across the table, Warren Gilley gave a high, short yelp, like a dog stepped on.

Vivienne laughed — a startled, delighted peal of laughter in the dark, the laugh of a woman who had held the flashlight for her father at eleven séances and had just been touched on the cheek by a wet glove on a stick for the first time in fifty years.

"*There's no need to be frightened,*" said Brother Amos reprovingly.

"I'm not frightened," said Vivienne. "I'm nostalgic."

Kev made a strangled sound. From the stage-right wing, very faintly, came Teddy Marchand's murmur to somebody on his headset — *static, static, we've got the static, love it, love it* — and Kev's damp fingers squeezed Archie's so hard the knuckles ground together.

"It's the Crane Static," Kev breathed. "That's the — the spirits interfere with the cameras. It happens every time she gets close."

*Every time,* thought Archie. *On cue. Like a man flipping a switch.*

He didn't say it. He'd promised.

And then, in the dark above the table, a light began to rise.

It was three thin bands of pale green, glowing faintly, one above another. The trumpet. It lifted off the table slowly and hung there, and then began to float — up and away to the left, turning as it went, the bands tilting and swimming against the absolute black like something seen at the bottom of the sea.

It was beautiful. Archie had seen it done fifty times and explained it on television twenty, and it was still beautiful. *It works,* Gus had written, *because nobody in the room wants it not to.*

The trumpet stopped, high over the place where Shannon was sitting. It tilted toward her.

"*Shan,*" it said.

The voice came out of the air. It was a man's voice — or a woman's voice pitched low and roughened, coming down through two feet of aluminum cone — and it was hoarse and young and tired, and it had a Maine flatness in the vowels that Archie hadn't heard Lorelei use once in two days.

Shannon Mayo made a sound Archie had never heard a grown woman make.

"*Shan. It's me.*"

"Tommy?"

"*It's warm here, Shan. It's warm. The water's warm. I'm not cold anymore.*"

"Oh, God. Oh, Tommy—"

"*Tell the kids. Tell Liam I see him on the wharf. Tell Rosie—*"

Archie sat in the dark holding his mother's hand and a stranger's and felt it rise in him, the thing he had felt at nineteen in a church basement in Somerville, watching a medium tell an old man his dead wife forgave him and take forty dollars for it. He wanted to stand up. He wanted to drop Kev's hand and shout for the lights and walk around the table in the glare and lift Lorelei Crane's free hand up off the table and hold it up for the cameras and the crew and Merrow, Maine, to see.

He had promised Shannon he wouldn't humiliate her.

The voice in the trumpet was telling her about the kids. She was crying so hard she could barely breathe. If he did it now — if he flooded this room with light at this exact moment — the whole town would remember forever that Shannon Mayo had been sobbing to a tin horn when the lights came up.

He held on to the hands. He stayed in the dark.

"*I love you, Shan. I'll be waiting.*"

The green bands drifted away from Shannon, wavering. They hung for a moment in the middle of the air over the table. Then the voice changed again.

It was older now. Deeper. It had a crack in it and a laugh at the bottom of it, and it said:

"*Viv.*"

His mother's hand went rigid in his.

"*Vivi. Is that you, honey?*"

Vivienne didn't answer. Archie could feel her breathing, short and hard.

"*And Sparrow,*" said the voice. "*Sparrow's here.*"

Archie stopped breathing.

In the space where his breath should have been there was a memory he hadn't known he still owned: a kitchen, summer, a screen door banging, a big man with silver hair crouched down in front of a very small boy with a silver dollar between two fingers. *Watch closely, Sparrow. Watch my hands.* The smell of pipe tobacco and Brylcreem. The dollar gone. The boy's whole body lighting up.

*Sparrow.* Nobody had called him that in thirty-seven years. He hadn't remembered it himself until this second.

He knew how she'd done it, in the same instant — some old newspaper, some interview with a grieving town in 1988; a researcher with a library card. He *knew.*

And for one second, sitting in the black dark with the green bands floating over him, he didn't care. For one second he would have given her the two million dollars and the house and the hearse and everything else he owned if she had just kept talking.

"*The trunk, Sparrow,*" said Gus Fairweather's voice. "*You're looking in the wrong place. The answer's in the — the answer's in—*"

The voice caught.

It thickened and slurred. "*The answer'sh — in—*" It coughed. It coughed again, wetly, and the green bands lurched in the air.

"He's fading," said Kev, in a whisper of pure joy. "He's going—"

The trumpet fell.

It hit the edge of the table with a hollow aluminum *bonk* and rolled, and Archie heard it drop off the edge and clatter across the stage boards, and in the same instant a chair scraped hard across the floor, and Lorelei Crane said, in her own voice — a high, frightened, ordinary voice that sounded nothing like a cello —

"My mouth. My — I can't feel my mouth—"

Archie let go of both hands.

"*Lights!*" He was on his feet. "Hollis! *Lights!* Lights, now!"

The house lights came up all at once, white and enormous, and everyone at the table flinched.

Lorelei Crane was slumped sideways in her chair at the head of the table. Her face had gone a terrible gray under the makeup, and it was shining with sweat, and her dark red lipstick was smeared at one corner. Her eyes were wide open. Ivy was holding her left hand. Shannon, white as paper, still had her right.

Archie went straight across the table. He didn't go around it. He put one knee on the oak and went over, scattering the slate and the tambourine, and got to her as she started to slide out of the chair, and caught her, and went down with her to the stage boards, her head in the crook of his arm.

"Lorelei. Lorelei, look at me."

She looked at him. Her pupils were huge. Her breath was coming short and fast, and he could feel, through the velvet, her heart going in his arm like a bird thrown against a window — too fast, then a stumble, then too fast again.

"Somebody call—" he started.

"*Ambulance is coming!*" Hollis's voice, from up in the booth, high and carrying. "*I've got them on the phone! Keep her still!*"

Chief Pinkham was running up the aisle, his radio already at his mouth. Vivienne was on her feet with both hands over her face. Kev was standing behind his chair saying *oh my God, oh my God* in a small voice, over and over. Warren hadn't moved.

And Ivy Crane, at the corner of the stage just past her mother's chair, bent down and picked up the trumpet off the floor.

Archie saw it from where he knelt — down low, at the level of the boards, with Lorelei's head in his arm. He saw Ivy turn the narrow end toward herself. He saw her pull the silk scarf off her throat in one quick motion and wipe the mouthpiece with it, twice, hard, twisting — the way you'd wipe a spoon — and then push the scarf deep into the pocket of the coat on the back of her chair and set the trumpet down on the table.

It took perhaps three seconds. Nobody else was looking at her. Everybody else was looking at her mother.

Lorelei's hand found Archie's sleeve and closed on it. Her rings were cold.

"Honey," she whispered. Her lips were barely moving. "Did you catch me?"

He looked down at her — at the smeared dark mouth and the huge eyes and the white streak in her hair spread out over his arm — and he knew exactly what she was asking, and he did the only thing he could think of.

He lied to her.

"No," Archie said. "You were perfect."

Her mouth moved. It might have been a smile. Her fingers tightened on his sleeve.

Down the stairs, the doors banged, and boots came pounding up, and voices, and the rattle of a stretcher. Lorelei Crane's eyes stayed on his the whole time, wide and dark and full of something he couldn't read — as if, even now, she was waiting to see how the trick came out.


---

# Chapter 9

## The Waiting Room

The emergency department waiting room at the hospital in Rockport had eleven blue plastic chairs bolted together in rows, a television showing a cooking competition with the sound off, a vending machine that sold coffee and hot chocolate and something it called tea, and a fish tank with one large, depressed-looking goldfish in it. At nine o'clock on a Saturday night it was empty except for a man with a bandaged hand asleep in the corner, and Ivy Crane, and Archie.

Ivy had come in the hearse.

He hadn't planned it. When the medics carried Lorelei out into the cold on Main Street, Ivy had followed the stretcher to the back of the ambulance and then simply stopped. The medics were already working — two of them crouched over her mother, one calling numbers — and there was no room, and somebody said *Follow us, ma'am, it's the hospital in Rockport*, and the doors shut, and Ivy stood there on the pavement in her gray sweater with her own black coat bundled over one arm, holding her mother's handbag, not moving.

Teddy Marchand had been on the town hall steps with a phone to each ear. Archie had put his buffalo-check coat around Ivy's shoulders, steered her across the street to the hearse, and done up her seat belt, as he would have for June. She hadn't said a word the whole twenty minutes down Route 1. She'd sat with the handbag on her knees and her hands folded on top of it, looking straight ahead at the ambulance's taillights.

She was sitting the same way now, in the blue plastic chair, his coat still around her shoulders and her own black one folded on the seat beside her.

Archie brought her a cup from the vending machine. It was tea in the sense that it was hot and brown.

"You don't have to drink it," he said. "I wouldn't. But it's something to hold."

She took it. She held it. She didn't drink it.

He sat down one chair away from her, because sitting right beside her felt like too much and sitting across the room felt like abandonment, and for a while neither of them said anything. On the television a man in a white jacket was setting fire to a dessert. The goldfish turned around in its tank and swam back the other way.

Ivy was rubbing the tips of her right fingers against her thumb — slowly, over and over, as if there were something on them she couldn't get off.

"Is somebody coming for you?" Archie asked. "Teddy? Anybody?"

"Teddy's calling the network." Her voice was perfectly flat. "Teddy's always calling the network."

"Family?"

"She's my family." She stopped. "Was. Is. She's my family."

He let that sit.

He sat and was quiet, and was kind, he hoped, on the outside. Inside, the séance was running back and forth like tape. The bell. The touches. The static. The trumpet floating up on the left. *Her* left. The side where Ivy sat.

And Ivy's hands, at the end — one holding her mother's, one wiping the narrow end of a tin horn with a silk scarf, twice, hard, while her mother was dying on the floor.

He was almost sure he knew why. He'd done something like it himself after a thousand shows: you reset. You never let anyone pick up a prop you haven't cleaned, because the prop is where the method is.

He'd told himself in the car he wasn't going to ask. He asked anyway, quietly.

"Ivy. In the dark. Was your mother holding your hand the whole time?"

Ivy turned and looked at him for the first time since the opera house. Her eyes were dry and pale and absolutely empty.

"She held my hand," she said.

"Ivy—"

"She held my hand." She turned back to the television. "You can stop now."

He did.

At nine-fifty, a pair of double doors at the end of the room opened, and a doctor came out. He was in his forties, slight, with dark circles under his eyes and a stethoscope around his neck, and he crossed the waiting room at a speed Archie recognized from every hospital he'd ever been in: not fast, not slow, chosen. His badge said DR. A. RAO.

Ivy stood up. The coat slid off her shoulders onto the chair.

"Ms. Crane?"

"Yes."

Dr. Rao told her. He did it well — plainly, without hurrying, looking at her face the whole time. Her mother's heart had gone into a dangerous rhythm on the way in. They had tried everything, many times, but the rhythm wouldn't come back. She had died at 9:40. He was very sorry. They didn't know yet what had caused it. There would have to be an examination, because of the circumstances, and somebody would explain all of that to her.

Ivy listened to all of it standing perfectly straight with her mother's handbag in both hands.

"Can I see her?" she said.

"In a little while. Someone will come and get you."

"Thank you," said Ivy, as though he had given her directions.

Dr. Rao glanced at Archie, recognized him, decided it didn't matter, and went back through the double doors.

Ivy sat down. She picked his coat up off the chair and put it around her shoulders again, carefully, and folded her hands on the handbag, and went back to looking at the television.

Archie sat beside her. He didn't say anything. There wasn't anything to say that wouldn't be for him instead of for her.

At five past ten the outside doors hissed open on a gust of cold air, and Tess Calloway came in.

She was in the charcoal coat over the navy fleece, with a badge on a lanyard around her neck. She took in the room — chairs, goldfish, the sleeping man, Ivy — and then Archie. Her eyebrows went up a quarter of an inch. She spoke quietly at the nurses' desk, went through the double doors, came back out ten minutes later, and crossed the room to Ivy and crouched down in front of her chair so their faces were level.

"Ms. Crane. I'm Detective Sergeant Calloway, from the State Police. I'm very sorry about your mother."

"Thank you."

"I'm going to need to ask you some questions, but not tonight. Is there someone I can call?"

"No."

"All right." Tess's voice was quieter than Archie had heard it, and much kinder. "I'll be right over there if you need anything."

She stood, and looked at Archie, and tilted her head toward the vending machine.

He went.

"Chief Pinkham called us," she said, low, with her back to the room. "He says you told him on the stairs it wasn't natural."

"It wasn't."

"She's a fifty-eight-year-old woman under a lot of stress who collapsed with a heart arrhythmia. The doctor says that happens."

"She said her mouth was numb." Archie kept his voice down. "Before she collapsed. Her mouth and her lips — she said she couldn't feel them. And her voice went thick, slurred, right before the trumpet fell. Sergeant, there's a poison that does that. Numbness in the mouth first, tingling, burning, then the heart goes wrong. It's in half the gardens in Maine. Monkshood. Wolfsbane. Aconite." He saw her face. "I did a whole Borgia routine in 2009 — poisoned goblets, the works. I read everything. The Victorians used to call it the queen of poisons. It doesn't show up unless you look for it."

Tess Calloway looked at him.

"Go on," she said.

He told her about the trumpet. About how the medium speaks through the small end in the dark — Gus's book, page one-twelve — and how, if anyone wanted to hurt her, that was the one thing in the room that would go to her mouth and nobody else's. And then he stopped, because the next thing was the thing he'd seen from the floor, and he turned his head and looked across the waiting room at Ivy Crane, sitting straight in his coat with her mother's handbag on her knees, watching a man on television set fire to a dessert.

It would cost her. He didn't know what it would cost yet, but he'd been reading people for a living since he was nineteen, and he knew the shape of a thing that was going to cost.

The other thing was a lie.

"When the lights came up," Archie said, "I was on the floor with her mother. I saw Ivy pick up the trumpet and wipe the small end. With her scarf. Twice. Then she put the scarf in her coat pocket."

Tess didn't write it down. She didn't need to; he could see her put it somewhere and close the drawer.

"Wipe it," she said.

"It's — there's a reason a performer might do that. A completely innocent reason. It's called a reset—"

"I didn't ask for a reason." She was already turning. She crossed the room and crouched down again in front of Ivy's chair, and Archie watched Ivy's face as Tess spoke to her quietly — watched it go from empty to careful.

"Ms. Crane. The scarf you were wearing tonight. I understand it's in that coat. Would you be willing to let me take it, just for now? I'll give you a receipt."

Ivy's eyes went past Tess, across the room, and found Archie by the vending machine. They stayed on him for a moment. He made himself not look away.

"No," Ivy said to Tess, very politely. "It was hers. It was my mother's. I'd like to keep it."

Tess looked at her for a second. Then she nodded, once. "All right," she said, and stood up. That was all. Archie understood: an unexplained death in a hospital wasn't a crime scene, and a grieving daughter's scarf wasn't evidence of anything. Yet.

Tess went to the nurses' desk again. He heard her ask for Dr. Rao, and heard her say, when he came out, *a specific toxicology screen — I'll have the medical examiner's office call you — aconitine, A-C-O-N-I* — and then she had her phone out and was talking to somebody about the opera house. *Has Dale still got the table roped off? Good. Nobody touches anything on that stage until the evidence tech gets there. Especially the trumpet. Yes, Dale, the trumpet.*

She hung up and came back to the vending machine. She bought herself a cup of coffee, which came out of the machine looking like something drained from a crankcase, and drank some of it without wincing, and looked at him over the rim.

"Mr. Fairweather," she said. "How many people in Merrow, do you think, would know the symptoms of aconite poisoning off the top of their heads?"

He opened his mouth.

He shut it again.

Somewhere behind the double doors, a woman who had toasted the vanishing man with Bev's chamomile that evening was lying under a sheet. Across the room, Ivy Crane sat in his coat with her mother's handbag in her lap, watching them both.


---

# Chapter 10

## Pancakes

The plan was pancakes shaped like playing cards.

Archie had worked it out at four in the morning, lying awake listening to the bell buoy out past Shag Ledge, which meant fog. A normal Sunday. A family at a kitchen table, being ordinary at one another. If breakfast happened to be a full deck with blueberry pips, nobody had ever said ordinary had to be boring.

By seven the fog was pressed white against the kitchen windows, and the results were on a platter in the middle of the farm table. They did not look like playing cards. Pancake batter, it turned out, had no respect for corners. Every rectangle he poured relaxed at once into something rounder and more opinionated. The three and four of clubs had fused into a single lumpy continent. The queen of hearts looked like Ohio.

Harold watched from his bucket in the sink. Somebody had given him fresh seawater before anyone was up — there were wet footprints on the porch steps — and June had denied it.

"What is that," said June, from the doorway.

She was in pajama pants and a gray sweatshirt that said PENOBSCOT REGIONAL DEBATE, with her hair down, which meant it was early and she hadn't decided yet how to feel about the day.

"The king of spades."

"It's a potato."

"It's a king of spades in a transitional phase. Sit. Eat. Normal people talk about the weather on Sundays."

June sat. Her father had not been to bed. He was still in the shirt he'd worn at the hospital, with a dishtowel over one shoulder.

"Okay," she said carefully. "What's the weather?"

"Fog, burning off by ten. Fifty-two." He'd looked it up at four-thirty, in case anybody asked. "Syrup?"

He heard his mother on the back stairs and was waiting at the bottom with a cup of coffee, two sugars and the good cream, when she came round the turn.

Vivienne was dressed. Not in a robe — in a dark green wool suit, a silk blouse, coral lipstick, and a small black hat with a feather in it, pinned at an angle that could have started a war. She took the coffee without breaking stride.

"You're dressed," said Archie.

"It's Sunday. I'm in the choir." She sat at the head of the table and inspected the platter. "What is the queen of hearts doing in Ohio?"

"You don't have to go. I'll call the minister. They can manage one Sunday without an alto."

"Darling, they can't manage one *measure* without an alto. You've heard the tenors." She took something that had been the seven of diamonds and cut it into small squares she didn't eat. "And you can stop looking at me like that. I'm not a soufflé."

He sat down across from her. He had rehearsed this too, at four in the morning: pancakes, weather, choir. Keep the night out of the kitchen.

"I was thinking," he began, "that today we could all just—"

"He called you Sparrow," said Vivienne.

She laid her fork down and lined it up with the edge of her plate.

"I'd forgotten it," she said. "I lay awake last night trying to remember the last time I heard anybody say it, and I couldn't. Some summer. Eighty-six, eighty-seven. You'd been at the cookie jar, and he said, *That sparrow's been at the jar again.* It was his. Nobody else called you that. Not me. Certainly not your father. Only Dad." She raised her eyes. "So how did that woman know?"

"Research." It came out too fast, and he slowed down. "Mother, it's always research. An old interview in the *Packet*. Some magazine in nineteen-seventy-something, a photograph of me on his knee, a caption. She had a researcher. Ivy does everything."

"Then show it to me."

"Show you—"

"The interview. The magazine. If it's written down somewhere, you'll find it. You find everything." She picked up her coffee. "Until then, I heard my father say your name in a dark room last night, and I'd thank you not to tell me I didn't."

The griddle ticked as it cooled. A half-dollar was walking across the backs of Archie's fingers, knuckle to knuckle, index to pinky and under and back.

"You're doing the coin," said June.

He closed his hand on it.

"Those are pancakes," said Lenny.

He was at the counter in his watch cap, rolling up the three and four of clubs. Nobody had heard the pantry door. He ate half of it standing. "Dale called the shop phone at six. Said you weren't answering. State Police took the table."

"They'll have taken the trumpet," said Archie. "Lenny, it was the trumpet. Two hands loose in that circle, and one of them was hers."

"Rod or a hand," said Lenny. "You showed the whole diner."

"A hand. And her mouth went numb. She *said* so. That's not a heart attack. That's something on the horn."

"Grandma said you gave her tea," said June.

Archie turned.

"I'm just saying Grandma said it. Last night, to Ozzie."

"Chamomile. Out of Bev's thermos. I drank the same tea, Junebug, out of the same thermos — the whole cup — and I'm sitting here making pancakes. It wasn't the tea."

June put down her fork and lined it up with the plate. Archie knew exactly where she'd learned that.

"Okay. Premise. Somebody put poison on the trumpet."

"Yes."

"Then they had to know two things. One, she was a fake. She'd have a hand loose and pick it up herself. Two, she'd put her mouth on the small end."

"Exactly, that's—"

"I'm not done." One finger went up, and he closed his mouth. He had watched her do this to a boy from Exeter in a regional final, and the boy had gone home and taken up the cello. "Who knows that? Everybody who's read Gus's book, which half the town has had out of the library. Everybody who's had Mrs. Tibbetts for senior English in the last twenty years. Mattie can do the whole chapter. In voices." Second finger. "Everybody in the Galley on Friday, because you stood up and taught them." Third finger. "And everybody on that stage last night who watched you pick it up, look in it, and put your mouth on it. On camera."

"It was a raspberry."

"And then you're the one who told the police what poison it was. Before anybody even knew it was poison." She picked her fork back up. "I'm just making the list. But if you're right, Dad, it's a really long list. And you're at the top of it."

Lenny took another pancake.

"That," said Archie, "was beautiful."

"It was a list." June pushed a blueberry around her plate. "So let the police do it. That's what they're for. You promised me a regular year, and regular people don't put themselves at the top of murder lists."

"Nobody said murder."

"You did. With your face. All night." She didn't look up. "I heard you come in at one. You went up to the turret. I heard the dials."

He hadn't known she'd heard.

The right thing was in plain sight. He could say *okay*, the way he had in the library on Friday, and let Tess Calloway, who seemed to him the most competent person he'd met in Maine, do her job. He could keep his whole family in this kitchen with the fog against the glass, where nothing could get at them.

He looked at his mother. She sat straight-backed in her war hat with the cup in both hands. She had waited in the wings for fifty years, and she was waiting now, for somebody to tell her how a dead woman knew her father's name for her son.

*Watch closely, Sparrow.* A screen door. A silver dollar. He'd been four.

"I'm going to find out," Archie said.

June shut her eyes.

"No cameras," he said quickly. "No show. No circus. Quietly. You won't even know I'm doing it."

"Dad. You can't do anything quietly. You drive a *hearse*."

"I inherited a hearse."

"You had it painted."

"I had it *restored*," said Lenny. "There's a difference." He rinsed his fingers at the sink, on the side away from Harold, and went out.

Vivienne set down her cup. She didn't thank him, and he didn't need her to. She reached across the corner of the table and straightened the dishtowel on his shoulder, as if he were about to go on, and stood up and pulled on her gloves.

"Ozzie's taking me to church," she said. "And Archibald, Bev's thermos is on the hall table. I put it in my bag last night while the rest of you were running about. Somebody had to. Take it back to the Galley today, before she sends somebody. She counts them."

"Today. I promise."

"You promise a great many things." She kissed June on the top of the head and, after a moment's thought, kissed Archie on his. "And wear a coat. That poor girl still has yours."

She went out in a breath of perfume and fog.

June was looking at him.

"What?"

"Nothing." She cut the king of spades in half. "Quietly."

The phone on the kitchen wall rang. It was Gus's yellow rotary, which rang loud enough to empty a school, and nobody called it except Hollis and the oil company.

Archie picked it up.

"Mr. Fairweather. Tess Calloway."

"Sergeant. Good morning. We're having pancakes."

There was a short silence on the line, in which a decision not to ask was clearly made.

"I'm at the opera house with Chief Pinkham and an evidence technician," she said. "Can you be here at ten."

"May I ask why?"

"You told me last night you know how these things are done. I'd like to know what I'm looking at before I bag it." A pause. "Ten o'clock. Don't touch anything when you get here."

She hung up. Archie stood holding the yellow receiver, grinning.

"Dad," said June. "Your face."

He hung up the phone and took the dishtowel off his shoulder.

"That," he said, "was the State Police. Asking for my help."

"That's not what that was," said June.


---

# Chapter 11

## Walkthrough

There was yellow tape across the doors at the top of the opera house stairs, and Chief Dale Pinkham lifted it for Archie with the solemn joy of a man opening a velvet rope.

"Sign in, please," he said, holding out a clipboard. "Name and time. Sergeant's rules."

*A. Fairweather, 9:58.* The top name on the sheet was *T. Calloway, 7:15*.

The fog had burned off, and the one bare window at the stage-left end let in hard white Sunday light that made the hall look shabby: black drapes sagging on their rods, cables taped across the floor. On the stage the round oak table stood with its seven chairs pushed back at seven angles. The candles, the glasses, the bell and the trumpet were gone; small yellow plastic tents with numbers on them sat in their places.

Tess Calloway was on the stage beside a man in blue nitrile gloves who was photographing the floor. She was in the navy fleece today, and she had slept about as much as he had. She looked at him, and then at his coat.

It was Gus's pea coat from the hall closet. It smelled of cedar, and the sleeves stopped two inches above his wrists.

"Where's your coat?"

"Ivy Crane has it."

She let that go. "This is Gagnon, from the crime lab. He bagged the table last night." The man in gloves lifted a hand without looking up. "Officer Bissonnette."

Kayla Bissonnette was twenty-four, compact and upright in a Merrow PD jacket, with her dark hair in a braid down her back that Archie would have bet hadn't been a braid in August.

"Tell me what a woman like Lorelei Crane brings into a room to do what she did," Tess said, "so I know what I'm looking for. From where I put you. Don't touch anything."

"A telescoping rod, black, like a car antenna, for the bell and the tambourine," said Archie. "A damp glove on the end of it, for the touches. And something that kills the picture on cue." He nodded at the two cameras, still on their tripods with evidence tags on their legs. "Every time she reached a good part, the feed went to snow. Kev Duchesne thinks that's the spirits. It's a button."

"Could be a button."

"It's a button."

"Could be. I don't have a button. I have a woman who died in a hospital and seven people who were in the dark." She turned. "Mrs. Tibbetts. The prop room, please."

Hollis was at the top of the stage-left steps, going through her ring of keys.

"It's a short brass one," she said. "It's always on here."

Archie went down into the stage-left wing to the plain wooden door with its hundred-year-old warded lock.

"Officer Bissonnette. May I borrow a bobby pin?"

Kayla's hand went to her braid. She looked at Tess. Tess looked at the ceiling. Kayla handed one over, and Archie bent it straight, put a crook in the end with his thumbnail, slid it into the keyhole, found the ward, lifted and turned.

The bolt went back. Four seconds.

Behind him, Hollis held up a short brass key. "Here it is."

"Officer Bissonnette," said Tess, "write down that Mr. Fairweather opened the prop-room door at six minutes past ten, with your hairpin, after I asked him not to touch anything."

Kayla wrote it down.

"I didn't touch anything," said Archie. "I touched a lock."

Tess said nothing. He returned the bobby pin, which was no longer any shape a bobby pin could use. Kayla put it in her pocket with care.

The prop room was narrow and cold and smelled of old paint and mice, with a headless dress-form wearing half a pirate and shelves to the ceiling crammed with the Merrow Players' history: a papier-mâché moose head, tin swords, a pyramid of hats. On a middle shelf was an empty space about three feet long with a clean rectangle in the dust.

"That's where their things were," said Hollis. "Thursday night till yesterday afternoon. Their crew brought it all up for the show."

Archie was looking at the shelves instead. They were built of old crate boards, gray and splintery, and on the end of one, half under a hat, was a word stenciled in faded black: ST. PIERRE.

"Sergeant," he said. "Hear me out."

"No."

"Saint-Pierre. French island off Newfoundland. In Prohibition every rum-runner between here and Boston loaded there. These are Saint-Pierre crates, in a town hall on a harbor, with a trapdoor in the stage and a hatch to the alley. This building was a drop, Sergeant. And somebody unlocked this door on Friday night looking for something that's been hidden in here for a hundred years, and Lorelei—"

"Mrs. Tibbetts," said Tess, without turning her head, "is anything missing from this room?"

"Not that I can see."

"Gagnon, the shelf, please." She stepped back into the wing. "Mr. Fairweather, stand in the light where I can see you."

He went and stood in the light.

---

"Show me," said Tess.

They were back at the table. She'd listened with her arms folded while he explained the hand release, then said those two words, and Archie felt it go through him: an audience.

"Two volunteers. Chief, you're Ivy."

Dale Pinkham looked at the chair at the medium's left, and then at Tess, with naked longing.

"Sit," said Tess.

He sat.

"Officer Bissonnette, you're Lorelei. I'll be Shannon." Archie took the chair at the medium's right. "Shannon held this hand from start to finish. I believe her. So whatever came loose came loose on the other side." He put the chief's right hand into Kayla's left. "Chief, you're the plant. You're in on it. Close your eyes. Now I need a trumpet."

"The trumpet's in a bag in Augusta," said Tess.

"Something like it. Conical. About two feet."

Kayla was already up. They heard her boots on the stairs, the street door, a car trunk, and then she came back up two at a time with an orange traffic cone under one arm and set it on the table at her place.

"Officer," said Archie, "you're going to go far."

She went pink and sat. Archie took a teaspoon off the tray of Hollis's tea things in the wing, cold as the room, and pressed it under the table into Kayla's free hand.

"It's very dark," he said. "Everybody in this circle could swear on a Bible to the two hands they've got. The medium says the spirits are restless. The plant squeezes once—"

The chief squeezed. His knuckles went white.

"—and lets go."

Dale Pinkham, eyes screwed shut, let go and put his hand flat on the table.

"Now the medium's got a hand free, and so has the plant, and nobody else at the table knows. Officer, pick it up and say something."

Kayla raised the traffic cone, put the small end to her lips and said into it, in a voice as flat as a parking ticket, "*Shan. It's me.*"

It came out of the wide end enormous and rang off the pressed-tin ceiling.

"And while everybody's looking up at that," said Archie softly, "the medium does this."

Kayla had been told nothing about the spoon. She worked it out anyway. She reached across with her free right hand and laid the cold steel bowl flat against the back of the chief's neck.

Dale Pinkham went straight up off his chair, the same four inches he'd gone off the stool at the Galley on Friday. His eyes flew open. He looked at Archie, wounded to the core.

"I was *in* on it," he said. "I was the *accomplice*."

Somebody laughed.

It was short and startled and got out before anyone could stop it. Archie turned his head. Tess Calloway stood at the edge of the stage with one hand pressed over her mouth and her shoulders moving, once, and her eyes on the floor.

She took her hand away. Her face was perfectly composed.

"Go on," she said.

He didn't say a word about it. He wanted very badly to make her do it again.

"That's the trick," he said instead. "And whatever was on the small end of that trumpet went into the one mouth at the table that was meant to touch it."

"Or it was in something else," said Tess. "Could." She came up the steps. "Mrs. Tibbetts, a couple of things for the record. Who's had the key to that prop room this week?"

Hollis came out of the wing with the ring clanking at her hip. "Me. It lives on my ring. And Warren Gilley. I lent it to him Friday afternoon for his inventory, the posters and fixtures for the closing. He gave it back at the Galley that evening. Saturday morning the door was unlocked. He says he locked it."

"And the props on stage last night. Who handled them?"

"Everybody. Mr. Fairweather inspected the lot on camera and put his mouth on one. I lit the candles and set the water. Mr. Marchand had me slide the horn over, it was fighting the candle for his camera. Ivy came and went. That young man in the sweatshirt photographed everything until they made him stop." She shrugged. "It was a stage, Theresa. People touch things on a stage."

Tess wrote. Archie had stopped listening.

*Warren.* Warren, who'd gone white as milk when Lorelei leaned over and murmured in his ear. Warren, who'd left the stage at a quarter to eight holding his stomach. Warren, with a key to the room where the trumpet had spent two nights.

"Warren Gilley," he said.

"No," said Tess, still writing.

"I didn't say anything."

"You said Warren Gilley in a voice."

Hollis looked at Archie over her glasses as if he'd read the first chapter and announced how the book ended, and went back into the wing.

Archie walked to the lip of the stage and stood with his back to them, winding Gus's old steel watch in small turns between finger and thumb, and ran the end of the séance again.

Lights up. Ivy wiping the horn, pushing the scarf into a pocket. The medics, the stretcher, Ivy following her mother down the stairs with her coat bundled over one arm and the handbag in her hands. And somewhere on her, all through the dark, a thing with a button.

You didn't walk out past a police chief with the method in your pocket. You reset. You put it down somewhere ordinary on the way out, where nobody would look.

He went up the aisle, under the tape ("*Sign out*," said the chief, faintly), and down to the landing where the stairs turned beneath the poster of THE GREAT FAIRWEATHER DEFIES THE ASYLUM. In the corner stood a cast-iron umbrella stand holding four dead umbrellas. It had been there so long nobody saw it.

He stood over it. If he called up the stairs, Tess would come down with Gagnon, and whatever was in there would go into a bag and up to Augusta, and he would never learn whose it was. He reached down among the umbrellas anyway and closed his fingers on something small and hard.

It was a black plastic remote the size of a matchbox, with a single gray rubber button and a stub of antenna. He held it up into the light from the stairwell window.

"Mr. Fairweather."

Tess was on the stairs above him. Gagnon was behind her, pulling an evidence bag out of his jacket.

"Put it on the step," Tess said. "Now. Take your hand away."

He put it on the step.

"You've just put your fingerprints on that." She didn't raise her voice. "On top of whoever else's are on it. If it's anything at all, you've made it a third less useful to me and a great deal more interesting to my lieutenant."

"It was in the umbrella stand."

"I can see where it was. I'd have liked Gagnon to see where it was."

She watched the tech bag and seal it. Then she turned her back on Archie and made a call.

"Calloway. Is the Crane autopsy still this afternoon? — Good. Before anybody cleans her up, I want her lips swabbed. The lipstick, the skin, the inside of the mouth. Separate swabs. — Because I'm asking. Thank you."

She put the phone away and looked down at him on the landing.

"Autopsy's at four, in Augusta. I'm going up for it. I've asked them to look for your wolfsbane."

"Aconite," said Archie. "Wolfsbane's the garden name."

She came down two steps, so that they were nearly level.

"If they find it," she said quietly, "you understand where that leaves you."

He looked at the little black box in Gagnon's bag with his own prints on it now. What he mostly felt, God help him, was an itch to know whose were underneath.

"Yes, Sergeant," he said.


---

# Chapter 12

## The Captain's Suite

Bev took the thermos back without a word.

Archie set it on the Galley counter a little after noon — dented steel, masking tape, BEV — RETURN IT — and she picked it up by the handle, looked at it, and put it in a gray bus tub under the counter with three others just like it. Then she looked at him.

"Chowder," he said. "A quart. For somebody who hasn't eaten since yesterday."

"Clam or fish?"

"Which is better?"

"Clam's Sunday." She ladled it into a white paper tub, pressed the lid down with the heel of her hand, and added two packets of oyster crackers without being asked. "Seven-fifty."

He gave her a twenty. She gave him twelve-fifty back, exactly, and went to pour coffee for the liars' table, who did not look at him. Sixty-one.

The Spindrift Inn was three streets away, and Pru Haskell met him in the front hall with her hands knotted in a dish towel and told him that Ivy Crane was upstairs with the State Police and wasn't to be disturbed.

"Which room?"

"The Captain's Suite. Top of the house. Mr. Fairweather, I really don't think—"

He was already on the stairs. They went up three flights, narrowing as they went, to a single door under the eaves.

Tess Calloway opened it about eight inches and looked at him through the gap.

"No," she said.

He held up the chowder. "She hasn't eaten."

"You don't know that."

"She hadn't eaten at nine last night, and she hadn't eaten at eleven when I dropped her here, and I've known a lot of people who couldn't eat after a show. Clam. It's Sunday." He kept his voice low. "I'll put it down and go."

Tess looked at him for a moment. Then she looked back over her shoulder into the room. He couldn't see what she saw. Whatever it was, it made up her mind.

She opened the door.

The Captain's Suite took up the whole third floor under the roof: a sitting room with a sloped ceiling and dormer windows over the harbor, a bedroom through an open door, and in one corner a steep ladder-stair up to a hatch, which would be the widow's walk. It was warm, and it smelled of woodsmoke and menthol and Lorelei's perfume. Her long velvet coat hung on the back of the bedroom door. On the windowsill a paperback mystery lay face down, open to the page where she'd stopped.

"Sit there," Tess said, and pointed at a straight-backed chair against the wall by the closet, well out of the way. "Don't talk."

Ivy Crane was in an armchair by the dormer window, in the same gray sweater, with her knees together and her hands in her lap. Archie's buffalo-check coat was folded over the arm of her chair. She looked at the chowder, and at him, and at nothing.

He put the tub on the low table beside her with a plastic spoon and both packets of crackers, and went and sat in the straight chair against the wall, and folded his hands, and was quiet.

Tess sat down again across from Ivy. There was a small digital recorder on the table between them, its red light on, and a notebook open on Tess's knee.

"For the recording," Tess said, "Mr. Fairweather has come in. He isn't part of this interview. Ms. Crane, you're still here voluntarily. You can stop whenever you like."

"I know."

"You were telling me about the work you did for your mother."

"I research the sitters," Ivy said. "Before every show. Everybody does it. Obituaries, local papers, social media, church bulletins, whatever there is. Names, dates, how they died, what they called each other. It's in the binder."

"May I have the binder?"

Ivy got up and went to the closet. It was a deep old closet with a shelf above the rail, and she reached up and took down a fat black three-ring binder from the shelf, next to a round green hatbox, and handed it to Tess without a word. Then she sat back down.

Tess opened it on her knee. From the wall, Archie could see colored tabs. MAYO. GILLEY. DUCHESNE. FAIRWEATHER, V. FAIRWEATHER, A.

There was his name, on a pink tab. Tess turned to it.

The first page behind it was a photocopy of an old newspaper column, gone gray in the copying, with the *Merrow Packet* masthead across the top and a date in November 1988. One line had been run through with yellow highlighter. Archie had been reading upside down across card tables for thirty years, and he read it from the wall.

*Gus Fairweather is survived by his daughter, Vivienne, and his grandson, Archie, whom he called his "little sparrow."*

Some reporter had written down what an old man said about a boy. Thirty-seven years later somebody had gone looking for it, and found it, and drawn a yellow pen through it.

He had told his mother at breakfast that it would be research. It was research.

Tess turned the page, and then closed the binder and set it on the floor beside her chair, on the side away from Archie, and put her boot next to it.

"Saturday night," she said. "In the dark. Your mother was seated at the head of the table. You were on her left. Were you holding her hand?"

"Yes."

"The whole time? From when the lights went out until they came back on?"

"She held my hand," said Ivy.

Tess wrote it down. Archie watched Ivy's fingers in her lap. They weren't moving now. She'd stopped doing that sometime in the night.

He had told himself on the stairs that he would sit in the chair and be quiet. He had meant it. But he knew what it was like, the first morning after, when you've got to hold the whole thing up yourself and nobody in the world knows how heavy it is. He leaned forward with his elbows on his knees.

"Ivy," he said gently. "I saw you wipe the trumpet. When the lights came up. I told the sergeant." He let that sit for a second, so she'd know he wasn't hiding it. "I understand why you did it. You reset. Anybody raised in the business would have. It doesn't mean anything except that you were raised in the business."

Ivy turned her head and looked at him. Her eyes were pale and steady.

Then she turned back to Tess.

"I held my mother's hand," she said, clearly, to the notebook. "The whole time. Write that down."

Tess wrote it down.

Archie sat back. He'd thrown her a rope, and she had stepped around it.

He tried once more anyway, because it was true, and because she should have it.

"She was going to stop, you know," he said. "Your mother. She told me on Friday night, at the fire downstairs. This was her last season. She was going to buy a house somewhere warm and read mysteries. She said she hadn't told anyone."

For a moment nothing happened.

Then Ivy Crane's face went white and tight, every muscle in it pulled at once. It didn't crumple.

"She told *you*," she said.

"Ivy—"

"I asked her for a year." Her voice was flat and low and shaking very slightly at the bottom, as it had in the parking lot on Friday. "I asked her every month for a year. *When are we done. When can I stop.* And she said *when I say*. And she told *you*. At the fire. With a drink." She looked down at her hands. "She never told me."

Archie had a whole career of things to say into a silence, and not one of them was any use. Tess didn't write anything. She rested the pen on the page and let it be quiet.

The door banged open without a knock.

"Ivy. Sweetheart. I'm so sorry, I know, I just need two minutes." Teddy Marchand came in fast, a phone in each hand and mango vapor trailing off his fleece vest. He saw Tess, and Archie, and kept going anyway. "Sergeant, hi. Ivy. The network wants to air it. The full special, everything up to the — everything. In memoriam. Tasteful. A card at the end with her dates." He crouched beside her chair. "She'd want the last word, honey. You know she would. I just need you to sign off as next of kin."

"No," said Ivy.

"Ivy, it's what she—"

"You don't get to tell me what she wanted." Ivy stood up, so suddenly that Teddy rocked back on his heels. "Did she tell *you*, Teddy? That she was quitting?" Her voice went up, for the first time. "Get out."

"Okay. Okay, I hear you, I hear that you're upset—"

"*Get out.*"

Archie crossed the room in three steps. He put a friendly arm around Teddy's shoulders and lifted him gently off his heels and turned him toward the door, all in one movement, talking the whole time in the warm, easy voice he used for volunteers who'd stayed up too long.

"Teddy, let me walk you out. You've had a long night. We've all had a long night. Come on, there's coffee downstairs, Pru's got a—"

"Don't."

He stopped, his arm still around Teddy's shoulders. Ivy was standing in the middle of the room with her hands in fists at her sides.

"Don't help me," she said.

Archie took his arm away. Teddy looked from one of them to the other, put both phones in his vest pockets, and went out on his own, saying something about the network to nobody in particular. They heard his white sneakers squeak all the way down the stairs.

Ivy picked up the buffalo-check coat from the arm of her chair. She held it out to Archie across the room, at arm's length, folded.

"Take your coat," she said.

He took it. She sat back down in the armchair by the window and looked out at the harbor.

Tess clicked off the recorder. "Thank you, Ms. Crane. I'll give you a receipt for the binder." She wrote it, and laid it on the table beside the chowder, which nobody had touched. Then she looked at Archie, and at the door.

---

The third-floor hall was narrow and low, with a runner of faded red carpet and a window at the end over the parking lot. Tess walked to the window before she stopped, and Archie followed, with his own coat over his arm, and they stood there with the binder between them.

"Well," said Archie.

"She said it twice," said Tess. "*She held my hand.* Both times before I'd finished the question." She looked out at the lot. "People who are telling the truth answer the question you asked. She answered the one she'd been waiting for."

"That's a cop's read."

"That's my read." She shifted the binder under her arm. "And now it's on tape, in her words, voluntary, twice. If it isn't true, it's going to matter."

"I got something you couldn't," Archie said.

"I know you did." She didn't say it kindly or unkindly. "She didn't know her mother was quitting. Her mother told a stranger with a drink and not her own daughter, and you're the one who handed her that." Tess looked at him. "Was that a kindness?"

He opened his mouth. He didn't know.

"I let you in," Tess said, "because I wanted to see her face when she looked at the man who'd told me about the scarf. I saw it. That's what I came for." She turned toward the stairs. "Don't come to my interviews, Mr. Fairweather."

"You opened the door."

"Next time I'll keep it shut."

She went down. Archie put on his coat, which was still warm from the room and smelled of menthol, and went down after her as far as the street.


---

# Chapter 13

## Rosie and Liam

Shannon Mayo lived in a small gray-shingled house on the lane behind the lobster co-op, close enough to the water that the dooryard smelled of bait. There were traps stacked four high along the fence, green wire, with a buoy color Archie had seen on the stern of a boat called *Rosie & Liam* — yellow over blue. Nobody had sold them. On the front step sat a pumpkin carved by someone about seven years old, with a great deal of enthusiasm and no fear of triangles.

It was four o'clock, and the sun was already low over the harbor, laying long yellow light across the street. Archie stood on the step in his own coat with his hands in the pockets. He had thought about bringing something — flowers, a pie — and decided you didn't bring a present to say you were sorry. Now he was here with nothing in his hands, and his hands didn't like it.

He knocked.

Shannon opened the door. She was in sweatpants and a man's navy hooded sweatshirt much too big for her, with the sleeves pushed up, and her red-gold hair was tied back any old way. Her eyes were red. When she saw who it was, her face closed like a hatch.

"No," she said.

"Shannon, I only want two minutes. I came to say—"

"I don't want two minutes." She started to shut the door.

A small boy ducked under her arm.

He was seven or so, in socks and a Red Sox shirt, with a gap where his front teeth should have been, and his mouth fell open.

"Mom," he said. "*Mom.* It's the guy. It's the riverboat guy."

"Liam, go inside."

"You made a *whole boat* disappear." Liam pulled the door wide with the absolute strength of seven. "Mom, he made a *boat* disappear. On the *Mississippi.* Can you come in? Can you do one? Mom, can he come in?"

Behind him, in the hall, a little girl of about four appeared, wearing a red ladybug costume with black spots and two wobbly antennae on a headband, and holding a plastic pumpkin bucket that was empty. She stared at Archie in silence.

"That's Rosie," Liam said. "She's been a ladybug since Thursday. She won't take it off."

"It's a very good ladybug," said Archie.

Shannon looked at her son's hand on the door, and at her daughter in the hall, and at Archie. He watched her do the arithmetic of a mother who could not have a fight on her own doorstep in front of her children, and lose.

"Kitchen," she said. "Not past the kitchen."

The kitchen was small and warm and clean, with a table under the window and a refrigerator covered in drawings held up by magnets from the marine supply in Rockland. Most of the drawings were of boats. One was of a man in yellow oilskins, very tall, with a smile that went off the edge of his face. A pair of rubber boots stood by the back door, men's, size twelve or so, with the tops folded down. Nobody had moved them either.

"Do one," said Liam. "Please. Do one, do one."

Archie looked at Shannon. She'd folded her arms and leaned against the counter, and she said nothing, which he took as permission and knew wasn't.

"Name a card," he said to Liam.

"Um. The king of — the seven of — the ace of spades!"

Archie showed him both hands, empty, front and back, and pushed his coat sleeves up past the wrist. Then he reached into the air a foot in front of Liam's nose and closed his fingers, and when he opened them there was a card standing between the first two, and it was the ace of spades.

Liam screamed. Rosie, who had been hiding behind her mother's leg, came out.

"Me," she said. It was the first thing she'd said.

"What card would you like, ladybug?"

Rosie thought about it hard. "The princess one."

He reached up next to one of her wobbling antennae, slowly, so she could watch, and brought down the queen of hearts, and gave it to her. She held it in both hands and looked at it, and then up at him, and then she laughed — a small, astonished, hiccuping laugh — and ran to show her brother, and the two of them collided in the doorway and fell over together in a heap of ladybug and Red Sox, shrieking.

Liam sat up first, with the ace held against his chest. He looked at the card, and at Archie, and at his mother, and the shrieking went out of him.

"Is my dad really talking to Mom?" he said. "On the phone? Mom says he says stuff."

The kitchen went quiet. Rosie, on the floor, looked from one of them to the other. Shannon didn't move off the counter, and she didn't help him.

Archie had a dozen answers ready. There was one that would make the boy smile, and he knew exactly how it went.

He crouched down so his face was level with Liam's.

"I don't think so, Liam," he said. "I think the lady on the phone was guessing, and she was very good at guessing. That was her job." He waited until Liam looked at him. "But your mom wasn't guessing. She listened because she misses your dad. That part's true."

Liam thought about it. He looked at his mother.

"Okay," he said, and looked down at his card.

"Liam," said Shannon, without taking her eyes off Archie. "Take your sister and go put those somewhere safe. In your room. Go on."

They went, thundering. A door banged upstairs.

"That's what you do," Shannon said. Her voice was low and even. "Isn't it. You come in and you make them look at the pretty thing over here, and they laugh. That's the same thing. That's the exact same thing she did."

"Shannon, I came to say I'm sorry."

"For what?"

"I promised I wouldn't humiliate you. And last night, in the dark, in front of all those cameras, with Tommy's—" He stopped. "With that voice. It was worse than anything I promised you about. I should never have let you sit there. I picked you. I'm sorry."

"You think I don't know how it's done?" She pushed herself off the counter. "You think I'm stupid? I went to Penobscot Regional. I had Mrs. Tibbetts for senior English. I did the séance unit. I wrote the five-page paper — *How the Grieving Are Fooled*, typed, double-spaced. I got an A-minus." Her voice cracked. "And Hollis already sat at this table this morning, at seven o'clock, and gave me the whole thing again. The research. The Facebook. The obituary in the *Packet*. Page one-twelve. She was kind about it. She was so kind I wanted to throw the coffeepot at her."

"Then you know—"

"I know. I've always known. That's not the point. I don't need you to explain it, and I don't need her to explain it, and I don't need every person in the Galley tomorrow looking at me like I'm the poor dumb widow who cried at a tin horn." She was breathing hard. "He talked to me. Since the summer, he talked to me. I could stand in the kitchen at night, and he — and now she's dead, and everybody's going to say he was never there, and you'll be on television explaining how. That's what you did. You and your bet. You took him away again."

Archie stood in her kitchen with his empty hands at his sides.

"I'm sorry," he said. It was all there was.

The front door opened without a knock.

"It's me," called Hollis Tibbetts from the hall. "I've brought American chop suey, and I don't want to hear a word about it, because you haven't eaten and neither have those children." She came into the kitchen in her men's cardigan and a wool hat, with a covered Pyrex dish held in two oven mitts, and stopped when she saw Archie.

"Aunt Hollis!" Rosie came down the stairs much faster than a ladybug should, and hit Hollis around the knees.

"There's my girl. Careful — hot." Hollis set the dish on the stove, pulled off one mitt and put her hand on Rosie's head. Then she looked at Shannon's face, and at Archie's, and took the measure of the room in about the time it took to put down a casserole.

"Archie," she said. "Go home."

"I was just—"

"I know what you were just. Go home." She said it warmly and without any give in it. "Shannon, sit down before you fall down. Rosie, show me what you've got there. Is that the queen of hearts? Well. Aren't you grand."

Archie went. He went back down the little hall past the stairs, where Liam was sitting halfway up with the ace of spades held against his chest, watching him with enormous eyes. Archie lifted a hand. Liam lifted the card.

Shannon caught him at the front door.

She'd followed him down the hall, fast, and she held the door so he couldn't open it the rest of the way, and spoke low so the kids wouldn't hear.

"You didn't lie to him," she said. "Everybody lies to him. I lie to him." She held up her right hand. Across the backs of two fingers there was a line of small dark bruises, in a row, where stones had pressed. "So here. For your investigation. She never let go of my hand. Not once, start to finish. She held on so hard her rings cut into me. Four readings. The first one was free. Three at three-fifty. That's ten-fifty, borrowed against the boat. The last one was Friday night — after you, after the wharf. She called me at nine o'clock to get me ready. She said Saturday was going to be something special. Just for me." Her mouth twisted. "And it was."

She let go of the door. Archie went out onto the step, and she shut it behind him, not hard. He heard the lock turn.

He stood beside the pumpkin with its fearless triangles. The sun was nearly down.

After a minute the door opened again, and Hollis came out onto the step, pulling it to behind her. She had her car keys in her hand, and an empty oven mitt.

"Rolls," she said. "In the car. I forgot the rolls."

"Hollis, I didn't mean—"

"I know you didn't." She looked up at him over her glasses. "She'll forgive you, you know. She was always a good girl." She considered it. "Not this week, though. Possibly not this month. Go home, Archie, and eat something."

She went down the path to her car. Upstairs, behind a lit window, the children were laughing about something. Archie listened until they stopped, and then he went home.


---

# Chapter 14

## Five-Card Draw

Sunday-night poker at Fairweather House had been Vivienne's idea, in September, and like most of Vivienne's ideas it had become a law of nature within a week.

They played in the dining room at Gus's long mahogany table, with a fire in the grate and a green felt cloth over the wood. The chips were Gus's own — heavy old clay ones in a walnut case, which clicked in a way that made Archie happy every single time. Nickel, dime, quarter. Bev had announced the stakes the first night, and nobody had questioned them since.

By eight o'clock Archie was down four dollars and seventy cents.

"I'll take three," he said.

Ozzie Lund dealt him three, one at a time. Ozzie was seventy, square and weathered, with a white fringe of beard and enormous wrists, and he dealt cards with the deliberation of a man setting a mooring. Beside him Vivienne, in a plum kaftan, was eating cashews out of a silver dish and holding her cards very close to her pearls.

Archie picked up his three cards. A queen. A nine. Another queen.

Queens over nines. A full house.

He arranged his face into nothing, carefully. He had stood in front of thirty thousand people without letting them see a thing he didn't want seen; he could manage a dining room in Maine. He laid both hands flat on the felt and waited.

"Fold," said Bev, without even looking at her own cards.

"Fold," said Dale Pinkham.

"Fold," said Lenny, who had been folding every hand for an hour on the grounds that it was the only way to be sure.

Vivienne laid her cards face down on the felt and patted them, as if they were a dog that had disappointed her. "Darling, you're humming."

"I'm not humming."

"You're humming 'Hello, Dolly.'"

Ozzie folded too. Archie raked in a pot of eighty-five cents, all of it his own ante money, and looked around the table in genuine grievance.

"How," he said. "*How*. I didn't move. I didn't move a muscle."

"You stopped breathing," said Bev. "Then you started humming. Then you looked at the ceiling like the Lord had come down to see you personally." She shuffled, fast and without love. "You do it every time."

"You could learn to cheat," Dale Pinkham offered. He said it shyly. "I bet you could. I bet you could deal yourself anything you wanted. I've seen the specials."

"Dale. A magician who cheats at cards is just a crook with nice hands. It's against the code."

"What code?"

"*The* code."

"There's no code," said Lenny.

"There is a code, Lenny, and you've seen me walk out of a game in Reno over it." Archie anted a nickel. "I would rather lose to Bev every Sunday for the rest of my life than win once dishonestly."

"Good," said Bev, and dealt.

"You still want to go hauling?" Dale asked him, sorting his cards. "Me and Mattie are going out in the morning, before the weather turns. Five-thirty at the float. Bring that lobster of yours."

Archie had been angling for an invitation onto a lobster boat since August. He put down his cards. "Dale. *Yes.*"

"Don't be late. Mattie leaves people."

He lost that hand to Bev too, bluffing on a pair of fours that she called without blinking. It took him down to six dollars and five cents. She stacked his chips in front of her in a neat red column, and while she stacked she said, to the table generally:

"Pru Haskell says the State Police booked two rooms at the Spindrift from tomorrow. For a week. A lieutenant, from Augusta."

There was a little pause around the table. The fire popped.

Dale Pinkham looked very hard at his cards. "Brannock," he said, at last. "Neil Brannock. Major Crimes. He's—" He searched for the word. "Thorough."

"Is that good?" said Vivienne.

"It's thorough," said Dale, and discarded two.

Archie didn't take a card. He sat back in Gus's chair at the end of the table with his hands flat on the felt, and the idea arrived the way they always did, all at once and fully dressed, with a hat on.

"Hear me out," he said.

"No," said Lenny.

"Teddy Marchand." Archie leaned forward. "Teddy's show is about to end — she told me herself, she was quitting. Teddy also has an insurance policy on her through the end of the season; I heard him say so through a door. Now. What's the biggest television event in history? A medium who dies on camera, in the dark, talking to the dead, in the most haunted theater in New England. That's not a special. That's a *franchise*. And what does Lorelei want? A house somewhere warm, with no phone, and a stack of mysteries." He spread his hands. "Everybody wins."

"She did die," said Bev.

"*Did* she?"

"Archie," said Vivienne.

"A hospital where nobody'd ever seen her except on a bus. A doctor I'd never met. And then she's gone, into a room I couldn't go into." He was going faster now; he could feel it carrying him. "I've made a woman go gray onstage, Mother. Greasepaint and glycerin, twice a night in Vegas. Right now Lorelei Crane is on a beach in Baja with a paperback, and Teddy Marchand is on the phone to the network with tears in his voice, and the ratings—"

"There'd have to be two ambulances," said Dale Pinkham, with dawning wonder. "One for the — and then a second one, see, at the hospital, round the back—"

"*Exactly*, Chief—"

"She's dead," said Lenny.

"Lenny—"

"Last night you said poison. You told the police poison."

"Last night I was tired."

June's voice came from the hall, where she was sitting halfway up the front stairs with a library book and a blanket, in a position from which she could see the whole table and pretend she couldn't. "Dad."

"I'm just *saying*—"

"Dad. You were holding her."

The fire popped again. Ozzie dealt him two cards, slowly, and Archie looked at them without seeing them.

Bev cleared her throat. She did not look at him. She picked up her coffee cup, found it empty, set it down.

"You brought my thermos back, anyhow," she said. "Took you long enough. I washed it. Cups and all." She took a card. "I wash everything that comes back. You'd be amazed what comes back."

Archie looked up.

The thermos. The two cups that screwed onto the top. The cup he'd poured for Lorelei, and handed to her across the table, on camera, and which she'd drunk half of. The one thing in the world that might have proved the tea was only tea.

"She'd wash the Pope," said Dale comfortably, "if he sat still long enough."

"She washed my hat," said Ozzie.

"It needed it," said Bev.

Vivienne laughed. Lenny didn't. He was watching Archie across the table. So was his mother.

He didn't say anything funny. He couldn't find anything.

"Archie?" said Dale.

"Dad," said June from the stairs, in a different voice. "There's somebody in the driveway."

Headlights had swung across the dining-room windows and stopped. A car door shut. Boots on the porch. Lenny, who was nearest, got up and opened the front door before anyone knocked, and there was Tess Calloway on the porch in her charcoal coat with the cold coming in around her.

She looked past Lenny at the table — the fire, the felt, the chief of the Merrow Police Department holding a pair of jacks — and her eyebrows went up a sixteenth of an inch.

"Evening, Dale," she said.

"Sergeant." Dale put his cards face down, fast.

"I've just come from Augusta." She didn't come in. She spoke to Archie, across the room, past Lenny's shoulder. "The autopsy's done. The toxicology's gone to the lab. I'll have it when I have it."

"Archie thinks she's in Baja," said Dale helpfully.

"I was in the room this afternoon, Dale." Tess said it quietly. "She's not in Baja."

Nobody at the table said anything. Archie looked down at the felt.

"You were at the Spindrift with Ivy Crane at one o'clock," Tess went on. "That I know about, because I was there. At four you were at Shannon Mayo's house."

"How do you—"

"It's Merrow." She took off one glove. "Stop. That's what I drove down here to say. Stop visiting my witnesses. Stop finding things in umbrella stands. Stop."

"Sergeant," said Vivienne. She had risen from her chair in her plum kaftan, and she was holding out her hand. "You look absolutely frozen, and you've driven an hour and a half to say one word. Come in and sit down. Ozzie, pull out a chair. One hand."

"I'm on duty, Mrs. Fairweather."

"Then you'll play carefully." Vivienne kept her hand out. "One hand. It would be rude to go straight back out into that."

Tess looked at her. Then she stepped in, and somehow she was sitting at the end of the table opposite Archie, with her coat over the back of the chair and a stack of Vivienne's chips in front of her.

Ozzie dealt. Five each.

Archie picked up his cards. Nothing. Ten high, two hearts, two clubs, a spade. He took four, slowly, watching her across the table, and got nothing back. Ten high still.

Bev could read his face; fine. Tonight he made it a wall. When the bet came round he pushed in his whole stack, six dollars and five cents in red and white clay, and sat back and looked at Tess with no expression, while his fingers riffled three chips over and under, over and under.

Bev folded. Dale folded. Vivienne folded with a sigh. Lenny had never been in.

Tess looked at her cards. She looked at him.

"Call," she said, and pushed her chips in, and turned over a pair of sixes.

He turned over ten high.

"How," he said. It wasn't a performance this time. "My face didn't move. I *know* my face didn't move."

"It didn't." Tess began, without hurry, to stack his chips. "It's your hands. When you've got something, they stop." She nodded at his right hand, which was still riffling three red chips. "You haven't stopped doing that since I sat down. You had nothing." She stacked another column. "You did it on the stage this morning, too, before you went down to the umbrella stand. Your hands stopped. That's when I knew you'd thought of something."

Archie sat at the end of his grandfather's table and watched her stack them.

His hands, he realized, had stopped.

"Thank you, Mrs. Fairweather," said Tess, standing. She left the chips where they were and picked up her coat. "Walk me out, Mr. Fairweather."

---

The porch was cold and black, and the stars were out thick over the harbor. Her gray car was parked beside the hearse, so precisely parallel to it that it looked measured.

"You could have called," Archie said. "About the autopsy. You didn't have to drive all the way down here."

"No."

"So why did you?"

Tess pulled on her gloves. "I wanted to see your face when I told you to stop."

"And?"

She looked at him in the light from the dining-room windows for a moment. Then she went down the steps.

"And you're not going to," she said, over her shoulder, and got into her car.

He stood on the porch until her taillights had gone down Shore Road, and then went back in. Bev was dealing. June had gone back to her book on the stairs and was pretending she'd never looked up from it.

Vivienne was gathering the chips Tess had left. She stacked them neatly, red on red, and slid the whole pile back across the felt to Archie's place, and didn't look up.

"I like her," she said. "She doesn't need you to be wonderful."


---

# Chapter 15

## Hauling

At five-thirty on Monday morning the town float was black and slick with frost, and Archie Fairweather stood on it in his buffalo-check coat with a white plastic bucket in both arms, so happy he could barely keep still.

It was cold — real cold, the first of the year, the kind that got into the bones of your face. The harbor was flat and dark and steaming faintly. Out past the moorings the bell buoy rang once, slow. Up at the head of the wharf the windows of the Galley were yellow, and in one of them, by the kitchen door, three shapes sat around a round table. One of them was looking down at the float. When Archie raised his free hand, it looked away.

Sixty-two.

"You brought it," said Dale Pinkham.

"I said I would."

"Ayuh. You did." The chief came down the ramp in orange oilskin bibs and a hooded sweatshirt that said KNOX COUNTY SHERIFF'S OFFICE SOFTBALL 2004, carrying a thermos and two pairs of rubber gloves. He peered into the bucket. Harold peered back.

"Gramp, we're losing the tide," said a voice from the boat.

*Second Wind* lay against the float with her engine already rumbling, a white lobster boat with a square wheelhouse and a work deck crowded with gear: the hydraulic hauler at the starboard rail, a long wooden bait box, a seawater tank. A girl in orange bibs was standing on the rail with a line in her hand. She was sixteen, broad-shouldered, with a round freckled face and a fuzzy black hat pulled down to her eyebrows, and she looked at Archie and his bucket with the open, cheerful contempt of the young for the useless.

"You're June's dad," she said.

"I am."

"June says you can't drive stick."

"June is a gifted researcher. Shouldn't you be in school?"

"Teacher workshop." She looked at the bucket. "June's asleep. June said you'd be late."
