"Permission to come aboard?"

"Don't stand in the rope," said Mattie Pinkham, and jumped down onto the deck.

---

They went out through the moorings in the dark, past *Miss Dottie* and *Three Daughters*, with their running lights on and the diesel thumping under Archie's feet. The bait box held a hundred pounds of salted herring and was the worst thing Archie had ever smelled. He stood in the stern with Harold's bucket against his chest and watched the steeple and the opera house bell tower go black against a sky that was going gray along the edge.

Dale throttled down off the end of Shag Ledge, inside the harbor, where the water was still flat.

"Here's good," he said. "Deep enough. Rocky bottom. He'll be all right here."

Archie set the bucket on the rail and lifted Harold out. Harold raised both claws, which were no longer banded — Archie had cut the bands on Friday because it seemed rude — and waved them.

"Hold on," said Dale. "Measure him first."

"Dale, he's from the co-op. He's legal. I paid for him."

"Measure him anyhow." Dale held out a brass gauge, worn bright at the edges. "You don't put a lobster over the side of my boat without you measure him. Them's the rules."

Archie measured him, eye socket to the back of the shell. Legal. Dale nodded, satisfied.

"Give him here a second," said Mattie. She took Harold from Archie with one hand, flipped him over with total efficiency, and looked at his underside. "Huh," she said. "Harold's a girl."

Archie stared at her.

"Swimmerets." Mattie pointed. "See, soft. That's a female. You named a girl Harold."

"She never said a word," said Archie. "Not one word in four days. She let me go on calling her Harold, and she never once corrected me." He took the lobster back and held her up at eye level in the gray light. "Harold. You absolute professional."

Mattie laughed out loud, a big unguarded honk that went off across the water. Even Dale's mustache moved.

Archie leaned over the rail and lowered Harold into the harbor. She hung for a second in the green-black water just under the surface, claws spread, and then she tipped forward and backed away down into the dark, tail flicking, and was gone.

He stayed leaning over the rail a moment longer than he needed to.

"Okay," said Mattie. "Can we *work* now?"

---

They worked.

It was a rhythm, and he saw it at once. Dale brought the boat alongside a buoy — white with a green band, his colors — and Mattie gaffed the line and threw it over the hauler's sheave, and the hauler whined, and the line came up dripping and coiled itself on the deck, and then the trap broke the surface with water pouring out of it and Mattie swung it onto the rail. Open the door. Pick the lobsters. Measure. Throw back the shorts. Band the keepers. Bait bag out, bait bag in. Shut the door. Push it over. Next.

"Let me do one," said Archie.

Mattie looked at her grandfather. Dale looked at the sky, going pink now in the east, and at the long string of buoys ahead of them. Three hundred traps to get up before the weather. He looked at Archie's face, and sighed.

"One," he said. "Mattie, stand by him. Archie, stay out of the bight."

"What's the bight?"

"The loop in the rope on the deck. When the trap goes over, the line goes with it. Your foot's in the bight, you go with it." Dale said it without any drama. "Fella over in Tenants Harbor went with it in 2011."

Archie stepped very carefully out of the bight.

He did one. He did it well. The trap came up streaming with four lobsters inside and a crab and a sculpin, and he had his hands on them before Mattie could say anything — the gauge, short, over it goes; the gauge, keeper; the band snapping onto the claw clean on the first try. He threw the sculpin back with an apology. He stuffed a new bait bag with herring, which was cold and slippery and unspeakable, and slid the trap over, and kept his feet out of the bight while the line went whipping after it.

He straightened up, breathing hard, with herring scales on his coat.

Mattie looked at the two banded lobsters in the tank. She looked at him.

"Not bad," she said, as though it had been taken out of her with pliers.

Archie was so pleased he could have sung. It lasted until they cleared the ledge.

Dale opened the throttle and took them out past the end of the ledge, where the harbor stopped being a harbor. The swell came in under the boat — long and slow and gray, nothing much, Dale said afterward; you'd hardly call it a sea. *Second Wind* lifted her bow and lay over a little and came back, and lifted, and lay over. The diesel fumes rolled back over the stern. The bait box breathed.

Archie had escaped from a safe at the bottom of Lake Tahoe. He had held his breath for four minutes in a water tank on live television.

He lasted about six minutes.

He made it to the lee rail, which was the one dignity he was afforded, and hung over it and was sick into the Gulf of Maine, comprehensively, while the gulls, who knew a good thing, came in low over the wake.

"Gramp," said Mattie, with enormous satisfaction. "He's chumming."

"Leave him be."

The radio in the wheelhouse crackled. A voice came out of it, flat and amused, from a boat Archie could see a quarter mile off to the east, a green hull with its hauler going.

"*Second Wind, Second Wind. That your magician, Dale?*"

Dale picked up the handset. "Ayuh."

"*He all right?*"

"He's fine."

"*Looks like he's making something disappear.*"

There was laughter on the radio — not just from the green boat. Somebody else on the channel, and somebody else. The whole working harbor was on that channel at this hour, and the whole working harbor had just watched him do it.

He was sick again.

---

Dale took pity on him after a while and throttled back and let the boat lie quieter in the lee of Shag Ledge, close to the bell buoy, while Mattie baited bags. Archie sat on an upturned bucket in the stern with his back against the traps and a cup of coffee from Dale's thermos in both hands, and the sun came up red over the islands to the east, and slowly the sea agreed to stop moving quite so much.

The bell buoy was thirty yards off — a big red iron cage on a float, with four clappers hung round a bell, rolling with the swell. Up close it wasn't a sound so much as a feeling: a slow, iron, irregular tolling that came across the water and into the deck and up through the soles of his boots.

"Right about here," Dale said. He was standing in the wheelhouse door with his own coffee, looking at the buoy. "Where they found the skiff. Eighty-eight. I was a deputy then, Knox County. Sheriff sent three of us down for the search, and I rode out on one of the Coombs boats. Every boat in the harbor went out. Coast Guard come up from Rockland." He tipped his cup toward the point. "She was drifting down toward the Light on the ebb. Oars shipped. Nobody in her. His cloak folded on the thwart, neat as you please, like he'd just stepped out for a minute."

Archie looked at the buoy. The bell tolled, and tolled.

"I was there that night," he said. "At the show. I was ten. I was in the second row."

Dale didn't say anything. He nodded slowly, and kept looking at the buoy, and after a while Mattie stopped baiting bags and looked at it too.

Dale's phone rang in the pocket of his bibs.

He looked at the screen and stepped into the wheelhouse to take it, pulling the door to behind him. Archie could see him through the salt-streaked window: the phone at his ear, his free hand flat on the wheel, nodding. Saying *ayuh* and *ayuh* and *what time*. Not looking at the stern.

He was in there a long time.

When he came out he stood in the wheelhouse door with the phone in his hand. He looked at Mattie and said, "Go mind the wheel a minute, Matts," and she looked at his face and went into the wheelhouse without arguing, which Archie gathered was rare. Then Dale came and sat down on the bait box beside Archie's bucket, heavily, and put his elbows on his knees.

"I'm not supposed to tell you this," he said.

"Then don't, Dale. I mean it. I don't want to get you—"

"That was the sergeant." Dale looked at his big hands. "The blood came back from the lab. Preliminary, she says, but it's what you said. Aconite." He said the word carefully. "They've opened it up. It's a homicide now. State Police, the AG's office, all of it."

Somewhere off to the east, the green boat's hauler whined.

"I shouldn't have told you," said Dale.

Archie looked down into his coffee. "Thank you."

"Lieutenant Brannock's down from Augusta. He's setting up in the select board room. He wants you in at two o'clock this afternoon." Dale turned his head and looked at Archie properly for the first time since the phone rang. "Bring a lawyer."

"I don't need a lawyer, Dale. I haven't done anything. I'll go in and explain about the trumpet and—"

"Ayuh," said Dale Pinkham. He stood up off the bait box, and put his hand on Archie's shoulder, once, and went back toward the wheel. "Bring one anyhow."


---

# Chapter 16

## Person of Interest

The select board room was on the ground floor of the town hall, and it was the coldest room Archie had ever been in that wasn't a walk-in freezer or a lake.

It was a long, plain room with an oak table, a flag on a stand, and a wall of framed photographs of past selectmen, most of them in beards. The radiator under the window was clanking with enormous energy and producing no heat.

The State Police had moved in: two laptops, a printer on a folding chair, an empty whiteboard, three banker's boxes, and a large Dunkin' cup in front of the man in the middle chair.

"Mr. Fairweather," said the man. "Sit down. Thank you for coming in."

Lieutenant Neil Brannock was in his fifties, tall even sitting down, with a gray crewcut, a wedding ring, and a pair of reading glasses that he took off as Archie came in and put on again a moment later, irritably, to read something. He wore a navy State Police fleece zipped to the chin over his suit. Tess sat at the end of the table to his left with a notebook and her charcoal coat still on. Her breath showed faintly in the air.

Archie sat. The folding chair was so cold he could feel it through his trousers.

"You don't have a lawyer with you," said Brannock.

"I don't need one."

"Everybody says that." Brannock pressed a button on a small digital recorder. He gave the date and the time and the names of the people in the room, in a flat, efficient voice. "Mr. Fairweather, you're here voluntarily. You're not under arrest. You can leave whenever you want, and you can have a lawyer whenever you want. Do you understand that?"

"Yes."

Brannock sniffed. He looked up over his glasses. "Have you been fishing?"

"Hauling. With Chief Pinkham. This morning." Archie had showered twice. The coat had not. "It's herring. I'm told it doesn't come out."

"It doesn't," said Tess, without looking up.

"Lieutenant, before we start, may I just say what I came here to say? Because I think it'll save us all a great deal of time." Archie leaned forward. "It wasn't the tea. It was the trumpet. Your sergeant saw how it's done yesterday. I can show you in thirty seconds."

He had a deck of cards in the inside pocket of his coat. He took it out, plainly, and fanned it on the oak.

Nobody had asked him to. Brannock looked at the cards and then at Archie, and didn't stop him, which Archie took for encouragement.

"Watch my right hand," Archie said. He picked a card out of the fan with his right — the eight of clubs — and showed it to the recorder, and to Tess, and to Brannock, and slid it into the middle of the deck with a great deal of fuss and a squared tap. "Now. Where's the eight?"

"On top," said Brannock.

Archie stopped.

"Your left thumb," said Brannock. He hadn't moved. "When you squared the deck. Your right hand made all the noise and your left thumb pushed the card back up. I don't know what that's called. I saw it because I was watching your left hand, because I was told that's what you'd do." He took his glasses off. "Do it again. Slowly. And tell me, the whole time, where your left hand is."

Archie looked at Tess. Tess was writing.

"That's the problem I have, Mr. Fairweather," Brannock said. "You've just shown me — voluntarily, for the tape — that you can make two people look at one of your hands while the other one does what it likes. That's not a thing I had to prove. You came in and did it." He tapped the deck. "Put those away, please."

Archie put them away.

There was a knock, and the door opened before anyone answered it. Two large young men in canvas work jackets came in backward, carrying an object the size of a jukebox wrapped in blankets. Behind them came a third man with a dolly, on which was strapped a tall finned oil-filled radiator, the industrial kind.

"Delivery," said the first young man, consulting a clipboard. "Fairweather? From Portland. Espresso machine, two-group, and the heater. Where do you want it?"

Brannock looked at the espresso machine, and at the heater, and then at Archie.

"Dale told me this room was freezing," Archie said. "And I've had the coffee at the Dunkin' on Route 1. Lieutenant, I'm not trying to bribe anybody, I'm trying to keep you all from getting pneumonia. It's paid for. It's a gift to the town. You can leave it here when you go."

"Take it out," said Brannock.

"It's paid for," said the young man.

"Then take it out paid for." Brannock didn't raise his voice. "All of it. Thank you."

The young men looked at each other. They began, with some difficulty, to back the espresso machine out the door.

"Leave the heater," said Tess.

Brannock turned his head.

"It's forty-eight degrees in here, Lieutenant," Tess said. She hadn't stopped writing. "I checked."

Brannock looked at her for a second. Then he looked at the radiator, which had chosen that moment to clank twice like a man hitting a pipe with a wrench. He said nothing. The third young man plugged the heater into the wall by Tess's chair, turned the dial to high, and left, and the door shut on the sound of the espresso machine going down the hall on its blankets.

Brannock opened the laptop in front of him and turned it so Archie could see.

"I've been through every frame Marchand Media has given us," he said. "There's a lot of it. This is the part I keep coming back to."

It was the stage in the work light: Archie in his black shirt with the small end of the trumpet at his lips, the raspberry coming out of the laptop's little speaker thin and tinny, the crew laughing. The time code in the corner said 19:35:12.

Brannock clicked. Archie filling the two cups from Bev's thermos. Ivy's hand coming flat over her mother's place. Lorelei laughing, taking the cup. *To the vanishing man.* 19:41:50.

"You poured her tea," said Brannock.

"I drank mine. The same tea. From the same thermos. I drank all of it."

"You drank from a cup you poured. She drank from a cup you poured." Brannock leaned back. "We went to collect the thermos this morning. It wasn't on the table when the evidence tech bagged it Saturday night. It went home in your mother's handbag. Mrs. Thibodeau washed it yesterday afternoon. The thermos and both cups, with soap. Somebody brought it back to her at about noon on Sunday. Who was that?"

The radiator clanked. The new heater ticked as it warmed.

"Me," said Archie.

"Noon on Sunday. Fourteen hours after you told Sergeant Calloway, in the hospital, that Lorelei Crane had been poisoned with aconite. Before anybody else in the state had used that word."

"My mother told me to take it back."

It sounded exactly as bad as it was. Across the table, Tess's pen paused, and went on.

"Mr. Fairweather," said Brannock, quite gently, "you knew the poison before the lab did. You poured her the only drink on that tape. You had two million dollars on her being a fraud, and forty minutes later she was doing your grandfather's voice in the dark. And the morning after she died, the cup she drank from went back to a diner sink."

Archie sat in the cold folding chair and felt the room tilt. It was only the shape of things, laid end to end by a man who was good at his job.

He thought about June counting on her fingers at the breakfast table. Then he told them the rest, because it was true and because they'd find it anyway.

"There's wolfsbane in my garden," he said. "Monkshood. A whole stand of it, along the fence at the bottom, where it goes down to the rocks. My grandfather planted it. He thought the name was funny, for Halloween. My mother cut some on Thursday and put it in a jug on the kitchen table." He looked at Brannock. "Half the gardens in this town have it. But mine's one of them, and I'd rather you heard it from me."

Brannock looked at him without expression. Then he wrote something down, carefully.

Tess put her pen flat on the notebook.

"Lieutenant," she said. "For the recording. Mr. Fairweather's here voluntarily, and he's now said three times he doesn't want a lawyer. Before we go any further I'd like him to hear again that he can stop this and have one."

Brannock turned his head and looked at her.

"He's heard it, Sergeant."

"I'd like him to hear it again. On the tape."

Brannock took his glasses off. "Mr. Fairweather, you can stop this interview at any time and consult a lawyer. Do you want to?"

"No," said Archie. "I want you to listen to me about the raspberry."

"The raspberry."

"Seven thirty-five. On your laptop. I put the small end of that trumpet in my mouth and blew, in front of two cameras and a dozen people. And I'm sitting here. I've never felt better in my life, apart from this morning on the boat." He tapped the table. "That horn was clean at seven thirty-five. Whatever was put on it was put on after. That's your window, Lieutenant. Seven thirty-five to eight o'clock. That's who you want."

Brannock actually thought about it; Archie watched him do it.

"Or," he said, "it's the one moment on that tape when a professional sleight-of-hand man had his hands, and his mouth, on the thing that killed her." He put his glasses back on. "Mr. Fairweather, your whole career is getting a camera to record something that didn't happen. You're very good at it. I've seen the riverboat. I'm not going to take the tape's word for what was or wasn't on your lips."

Archie opened his mouth and had nothing to put in it.

"Sergeant Calloway let you onto a sealed scene yesterday," Brannock went on, in the same flat voice, "and you picked a lock, and then you picked up a piece of evidence with your bare hands and put your prints all over it."

"That's on me, Lieutenant," said Tess.

"Yes," said Brannock. "It is." He didn't look at her. "We'd like to search your garden. And your kitchen. You can make us get a warrant."

"Search it," said Archie. "Search the garden. Search the house. Lenny'll show you the trapdoor in the pantry. Search the trunk in the turret, if you can get it open. I've been trying since August, and if you crack it I'll owe you dinner for the rest of your life."

Brannock almost smiled. It was there and then it wasn't.

"Sergeant Calloway will bring a consent form out to the house this afternoon." He clicked the recorder off. "And Mr. Fairweather. Don't go near my witnesses. Ivy Crane. Shannon Mayo. Warren Gilley. The production company. Kevin Duchesne. All of them."

Archie stood up. His knees were stiff from the cold; the heater by Tess's chair had only just begun to breathe warm air into the corner of the room.

"Lieutenant," he said, "I live here. I buy my coffee from Shannon Mayo. Warren Gilley is selling me the building you're sitting in." He buttoned his coat, which smelled of herring. "Where exactly would you like me to go?"

Brannock looked at him over his glasses.

"Somewhere I can find you," he said.


---

# Chapter 17

## Malvolio

Pudding Lane climbed away from Water Street for six houses, then lost interest and turned into a footpath through somebody's blueberry barren. Hollis Tibbetts lived in the fourth house: a small weathered Cape with a picket fence and a blue front door. At six o'clock on Monday evening it was full dark, and every window she owned was lit.

Archie parked the hearse along her fence, which used up most of the fence, and sat for a minute listening to the engine tick.

He had not gone home. A news van from Portland had been parked at the bottom of Shore Road since four, and his mother had texted: *Don't come in the front, darling. There is a young man with a microphone in the rhododendrons and Lenny has gone out to speak to him.* Then, five minutes later: *Lenny is back. Don't ask.* So Archie had driven around the peninsula instead, twice, in a hearse with his grandfather's name on the side in gold, which was not how a person of interest kept his head down. At five Hollis had called. She'd pulled some costumes for his show, and there was a fish chowder on the stove, and she had a fair idea which one he needed more.

She opened the door before he reached the step — oatmeal cardigan, fingerless gloves, glasses on their beaded chain, and an apron that said MERROW PLAYERS · *THE MUSIC MAN*. She looked him over. Thirty-five years of sophomores had been looked over like that, on mornings they'd turned up without the book.

"You've been in that select board room," she said.

"Two hours and a quarter."

"It shows. Shoes off. Mind the cat."

The cat was on the third stair. He was large and gray and flat-faced, with yellow eyes, and he sat with his front feet together and considered Archie, who was not on his list.

"That's Malvolio," said Hollis. "He bit the Methodist minister at Easter, so you're in good company."

"Why Malvolio?"

"He's pompous, he has no sense of humor, and he believes the lady of the house is secretly in love with him."

"Is she?"

"Kitchen," said Hollis.

He followed her through the front room, which had stopped being a front room a long time ago. The Merrow Players' wardrobe lived there now: thirty years of shows standing about on dress forms in the lamplight like guests nobody had told to sit down. A widow in black bombazine. A Union cavalryman. A Puss in Boots with one boot. A sewing machine on a card table still had a hem caught under its foot, and there were books on every flat surface. A woodstove ticked in the corner. Against the far wall stood an upright piano under a fringed shawl, its top crowded with photographs.

The kitchen was low and warm and smelled of onions and salt pork and hot milk. Hollis ladled two bowls from a dented pot, set out oyster crackers and the heel of a loaf and a saucer of butter, and sat down across the oilcloth from him.

"Eat first," she said. "Then you can tell me how dreadful everything is."

He ate. It was haddock and potatoes and a great deal of black pepper, and it was the best thing that had happened to him since Saturday night. He finished the bowl. She took it and filled it again without asking.

Halfway through the second bowl, something heavy landed in his lap.

Malvolio turned around twice, kneaded Archie's thighs with great thoroughness, and lay down. He was heavy and warm.

Hollis stared at him. "Well," she said. "I'll be."

"Animals love me."

"That one doesn't. He's never sat on anybody in his life." She looked at the cat with real concern. "He must be coming down with something."

Archie couldn't reach the bowl now without disturbing him. He took a quarter out of his pocket instead and walked it across the knuckles of his right hand above the cat's head — over the forefinger, under the middle, over the ring finger and back. The yellow eyes followed it. A gray paw came up off Archie's knee and hung in the air, considering.

"Don't let him," said Hollis. "He'll have your finger off at the joint."

The paw went back down. Malvolio shut his eyes. He had decided the quarter was beneath him.

"So," said Hollis. "The show."

"I don't know that there's going to be a show."

"There is going to be a show. It's on the posters. Deb Coombs put one up in the library over the new fiction, which she has never done for anybody, including the governor." She buttered a piece of bread and handed it to him. "Go on, then. Say it."

He said it. He hadn't meant to say all of it, but the kitchen was warm and the cat was heavy and nobody here was taking notes. He kept seeing Lorelei on the stage boards with her head in the crook of his arm. She'd asked him if he'd caught her, and he'd told her no. He'd sat across a fire from her on Friday night and liked her better than anybody he'd met in years, and now she was in the medical examiner's office in Augusta, and if he'd shouted for the lights five minutes sooner—

"Stop that," said Hollis.

"I'm only—"

"I know what you're only. Stop it. You didn't put her in that chair."

"She was funny," Archie said. "Hollis, she was so funny."

"I expect she was." Hollis pushed the butter toward him. "Eat your chowder."

---

When he'd finished, she made him put the cat on the floor, which Malvolio took as a betrayal of the first order, and marched him into the front room.

She had pulled three things for him and hung them on the back of the door. He tried on eleven. A fez. Puss in Boots's one boot, which made Hollis bark — the short, sharp note that was her laugh — without looking up from the tailcoat she was letting out. Felt horns and a pitchfork from a *Faust* nobody in Merrow would admit to having seen. He was in front of the long mirror in a striped convict suit from some melodrama, holding the number board under his chin, when he stopped clowning and looked at himself.

"The lieutenant would like me in this," he said.

"Then take it off. Don't give him ideas." Hollis was sitting on the arm of the sofa with her glasses on and the tailcoat across her knees. "What did he do to you?"

"Nothing. He was perfectly fair. That's what's so awful about him." Archie started on the buttons. "He's got film of me pouring her tea. He's got film of me with my mouth on the horn. And he said I fool cameras for a living."

"You do."

"He made it sound like a crime."

"It isn't one, or they'd have to put a fence around Las Vegas." She bit off a thread. "That man sat in my booth this morning with a Dunkin' cup the size of a fire extinguisher and asked me to explain the dimmer board to him slowly. So I explained it slowly, twice, and told him there'd be a quiz on Friday." She held the tailcoat up to the lamp. "Archie Fairweather, I spent thirty-five years in a classroom watching people decide what they were going to be. I can spot a fool across a gymnasium with the lights off. You're not one. You're a show-off, which is a different thing entirely, and it isn't fatal."

He stood there in the convict trousers with his mouth open, grinning for the first time all day.

"Is that your professional assessment?"

"It's going on your permanent record. Try the cape."

The cape was the last thing on the door: black wool, floor-length, with a high collar. It wasn't Gus's. Gus's lay folded on the trunk at home. But it was near enough, and when he put it on and turned to the mirror, the lamp behind him did something to the shape of it, and he stood still.

Hollis set the tailcoat down.

"I was in the booth that night," she said. "Did you know that?"

He didn't.

"Twenty-nine. Running the board for him with my coat on, because the radiators were worse then, if you can believe it." She was looking at him in the mirror. "From up there you can see past the edge of the proscenium into the stage-left wing. Nobody down in the house can. Before the Chair, he stood in that wing with his hand on the curtain for a full minute. The stage manager was beside himself. Gus didn't move. He was looking out at the second row." She got up and came over and lifted the cape off Archie's shoulders, briskly, and folded it over her arm. "I always supposed he was looking at you."

He'd been ten years old in a clip-on velvet bow tie, and the minute had simply been part of the waiting.

"I didn't know that," he said.

"Well. Now you do." She was already turning away. "Sit down, I want to see you in boots."

He sat on the piano bench and pulled on a pair of cavalier boots from somebody's *Three Musketeers*, and Malvolio came in from the kitchen, looked at him with loathing, and climbed back into his lap. The photographs on the piano crowded just above Archie's shoulder: senior classes on the gym steps, a dozen Players casts in a dozen costumes, a black dog in a snowbank, a young couple on the running board of an old Ford, and a boy of about twelve on a dock, sunburned to the ears, holding up a mackerel in both hands as if it had put up a tremendous fight.

"Who's on the Ford?"

"My mother and father. He never once let her drive it." Hollis was hunting through a cigar box of buttons. "She drove it the day he went into the hospital and backed it into the post office."

"And the big-game fisherman?"

"Danny. My son." She handed him a waistcoat over the cat's head. "Try this."

It was plum velvet, with a shawl collar and jet buttons, cut long in the body. He stood up, dislodging Malvolio a second time, and put it on, and it fit everywhere a waistcoat had never once fit him off a rack.

"Made for a Scrooge in 2009," said Hollis. "He came down with shingles the week we opened. Nobody's ever worn it." She stood back with her head on one side and nodded. "That one. And the tailcoat, now I've let it out. And a box of white gloves for your ushers. Now go home. You've got a daughter."

She loaded him at the door: the waistcoat over one arm, the tailcoat over the other, the box of gloves on top, and a foil-covered dish of chowder for his mother wedged under his chin. He went down the path under the porch light with his arms full of velvet and got everything into the hearse without spilling the chowder, which he counted as the first unqualified success of his week.

The news van was gone from the bottom of Shore Road. He was halfway up the drive before he noticed he'd been humming.

The kitchen light was on. June was at the end of the farm table in pajama pants and her school cardigan, with a pencil through her bun and her laptop open. It was half past nine. She had clearly been waiting up, and had clearly decided not to look as if she had.

"Put that down, Dad," she said. "I need to show you something. Upstairs."


---

# Chapter 18

## Harborview Antiques

June's room was the only room in Fairweather House that didn't do anything.

She had chosen it in August for exactly that reason, after an afternoon going around the second floor with a flashlight and a butter knife, tapping paneling and pressing knots in floorboards, while Lenny followed her with his hands in his sweater pockets and said "No" at intervals, meaning there was nothing there. It was a narrow room at the back of the house, over the kitchen, with a ceiling that sloped down to a single window on the harbor. A desk made from an old door laid across two filing cabinets. A narrow bed under a quilt Vivienne had bought at a church fair. Her Doc Martens side by side, toes to the wall. And above the desk, four thumbtacks pressed into the plaster in a neat rectangle, with nothing between them.

June sat cross-legged on the bed with the laptop on her knees. Archie sat on her desk chair, which had been built for someone a foot shorter, with his knees somewhere near his chin.

"Three conditions," said June.

"I love conditions."

"One, you don't take over. Two, you don't do anything tonight. Three, whatever you do do, you tell me first. Before. Not after."

"June—"

"Those are the conditions."

He looked at her: the pencil through the bun, the freckles, the set of her mouth. It was the face she wore at the podium, and he had once watched it dismantle the state champion from Bangor in a cross-examination that lasted four minutes.

"No," he said. "No conditions, no laptop. You're sixteen. You have school in the morning. There's a lieutenant downtown who thinks somebody in this house—"

"I know what the lieutenant thinks. Grandma told me at dinner. Then Lenny told me again, because he didn't think Grandma had said it right." June didn't raise her voice. "So I'm not going to sit downstairs doing chemistry about it. Those are the conditions. You can have them or you can go to bed."

He could have gone to bed. He considered it, honestly, for almost a second.

"Fine," he said. "Show me."

She turned the laptop around.

It was an auction site, the big one, open to a seller's page: HARBORVIEW ANTIQUES · MERROW, MAINE · member since 2011 · 214 ratings · 100% positive. Below that was a grid of little square photographs — a painted lobster buoy, a ship's lantern, a stack of Grange cookbooks, a brass barometer.

"I was looking for a poster," June said. "One of Gus's."

"What for?"

"My own reasons."

He looked at the four thumbtacks over the desk, and then away again. He didn't ask. He'd been practicing not asking since a Friday afternoon in the library.

"I set an alert," June went on. "'Great Fairweather.' Mostly it's programs and reprints. But in September there were three real ones. Originals, framed. All sold by Harborview Antiques, of Merrow." She clicked. "Opera house provenance."

The first listing came up: a framed poster leaning against a wall, lit by a flash, a man in white tie striding into a brick wall with half his body already through it. THE GREAT FAIRWEATHER WALKS THROUGH WALLS! *From the Merrow Opera House (Maine), removed during restoration.* SOLD · $1,150.

Archie knew the frame. Black, with a narrow gold lip inside, the twin of the frame on the ASYLUM poster in the opera house stairwell. A few feet past that one, on the landing, there was a clean pale rectangle on the old cream paint with two nail holes along the top. He'd walked past it forty times since August. He'd assumed Hollis had taken something down to be reframed.

"Removed during restoration," he said. "I'm the restoration."

"There are two more." June clicked. RADIO'S MASTER OF MYSTERY, 1949, $1,400, to someone in Connecticut. And a third, which made him lean forward so fast the little chair squealed: a girl of about fourteen in spangles and a feathered headdress, lying in a long box with her head out of one end and her feet out of the other, while a tall silver-haired man in a cloak stood over her with a saw. THE GREAT FAIRWEATHER SAWS A LADY IN HALF — TWICE NIGHTLY! 1966. SOLD · $975.

"That's your grandmother," he said.

June looked. "Seriously?"

"Four thousand times, she says. You'll hear about every one of them." He stared at the screen. "Somebody sold my mother to a man in Ohio for nine hundred and seventy-five dollars."

"In two pieces," said June. "Keep going. This is the part."

The fourth listing had gone up on Saturday morning at 6:48. It wasn't framed. The poster lay flat on a desk under a lamp, curling at the corners, with a soft crease across the middle: THE GREAT FAIRWEATHER · HALLOWEEN AT THE OPERA HOUSE · 1962. *Rare. From storage at the Merrow Opera House.* Buy It Now, $1,200. Across the top ran a gray bar: *This listing was ended by the seller on Sunday at 12:04 p.m.*

"From storage," said Archie slowly.

There was a big gray steel flat file at the back of the prop room, wide shallow drawers full of old programs and, Hollis had told him once, who knew what else. He'd never opened it.

"Now look at the third photo," said June.

It was the same poster, closer, the lamp moved for a better angle. In the bottom left corner, half cut off by the edge of the picture, was the corner of a dark green leather desk blotter with a gold-tooled border, and a few letters stamped in gold: *—LEY INSURANCE & REA—*

June didn't say anything. She let him have it. She'd learned that from someone.

"Gilley," said Archie.

"I've been in that office. Grandma took me in September when she was fighting with him about the homeowner's policy. He's got that blotter. I think it was his grandfather's." June pulled the laptop back toward her. "And I asked the seller a question. About the cookbooks. He answered in four minutes and signed it *W.*"

"You asked about a Grange cookbook to identify a seller."

"It's a good cookbook. There's a whole chapter on aspic."

Archie got up out of the little chair. He couldn't sit in it anymore, and it wasn't his knees. It was the thing that always started in him: the small sweet click of a lock turning over in the dark. He walked the four steps the room allowed, to the window and back, winding his grandfather's watch.

"Saturday night, at the table," he said, "Lorelei leans across Ivy and says something in Warren's ear, and he goes white. I saw it, June. I was right there. Seven thirty-eight. A few minutes later they call cut, the work light goes down, Lorelei and Ivy go downstairs, and everybody else gets up and wanders around. Not Warren. Warren sits at his place in the half-dark with that horn a reach from his elbow." He turned at the window. "And then at a quarter to eight he's suddenly sick. Up and past me and the chief and down the stairs, holding his stomach. Nobody's sick on cue, Junebug. That's an exit. You get off the stage before anybody looks at the table."

"Dad."

"It isn't the key. I thought it was the key, but whatever he did in that prop room on Friday, I put my mouth on the horn at seven thirty-five on Saturday and here I am. It was after. It was at the table." He stopped at the end of the bed. "And if Lorelei knew about the posters — and she knew everything, she had Ivy, Ivy researched every one of us — then she wasn't just whispering to him. She was *telling* him. And the sale doesn't close until the fifteenth of November. Something bad enough happens in that theater before then, with the new owner standing in the middle of it, and it goes back to the town, and Warren gets his condominiums."

June looked up at him from the bed. Then she looked down at the laptop and did something unnecessary with the trackpad, and when she looked up again her face was back to normal.

"That's a theory," she said. "It's not evidence. Everything after the blotter is you."

"Everything after the blotter is *beautiful*."

"That's not a legal standard."

"It should be." He sat down on the end of the bed, which bounced her, and she grabbed the laptop. "June. You found this. In an evening. The State Police have had three days and a lieutenant from Augusta, and they're looking at *me*. You found Warren Gilley's blotter."

"I found a corner of a blotter."

"It's a beautiful corner."

She pressed her lips together hard. It didn't entirely work.

"Condition three," she said.

"I'm going to ask Warren about it."

"Where?"

He didn't answer fast enough. He was already seeing the Galley at seven in the morning: the long counter, the booths full, Warren at the end stool in his bow tie. He was thinking of how a room leans in when you lay three things face down on a counter and ask somebody to pick one. He could feel it in his hands.

June closed the laptop.

"Dad," she said. "Don't do it in public."

"Junebug. Would I?"

She looked at him a while longer. Then she lay back on the pillow, pulled the church-fair quilt up to her chin, and rolled over to face the wall with the four thumbtacks on it.

"The printer's in Lenny's workshop," she said. "Don't wake him up."

He went down the back stairs in his socks with her laptop under his arm, already deciding which stool.


---

# Chapter 19

## Town Business

By seven o'clock on Tuesday morning the fog had come in off the harbor so thick that the Galley's windows showed nothing but white, and the bell over the door rang every minute or so to let in another wet wool coat and another person who had heard something.

Archie had been on the stool nearest the register since ten to seven, with a manila folder under his elbow and a mug of coffee he had poured himself.

That was new. Shannon Mayo was working the counter — ponytail, apron, the gray thermal shirt — and she'd come down the line with the pot, filling mugs without asking, *there you go, hon*, *morning, hon*, and when she'd reached Archie she had set the pot down on the counter in front of him and walked on. He'd poured. It was the first time in nine weeks he had poured his own coffee in the Galley, and he'd done it carefully, without spilling a drop, and nobody had seen him do it except everybody.

At the liars' table, Elwin and Dickie and Pickle looked out the window at the fog. Sixty-three.

Chief Pinkham was two stools down with a plate of corned beef hash. He'd looked at the folder twice, and he hadn't said one word about the riverboat. Hollis Tibbetts was in the corner booth by the window with the *Packet* folded to the crossword and a pot of tea, her glasses on the end of her nose. She'd lifted two fingers to Archie when he came in, and gone back to seven across.

Warren Gilley came in at seven exactly.

Camel overcoat, the fog beaded on its shoulders. A bow tie, navy with small white dots — a good one, a Sunday bow tie on a Tuesday. In one gloved hand he carried a bunch of yellow chrysanthemums in grocery-store cellophane. He hung the coat on the hook by the door, took the end stool by the pie case, laid the flowers carefully on the empty stool beside him, and said, "Two poached, Bev, dry wheat toast, thank you," in the voice of a man who had said exactly that at exactly this hour for thirty years.

"Morning, Warren," said Bev.

"Good morning."

Archie picked up his folder and his mug and moved down the counter to the stool on Warren's other side.

"Flowers, Warren," he said. "Courting?"

"Good morning, Mr. Fairweather." Warren didn't look at him. He unfolded his paper napkin and laid it across his knee.

"I've got something I'd like to show you."

"I'm having my breakfast."

"It'll take a minute. It's a trick, actually. You'll like it." Archie raised his voice — not by much; he never needed much — and the counter heard him, and the booths heard the counter. "I'll need three volunteers. Bev?"

"No."

"Chief?"

"Archie," said Dale Pinkham, low. "Maybe not here."

"Corey!"

Corey — C-plus, two *e*'s in *medium* — was already sliding out of the second booth, grinning, and the woman from the hardware store came with him, and a big lobsterman in orange bib pants whom Archie didn't know. Then there were three people standing at the counter and the whole room had turned in its seats to see what the magician was doing now, and he felt it come up through the floor into his feet: the lean. A room leaning in. There was nothing in the world like it. There never had been.

He took three sheets of paper out of the folder and laid them face down on the counter in a row, in front of Warren's coffee cup. He moved them — not fast; this wasn't Harvard Square — left over right, right over center, the old three-card shuffle, the paper whispering across the Formica.

"Find the Fairweather," he said. "Corey."

Corey pointed. Archie turned the sheet over.

It was the WALKS THROUGH WALLS listing, printed in color in Lenny's workshop at five that morning. SOLD · $1,150. *From the Merrow Opera House, removed during restoration.*

The woman from the hardware store read it out loud. Somebody in the booths said, "Isn't that the one off the stairs?"

"That's the one off the stairs." Archie turned the second sheet. RADIO'S MASTER OF MYSTERY, $1,400, Connecticut. Then the third, slowly, because it was the best one — a girl of fourteen in feathers, in two halves. "And that," he said, "is Vivienne Fairweather, sold to a gentleman in Ohio for nine hundred and seventy-five dollars. She doesn't know yet. I'm letting somebody else tell her."

The room laughed — it couldn't help itself — and Archie laughed with it, and beside him Warren Gilley sat with both hands flat on the counter on either side of his plate, his eggs going cold, and didn't move.

"All three sold in September," Archie said, "by a seller called Harborview Antiques, right here in Merrow. Lovely seller. A hundred percent positive. Answers your questions in four minutes and signs off *W.*" He took one more sheet out of the folder. This one he didn't lay face down. He held it up. "And on Saturday morning at 6:48 — the morning after somebody borrowed the key to the prop room — Harborview Antiques put up this. From storage. And if you look down in the corner of the photograph—" he laid it in front of Warren's plate and tapped it "—you'll see a very handsome green leather desk blotter. I wonder whose."

It went quiet in a wave, from the counter outward. Archie heard the coffee urn tick.

"Archie," said Pinkham.

He heard it. It didn't reach anywhere that made decisions. Every face in the Galley was turned his way, and he had been waiting sixty-three days for this room, and he went on.

"And Saturday night, at the table, with the cameras rolling, Lorelei Crane leaned across her daughter and whispered something in Warren's ear. I was sitting right there. I watched him go white." He turned on the stool to face Warren. "And then you sat at that table, Warren, with her trumpet a reach from your elbow, while everybody else was up and moving. And at a quarter to eight you were suddenly sick. What did she say to you? Did she tell you she knew? Because the sale doesn't close until the fifteenth — you told us that, right here, Friday, in front of witnesses — and if something terrible happened in that theater before then, with the new owner right in the middle of it—"

He didn't finish. He didn't need to. In one of the booths a woman said, "Oh, *Warren*," and someone else, in a voice Archie didn't know, said, "Good Lord."

Warren Gilley put down his fork.

He did it carefully, laying it across the rim of the plate at an angle, tines down. Then he picked up the sheet with the blotter on it and looked at it for a while, and set it down again on top of the others, and squared the edges.

"Carol," he said.

He said it to the counter. Then he turned his head and looked at Archie for the first time that morning, and his face wasn't white or pink or anything Archie had a word for. It was only tired.

"She told me Carol understood. About the pictures. She said Carol wanted me to know she wasn't angry." He said it quite clearly; everyone could hear. "My wife isn't dead, Mr. Fairweather. She's up the hill at Sea View. You've met her. You do your card tricks for her on Fridays. Marisol tells me she cried at one, but she cries at most things now, so I wouldn't take it to heart."

Archie could see her. Early sixties. Neat gray hair. A cardigan buttoned wrong. *Is Warren coming? Is it Tuesday? He comes on Tuesday.*

It was Tuesday. The flowers were on the stool.

"Eleven thousand six hundred dollars a month," said Warren. He said it to Archie, and to the counter, and to nobody. "That's what it costs. Her long-term care policy ran out in June. I've taken a second mortgage on a house my grandfather built. I sold the boat in July. In September I took three of your grandfather's pictures off the walls of that building and sold them to strangers, and on Friday I took one more out of the flat file in the prop room with Hollis's key, because the October bill was due Monday. I locked the door after. I thought I locked it." He took the napkin off his knee and folded it in half, and in half again. "Gus Fairweather bought her about five weeks. She doesn't know where she is for most of it. But her window looks down on the bay, and some mornings she likes to watch the boats go out. I'm sorry nobody asked him."

Nobody in the Galley moved.

Archie looked down at the counter, at his mother in two halves, at the man walking through the wall, at the corner of the green blotter. He knew what came next in a trick: you turned the cards back over and showed them the whole thing had been undone. Gus had taught him the other thing first, at four years old, at the kitchen table. Once they've seen it, they've seen it.

"Sit down, Archie," said Bev.

He was standing. He sat down.

In the corner booth, Hollis folded the *Packet*, let her glasses drop on their chain, and got up. She took Warren's camel coat down from the hook and brought it over and held it open behind him, and after a moment he stood and put his arms into it without a word. She picked the chrysanthemums up off the stool and put them in his hands.

"Come on, Warren," she said. "I'll drive you up. I want to see Carol anyway, and she likes my car."

She didn't look at Archie. She steered Warren toward the door with a hand under his elbow, and the room made way for them, booth by booth, people suddenly busy with their eggs.

Archie went after them. He caught them at the door, with the bell still ringing overhead and the fog pouring in around their ankles.

"Warren." His voice came out wrong. He didn't own a voice for this. "Let me. Please. Sea View — all of it, whatever it costs, as long as it costs. Nobody would have to know. I'd consider it a—"

Warren stopped on the step and turned around. He was a head shorter than Archie, sixty-one years old, in a camel coat and a polka-dot bow tie, holding a bunch of supermarket flowers, and he drew himself up to every inch he had.

"No," he said.

"Warren—"

"You bought the opera house out from under this town with your checkbook, Mr. Fairweather." His voice shook, once, and then it didn't. "You are not going to buy my wife."

He turned and went down the steps with Hollis into the fog, and the white closed over them before they'd gone the length of the wharf.

The bell stopped. Archie stood in the open doorway with the cold on his face and the whole Galley at his back, not making a sound.

Then someone came up the steps out of the fog and stopped in front of him. Charcoal coat. Braid. Her phone still in her hand. She must have passed them on the wharf; he could see in her face that she had. Behind him, two stools down from the register, Dale Pinkham was looking very hard at his own phone and not up.

"Outside," said Tess Calloway. "Now."


---

# Chapter 20

## Outside. Now.

She didn't stop at the bottom of the steps. She walked him the length of the town wharf — past the bait shed and the stacked traps and the fuel dock, all the way out to the end, where the planks stopped above the water — and she didn't say a word the whole way, and for once neither did he.

The fog was starting to lift. It had pulled back off the wharf and hung over the harbor in a low white bank, so that the nearest moorings showed, *Second Wind* and *Miss Dottie* dripping on their lines, and everything past them was only shapes. It was half past seven and cold enough to see his breath, and the granite memorial stood at the end of the wharf with the wet shining on it. Tess stopped beside it and turned around.

"Half that room can read lips," she said. "Keep your voice down."

"Sergeant—"

"Who printed those?"

"I did."

"Who found them?"

He didn't answer. She looked at him, and he watched her work it out and decide not to say it.

"Okay," she said. "Go ahead. Tell me."

He told her. He meant to tell it well — he'd told it beautifully fifteen minutes ago — but it didn't come out the same at the end of a wharf to one listener who wasn't leaning in. The whisper. Warren at his place in the half-dark with the horn at his elbow. The sudden stomach at a quarter to eight. The posters. The sale. He heard it getting thinner as he went, and went faster to make up for it, and finished with, "He had six minutes at that table, Tess, and every reason in the world to want her quiet."

"Are you done?"

"I think so."

"Warren Gilley walked into my command post at half past nine on Sunday night," Tess said. "Dale's back office, an hour after I left your poker game. Nobody asked him. Nobody had any idea. He sat down across the desk from me in his bow tie and told me he'd taken a poster out of the prop room Friday, and listed it Saturday morning, and taken the listing down Sunday because he was ashamed of himself. He told me about the three in September. He told me about Carol. He told me what Lorelei Crane said to him at the table, and why he went white. He did all of that before I'd asked him a single question, because he thought it was the right thing to do." She hadn't raised her voice once. "I've known since Sunday night. You've known for about ten hours, and you used it for a card trick."

"I didn't—" He stopped. He had.

"The rest of it is your theory," Tess said. "It isn't ours. You've got her dying off that trumpet. The lieutenant's got her dying off something she swallowed, and until a lab tells me different, so have I. Warren was never within reach of anything that woman swallowed. Ivy poured every drop she drank except your tea, and Warren didn't touch your tea." She looked at him. "As for his stomach, Dale watched him go. Dale says he was green to the ears. Dale stood at that stair door until he came back up at three minutes to eight, still green, and he sat down and didn't get up again until the lights went out. You were standing next to Dale when he went. You were talking about a riverboat."

*Nerves*, the chief had said, kindly, watching Warren go by with a hand on his stomach.

Archie looked at the granite. THOMAS MAYO, at the bottom, the letters still pale inside the cuts.

"And the posters were Gus's," said Tess. "He lent them to the opera house in 1985. There's a letter in the town office files that says so, on his stationery. They were never the town's to sell and they were never Warren's. They belong to Gus's estate." She paused. "That's your mother. I called her last night and told her. It's her decision whether there's a complaint."

"What did she say?"

"She asked if Warren was eating."

He laughed, and it sounded terrible, and he stopped.

"And Carol Gilley," said Tess, "is two doors down the hall from my father."

He turned and looked at her.

She stood with her hands in the pockets of the charcoal coat and her face turned partly to the water. The fog had laid a fine wet shine over her braid. She didn't look at him.

"She sits with him at lunch. She thinks he's her brother Paul. He lets her. On Tuesdays Warren brings her flowers, and he brings Dad a roll of Necco wafers, because Dad likes the brown ones and nobody else on earth does." Her voice had gone Maine-flat, every corner sanded off. "I've known Warren Gilley my whole life. He wrote the policy on our house. He's a pompous old man with a bad haircut, and he never did a dishonest thing in his life until this year. And you stood up in the Galley in front of forty people and called him a murderer with a magic trick."

"I didn't call him—"

"You didn't have to. You built it so you wouldn't have to." She looked at him then. "I know how a trick works, Archie. I've watched yours."

There was nothing to say to that. He said something anyway.

"All right. Not Warren. I was wrong about Warren. I'll be wrong about Warren in writing if you like, in the *Packet*, a full page." He took a breath. "Kev Duchesne."

Tess closed her eyes.

"Eleven thousand subscribers on Friday. Six hundred thousand this morning. I looked. He's got a mattress sponsor. Who wins the contest to sit in the most-watched séance in America? A nobody from Haverhill with a cartoon of himself on his chest. Nobody wins those contests, Tess. They're rigged. I've rigged them. So what if he's not a nobody? What if he's a plant — a rival network, one of the streamers—"

"Kev Duchesne," said Tess, opening her eyes, "sweated through his sweatshirt in my interview on Sunday. He asked me if I could get him your autograph. He cried when I asked for his home address." She took a breath. "He's lying to me about something. I don't know what yet. He's not a spy."

She was still angry, and he couldn't bear her being angry. He stepped in close and lifted his right hand to make a point about rival networks, and his left went to the slash pocket of her coat, where the black edge of a notebook had been showing all along.

His fingers closed on it. Her hand closed on his wrist.

She didn't grab. She simply had it, gloved fingers around the bones of his wrist with his hand still in her pocket, and she held it there. He could feel the steel of her watch against the back of his hand. Neither of them moved. Out in the white, the bell buoy rang.

"Give it back," she said.

He took his hand out of her pocket empty. He hadn't even gotten the notebook clear.

"That's twice," said Tess, and let go. "The pen. And this."

"You're keeping count."

"I'm a cop. I keep everything." She moved the notebook to an inside pocket and buttoned her coat over it. Then she looked at him, gray-eyed, with the fog behind her, and seemed to come to a decision she didn't care for.

"I'm going to tell you one thing," she said, "so you'll go home and stop tearing my town apart. I asked the crime lab to do the trumpet first. Your trumpet. The lieutenant signed off on it last night. It goes ahead of everything else they've got." She turned up her collar. "That's all you get. Go home, Archie."

She went back up the wharf toward the gray Interceptor parked exactly between the lines in the little lot by the co-op. She didn't look back.

*He's lying to me about something.*

"Sergeant," he called.

She stopped halfway up the wharf and half turned.

"That man wears his own face on his sweatshirt," Archie said. "He's never kept a secret from an audience in his life."

"He's my witness, Archie."

He went the other way, up the far side of the wharf toward Main Street, fast, with his hands in his pockets and his breath going on ahead of him.


---

# Chapter 21

## Spectral Kev

By ten o'clock the fog had burned off, and Main Street was all hard bright light and long blue shadows. Officer Bissonnette had taken the yellow tape off the town hall's side door at nine, and Archie knew this because Kev Duchesne had narrated the whole thing, live, to six hundred thousand people.

Kev was on the town hall steps in a black beanie, with the SPECTRAL KEV hoodie under an unzipped puffer jacket and his phone on a little motorized handle that swiveled to keep his face in the middle of the screen.

"—so the State Police have *released the scene*, guys, which means technically, *technically*, it's just a building again, and I'm going to see if anybody will let me—" He saw Archie coming up the sidewalk. His face went through delight, terror and delight again. "Oh my God. Guys. Guys, it's—"

Archie put one finger over the lens.

"Hi, Kev."

"I'm live," whispered Kev.

"Not anymore."

Kev turned it off. Up close he hadn't slept since Saturday. He smelled of energy drinks.

"I want to go in," he said. "It's the Great Fairweather's *theater*. It's the site. Do you know how many people want to see that room right now? Six hundred and four thousand." He said the number like the name of a saint. "On Friday I had eleven thousand. I got a check, Mr. Fairweather. Nobody's ever sent me a check. A mattress company wants me to say their name at the top of every video. I don't even like the mattress."

"I have keys," said Archie.

Kev stared at him.

"One hour. In the dark, the way it was. Just you and me. No camera."

"Audio?"

"No."

"Okay. Okay." Kev was nodding hard. "What do you want?"

"Whatever you're not telling Sergeant Calloway."

Kev swallowed.

---

Marchand Media's black drapes still hung over every tall window in the hall, and with the doors shut it was as dark as it had been on Saturday night. Darker; there was no red lamp. By flashlight, Kev unpacked on the stage boards: two EMF meters, a spirit box, a thermometer on a lanyard, a roll of glow-in-the-dark tape and a gallon freezer bag of batteries. The round oak table was still there, its seven chairs pushed back where people had left them. The props were in evidence bags in Augusta.

Kev switched on the orange EMF meter — the one Archie had signed — and walked toward the stage-left wall, holding it out in front of him like a dowsing rod.

It shrieked. Every light went red.

"Oh my God," Kev breathed.

"That's the new wiring," said Archie. "Eleven thousand dollars of it, from a man in Thomaston. I can show you the invoice."

Kev moved the meter over the table. It said nothing.

"Huh," said Kev, sounding betrayed.

"Sit. Your own chair. And lend me that." Archie nodded at the selfie handle, and Kev handed it over without a thought. Archie pulled out the chair across from the head of the table and sat, and switched off his flashlight, and after a moment Kev did the same, and there was nothing.

Then, from somewhere inside the table, three sharp knocks.

Kev made a small sound.

"Toe," said Archie in the dark. "Against the table leg. Again?" Three more. "You can't place it, can you? In the dark the room tells you where a sound came from, and the room lies."

"Okay," said Kev, faintly. "Okay, but—"

Something cold and wet and soft touched the back of his neck.

Kev screamed. It was a high, pure, operatic note. It went up into the dark of the pressed-tin ceiling and came back off the balcony, and somewhere in the middle of it Archie distinctly heard the word *Amos*.

He switched on his flashlight and pointed it at himself. He was holding Kev's selfie handle, fully extended, and clipped where the phone should have been was Archie's own glove, dunked in Kev's water bottle.

Kev looked at the glove for a long time.

"That's the cold touch," he said. "That's — Warren yelped. Your *mom* laughed. That was a *stick*?"

"A rod. Hers was nicer — black, telescoping, it'd go up a sweater sleeve." Archie unclipped the glove.

He took the glow tape and a rolled-up program from the box of old ones in the wing, and wound three bands of tape around the paper cone where Kev could watch him do it. Then he switched off the light. The three pale green rings hung in the black. They rose. They tilted, and drifted, and swam up over the table, up and to the left, as they had on Saturday night, and stopped high over Kev's head and turned toward him.

"*Kevin*," said the paper horn, in a slow, deep, faintly Welsh voice.

"Please stop," said Kev.

Archie stopped. He brought the program down and switched the flashlight back on. Kev was sitting in his chair with both hands over his face.

"I did forty minutes on the Crane Static," he said through his fingers. "Forty minutes. With graphics. A guy in Brazil did subtitles. I told six hundred thousand people Brother Amos was in the room with me." He took his hands away. "I *felt* him."

"You felt my glove."

"Before that. Saturday. I felt *something*." He looked at the empty table. "Didn't I?"

Archie let that one go by.

"Kev. Who's paying you?"

"Nobody! The platform! The mattress!"

"And the prop room?"

Kev's mouth opened and nothing came out of it.

Then it came out all at once. Friday night, half past nine, Marchand's crew still up here rigging the drapes and the street door propped open. He'd carried a coil of cable off the van and up the stairs, "because nobody stops a guy carrying cable, that's like a rule." The prop-room door wasn't locked. He'd just turned the handle. "I swear. I don't even know how to pick anything." The trumpet had been on a middle shelf in an open case lined with red velvet.

"I just wanted to film it. Ninety seconds. I put on gloves first! Nitrile! I'm not an *animal*. I held it up to the light like you did, I looked down it." His voice dropped. "And I took one selfie. With it. Not touching my face. Near my face."

"And after Saturday?"

"I deleted the selfie." Kev swallowed. "Then I deleted it out of Recently Deleted. Then I googled *can the police recover deleted photos*. Then I deleted my search history. Then I googled *can the police recover search history*." He looked up, stricken. "Then I deleted that."

Archie didn't laugh. It cost him something.

"Kev. At 7:35 on Saturday night I blew a raspberry through that horn on two cameras. Whatever you did to it on Friday, I'm sitting here."

"Oh," said Kev. "Oh. You're the — you're like the control sample."

"I'm exactly the control sample."

Kev put his head down on his knees for a moment. When he came up he looked five years younger.

"I just wanted to see it," he said. "It's *the* horn. All eight seasons, and the church halls before that — there are photos from a Grange in Vermont with the same dent. Except for the Trumpet Hiatus."

"The what?"

"Oh-three or oh-four, depending who you ask, to oh-six. Sister Lori stops using the horn in private sittings. Then it's back like nothing happened." Kev waved a hand. "There's a forty-page thread. Half of it's people fighting about when it starts. Most people think Brother Amos told her to rest it. One guy in Ohio thinks it was her rotator cuff."

"There's a *thread*."

"There are *eras*, Mr. Fairweather. The Church Hall Era. The Hiatus. The Cable Years."

"Do I have eras?"

Kev looked at him with pity. "Pre-Fenway and Post-Fenway," he said. "Obviously."

Archie decided he could live with that.

"Is it true about Gus's trapdoor?" Kev asked.

It was a few feet downstage of the table, two leaves cut into the boards with a flush iron ring at one edge. Archie should have said no. He knew it as he got two fingers into the ring and pulled, and cold air came up out of the black opening smelling of earth and mice and a hundred years.

He shrugged out of his coat — it would be filthy down there — dropped it over the back of Lorelei's chair, and went down the iron ladder first in his velvet jacket. The crawlspace ran the length of the stage, three and a half feet high, on brick piers, with a packed-dirt floor. Old programs and a rusted paint can lay in the dust.

"It's eleven degrees colder down here," Kev reported.

"It's a cellar, Kev."

They sat with their backs against a pier, knees up. Overhead the old building creaked in the wind off the harbor.

"Just tell me one thing," Kev said, quietly, to the dirt between his sneakers. "You've been in more dark rooms than anybody alive. I had your Challenge poster over my bed all through high school. *Nobody fools Archie Fairweather.* There must have been one time. One. Something you couldn't explain. You don't have to tell me what. Just tell me there was one."

Archie looked at the beam of the flashlight lying across the dust.

He thought of a church basement in Somerville when he was nineteen. A woman in a hot tub in Reno. Four hundred rooms, give or take, and the same hands in all of them, and him in every one with his eyes wide open, wanting.

"I've been looking since I was seven years old," he said. "I've never found one, Kev. Not one."

"Then why do you—"

"I haven't stopped looking."

Kev didn't say anything. After a while he nodded.

A shaft of white light came down through the open trap and swept the crawlspace and found them, two grown men sitting in the dirt under a stage with their knees up around their ears.

"The clerk downstairs," said Tess Calloway's voice from above, "says there's been somebody screaming up here for twenty minutes."

She was crouched at the edge of the trap. She took them both in: the dirt, the thermometer, the wet glove sticking out of Archie's pocket.

"Mr. Duchesne," she said. "Mr. Fairweather."

Kev looked up into the light, and then at Archie. Then he got his phone out with a hand that wasn't steady and scrambled on his knees to the foot of the ladder.

"I have to show you something," he said. "Both of you. I should have on Sunday. I'm sorry."

Tess came down two rungs. Archie crowded in beside Kev.

"I had another camera," Kev said. "In my hoodie pocket. Infrared, thirty-nine dollars, I cut a hole for the lens. The contract said no devices, and then she — and the lawyers—" He gave up and pressed play. "I started it when she said hands, everybody. It never got the static. Not once."

The picture was gray-green and grainy, shot low across the table: the edge of the tabletop, a candle stub, and lying on its side in the middle, the trumpet, its three bands glowing hard white in the infrared. The clock in the corner said 19:59. The house lights went, and the picture didn't change. Tinny voices came out of the phone — the prayer, Brother Amos, three raps. Kev dragged his thumb along the bottom of the screen. 20:06. 20:10. A peal of laughter that was Vivienne's. The horn lay where it was. Nothing moved near it.

At 20:17:04 a hand came into the top of the frame.

It was a woman's hand, and there were rings on three of its fingers, big stones that blazed pale in the infrared. It closed around the narrow end of the trumpet and lifted it out of the picture, and the bands went up and away to the left, and were gone.

Nobody said anything. Down in the dirt, very small, the phone said, *Shan.*

Kev stopped it.

"How long," said Tess, "have you had this?"

"Since Saturday."

"On Sunday you sat across a table from me and said you hadn't recorded anything."

"I said I hadn't recorded anything on my *phone*. Which was technically—" Kev saw her face and closed his mouth.

Tess looked at the frozen screen a while longer. Then she climbed back up.

"Upstairs," she said. "Both of you. Now."

Kev climbed. Halfway up he turned and looked down at Archie.

"They're going to arrest me," he whispered.

"You?" Archie put a hand on the bottom rung. "You're getting a thank-you, Kev. The arrest is for somebody else."


---

# Chapter 22

## Evidence

The select board room had been freezing on Monday. On Tuesday afternoon it was freezing everywhere except one corner, where the space heater Archie had ordered from Portland sat under Tess's end of the long table, ticking and glowing orange, with Tess's boots as close to it as the law allowed.

It was ten past two. Lieutenant Neil Brannock had driven down from Augusta at noon, and in the forty minutes since he'd walked in he had watched Kev Duchesne's footage seven times on Tess's laptop, said about a dozen words, and drunk most of an extra-large Dunkin' coffee. Kev was next door with a trooper, giving a formal statement that would include Friday night in the prop room. His little camera had been bagged at eleven, and he had folded the receipt into a very small square. Archie sat at the long table because nobody had told him where to sit and nobody had told him to leave. On the wall above him hung framed photographs of every chair of the Merrow select board since 1899. The one nearest the door was a stern man in a bow tie who could only have been Warren's grandfather. Archie had been not looking at it for half an hour.

Brannock took off his reading glasses and looked at him.

"The best piece of evidence in this case," he said, "spent seventy-two hours on a YouTuber's laptop, and then it reached me out of a crawlspace by way of my person of interest. It goes to the lab tonight for verification."

"Nobody touched it."

"Then the lab will tell me so." He put the glasses back on.

His phone buzzed on the table. He looked at the screen and picked it up. "Brannock." He listened. "Say again." He listened some more, and took a pen out of his shirt pocket and wrote something on the legal pad in small square capitals and underlined it. "Inside. Not the outer surface. Okay. I want it in writing in an hour." He put the phone down and sat for a moment looking at what he'd written.

Then he turned the pad so that Tess could read it, and after a second, so that Archie could too.

ACONITINE — POS — INSIDE SMALL END (SEAM).

"The lab," Brannock said. "Inside the mouthpiece. In the seam where the metal's rolled over. The outside had been wiped. The inside wasn't, or not well enough." He pressed the bridge of his nose between finger and thumb. "So. You were right about the horn, Mr. Fairweather."

"Holy Houdini," said Archie.

He was on his feet. He'd been right. It wasn't the tea and it wasn't Bev's thermos and it wasn't the man who'd poured it; it was the horn, it had always been the horn. He wanted to hug somebody. He looked at Tess, who took her boots off the heater and moved her chair six inches back, and he looked at Brannock, and decided against it.

"Sit down, Mr. Fairweather," said Brannock.

Archie sat. He was still grinning.

Brannock let him grin. He finished his coffee, set the empty cup down precisely on the corner of the legal pad, and folded his hands.

"Here's what your fan's footage does for me," he said. "Two things. It shows the victim's left hand free in the dark at 8:17, which means the daughter lied when she said she held it. You'd told us as much. Now I can show it to a jury." He turned the laptop so that the frozen frame faced Archie: the horn on its side on the table, three white bands. "And it shows that horn lying there in the dark, untouched, from the moment the lights went out until the moment the victim picked it up. Seventeen minutes. Nobody reached for it. Which means whatever was in that mouthpiece was in there before the lights went out."

Archie stopped grinning.

"Saturday night, at the hospital in Rockport," said Brannock, "you told Sergeant Calloway that when the lights came back up you saw Ivy Crane pick up the trumpet and wipe the small end with her scarf. Twice. Then put the scarf in her coat pocket." He waited. "Is that still your statement?"

Archie saw it then, all at once, as you see a trick from the wrong side of the table when the volunteer's back is turned and the whole method is lying in your palm.

The daughter who poured everything her mother drank. Who sat at her left in the dark and said twice, on tape, that she'd held her hand. Who had picked up the horn in the full glare of the house lights while her mother was dying on the boards, and wiped it, and wouldn't give up the scarf.

He'd told Tess it was a reset. He still believed it. He had done it himself ten thousand times.

It didn't matter what he believed. It mattered what he'd seen.

He looked at Tess. She looked back at him with no help in her face, which was right. Under the table the heater ticked.

He could say he'd been on the floor with a dying woman's head in his arm. He could say it had happened very fast, and he couldn't swear to it now. He was a wonderful liar. He had lied to stadiums. He had lied to Lorelei Crane on the floor of his own stage, and she had smiled at him.

"Yes," he said. "That's what I saw."

Brannock nodded once and wrote it down.

"And I want something put in with it," Archie said. "That a performer does that. After a show you wipe the props. It's how you protect the method. She's been doing it since she was twelve years old. It's called a reset."

"Put it in," said Brannock. "A jury likes a reason." He drew a folder from under the legal pad. "The remote you pulled out of the umbrella stand kills the infrared feed on both of Marchand's cameras. The prints on it that aren't yours are Ivy Crane's. We took the scarf from her room last night, on a warrant. It's at the lab." He closed the folder. "And she inherits."

Archie said nothing. There was nothing left in him that would have helped.

"Sergeant," said Brannock, "get me an arrest warrant for Ivy Crane. Tampering with physical evidence, falsifying physical evidence. I'll call the AG's office and tell them what's coming."

Tess stood. For a fraction of a second she didn't move. Then she picked up her laptop and her coat.

"Yes, sir," she said, and went out, and didn't look at Archie on the way.

"Trooper Lavoie is going to take your formal statement," said Brannock. "On tape, from the beginning. You're here voluntarily and you can leave whenever you want." He stood and picked up his empty cup. "I'd rather you didn't."

"I'm not going anywhere."

Trooper Lavoie was a large, mild young man with a head cold, and he took Archie from the straitjacket on Thursday morning to the waiting room on Saturday night, and then through the last two minutes of the séance four different ways. Archie answered the same way four times. He talked too much, as he always did when he was frightened, and Lavoie let him and blew his nose and wrote things down. The tall windows went from white to gold to a deep cold blue. Someone brought the lieutenant another coffee, and nobody brought anything for Archie.

At 5:26 Lavoie turned off the recorder. At 5:30 Archie came out through the town hall's front doors onto the granite steps.

The sun was behind the hill. Main Street was going dark from the bottom up, and the streetlights were coming on one at a time along the sidewalk with a faint buzz. His coat was upstairs on the back of a dead woman's chair, and he couldn't face the stairs to get it.

A State Police cruiser came out of the lot beside the town hall, without lights, without hurry. Tess's gray Interceptor came after it. At the bottom of the hill, where Main Street met the harbor, both of them slowed, and turned left.

Left was Water Street. On Water Street was the Spindrift Inn.


---

# Chapter 23

## The Wrong Woman

At five thirty-one he was running.

The hearse was in the lot behind the town hall, but it took two tries to start on a cold night and woke every dog it passed, and the Spindrift was at the bottom of the hill. Main Street went down to the harbor in one long slope, and he went down it in his velvet jacket with no coat, past the hardware store and the dark ice-cream stand with its sign that said SEE YOU IN MAY, his breath going out ahead of him in clouds. He was forty-seven and he had legs like a heron's, which were wonderful for stepping over things and no use at all for this. Below him the harbor was black, with the riding lights of the moorings strung across it.

He wanted Ivy to hear from him what he'd told the trooper. It wasn't much. It was what he had.

The Spindrift Inn stood at the corner with every window lit. The front door was open, letting the heat out. Both cars sat in the curved drive with their engines running, and on the porch steps, in a Merrow PD jacket with her thumbs hooked in her belt, stood Officer Kayla Bissonnette.

"Mr. Fairweather." She came down one step. "You can't go in."

"I'm not going in." He was breathing like a bellows. "I'm going to stand here."

"Then stand there."

He stood there. Kayla was twenty-four and had been told to hold a door by someone she admired, and she was holding it. Past her, in the front hall, Pru Haskell stood in the parlor doorway with a dish towel in both hands, and two troopers came down the stairs carrying cardboard cartons and a laptop in a plastic bag.

Then Tess came down the hall from the back of the house with Ivy.

Ivy's hands were behind her. She was in the big gray sweater and canvas sneakers with no socks, and Tess had a hand on her elbow, not gripping it, and was speaking in a low, even voice — words Archie had heard on television his whole life and never once heard a person he knew say to another. Ivy nodded at each sentence. Without the scarf her neck looked thin and much younger.

They came down the hall. Kayla stepped aside for them at the door, and that left Archie at the foot of the steps in the porch light with nothing between him and them but the cold.

"Ivy," he said. "I told them why. I told them it was a reset—"

"Mr. Fairweather." Tess didn't raise her voice. "Step back."

He stepped back one step. "—that every performer in the world—"

"Further."

He went further. Ivy stopped at the top of the steps, and Tess let her stop. For the first time since the day they'd met, Ivy Crane wasn't looking at his hands. She was looking at his face.

"You told them about the scarf," she said.

"I told them what I saw."

"You told them the truth." She said it flatly, a fact she was filing. "Mom always said that about you people. You tell the truth, and then you stand there waiting to be thanked."

Tess moved her on. Down the steps, across the gravel, to the back door of the gray Interceptor, which Kayla had gone ahead to open.

"Sorry — sorry, Archie, one sec." Teddy Marchand had come out the side door by the parking lot with a phone at his ear and a mango-smelling cloud of vapor around his head, and he gave Archie the little chin-lift that men in fleece vests give each other at airports.
