Into the phone he said, "No, I'm here. I'm literally watching it. It's awful. It's genuinely awful, nobody's saying it isn't." He listened. "Sure. But between us? The shot of her coming down those steps? The numbers are going to be insane."

Archie had never hit anyone in his life. He gave it some real thought.

He took out his phone instead.

Margaret Okafor practiced criminal defense on Exchange Street in Portland and was, by the unhappy agreement of every prosecutor in New England, the best there was. Twelve years ago, at her own written request, Archie had made her wedding cake disappear — six tiers, commissioned by her mother-in-law without consultation — in front of a hundred and forty guests. Margaret had cried, and told him, *Anything, Archie. Ever.* She had been on his speed dial since, between June and Lenny.

She picked up on the second ring. "Who's dead?"

"Nobody new." He said it loudly, for Teddy. "Margaret, a woman named Ivy Crane is about to be driven to the jail in Rockland. Tampering with evidence and falsifying it — that's what they're saying tonight. They'll say more tomorrow. She's twenty-nine and she hasn't got anybody. Her mother was Lorelei Crane."

"The medium."

"The medium. I want you to represent her. I'll pay. Whatever it costs. I don't want to know what it costs."

"You'll know. I'll send you a letter and you'll need to sit down." He heard a drawer, then keys. "Knox County, by nine. Don't speak to her. Don't speak to the police about her. Don't call me on anything but my office line. Is she going to want me?"

Archie looked across the gravel. Tess had her palm on top of Ivy's head, guiding it in under the roof of the car.

"No," he said.

"Fine," said Margaret Okafor. "They never do." She hung up.

Teddy had lowered his own phone. "Buddy. Are you paying for her lawyer? Because the optics of that—"

Archie walked past him.

Ivy was in the back seat with her hands behind her, sitting up straight, as she had in the blue plastic chair in Rockport. The door was still open. Tess was saying something to Kayla across the roof. Ivy turned her head and found him at the edge of the gravel.

"Don't help me," she said.

"Her name's Margaret," said Archie. "She'll be there at nine. Don't say a word until she comes."

Ivy looked at him a moment longer. Then Tess shut the door.

Tess came around the car and stopped beside him, close enough that he could see her face wasn't only cold.

"Go and get some sleep," she said.

"Is that an order, Sergeant?"

"It's advice. The orders start tomorrow." She got in behind the wheel.

Pru Haskell had come out onto the porch with the dish towel. "Can I get you something?" she said. "Coffee?"

"No. Thank you."

"I'll make it anyway," said Pru, and went back in.

The Interceptor pulled out of the drive, and the cruiser after it, and Archie stood alone at the edge of the gravel and watched the two pairs of taillights go down Water Street along the harbor — past the dark co-op, past the fuel dock, red and small and getting smaller — until they turned at the corner by the Galley and were gone.

Then he went back up the hill for the hearse.


---

# Chapter 24

## Family Meeting

At ten o'clock on Tuesday night the kitchen at Fairweather House smelled of soup that nobody had eaten, and Archie was drawing a table.

He'd sat in the lobby of the Knox County Jail for an hour, on a bench bolted to the wall, until a steel door buzzed at nine-forty and Margaret Okafor came through it in a camel coat. Her client, she said, was eating a sandwich. Her client did not wish to see him. His part in this was the paying part, and he could do that from home.

So he was home, at the scarred farm table, with a yellow legal pad. He had drawn a circle for the séance table and seven small circles around it, labeled in capitals — LORELEI at the top, then IVY, WARREN, MOTHER, ME, KEV, SHANNON — and a long skinny cone in the middle for the trumpet. A half-dollar walked across the knuckles of his left hand, over and under, and back. He didn't watch it.

Vivienne set a bowl of soup at his elbow. She was in a green kaftan with a man's gray cardigan over it.

"Eat that."

"I will."

"You've said 'I will' to three bowls," said his mother, and sat down across from him. "I counted."

June was at the far end of the table with her laptop open and her chemistry book shut beside it, which was the wrong way around for a Tuesday. When he'd come in she had looked up and said "Dad," in the voice that meant *I saw it on the news*, and he'd said, "I know," and that had been the whole conversation so far.

He didn't hear Lenny come in. He looked up from the pad and Lenny was standing at his elbow, looking at the soup, and then at the drawing, and then he pulled out a chair and sat down at the table — which he did not do, in this kitchen or any other, unless he meant to stay. Archie felt it like a hand on his shoulder.

Lenny turned the legal pad around with one finger. He brought his reading glasses down from his forehead and studied the drawing.

"Who touched it," he said, "after you?"

Archie put the coin down.

It was the right question. He had spent four hours asking *why did she wipe it* and *what is she eating in there*, and Lenny, who in thirty years had never asked about anything but hardware, had asked about the hardware.

"I put it down dead center," Archie said. "Teddy called cut. Lorelei and Ivy went down to the green room, and I went to the back of the hall and talked riverboats with Dale." He went through the rest as flatly as he could. "Ivy came back up at some point and put the rod under the cloth — there was nothing under that table at seven-thirty but table. Quarter to eight, maybe. I didn't see it. Kev was over by the radiator. Mother was at her chair. Hollis slid the horn a few inches when Teddy called to her about the candle. That's what I saw, and I saw it from the back of the room."

Lenny put one blunt finger on the cone in the middle of the drawing.

"Then it's the girl," he said, "or it's nobody."

"It's not nobody." Archie pulled the pad back. "And it's not what anybody saw. The tripod cameras weren't recording — Teddy cut them, I heard him. *Cut controls, fifteen minutes.* On Monday Brannock showed me every frame Marchand Media had given him. Me with the horn at my mouth. Me pouring the tea. The same two angles, dead level, wide. Tripods. Then the dark, from Kev." He tapped the pad. "There was a third camera. On a man's shoulder. Drifting around that stage the whole time I was at the back — the candles, the window, the empty chairs. If Teddy had handed that over, Brannock would have been dying to show it to me. Me, from a new angle, near the horn. Teddy didn't give it to them."

"Who's the cameraman?" said June.

"The big fella. Black watch cap." Archie had stood beside the man on Saturday afternoon discussing the light off the harbor window. "His name is—"

"Marco," said Lenny.

"How do you know that?"

"He borrowed my gaffer tape. Thursday." Lenny pushed his glasses back up onto his forehead. "He brought it back." From Lenny, that was a recommendation for a medal. "You never look at the crew."

"I look at the crew."

"Thirty years you've had crews." Lenny folded his arms. "Who ran your flies in Las Vegas? Nine years. Every show."

Archie opened his mouth. A face arrived: young, sunburned, a goatee, a Dodgers cap. Nothing arrived with it.

"Dwayne," he said.

"Luis."

"Luis. Of course. Luis."

June's laptop was already turned toward him. "Camera operators all have reels online," she said. "If he's Marco and he works for Marchand, he'll be on a crew list with his last name, and then he'll have an Instagram, and it'll say where he is. Give me twenty minutes."

"No."

"Dad, I found the posters in about an hour—"

"I know you did." He heard how it came out, and tried again, gentler, and it came out the same. "June. There's a woman in a cell in Rockland tonight because of something I said. I'm not putting your name anywhere near this. Not this one."

"It's not your name. It's mine." She said it evenly. "I get to decide where it goes."

"Not tonight."

June looked at him down the length of the table, and he watched her decide not to argue. He'd seen her do it at a debate podium, when she'd judged a round was lost and her time was better spent elsewhere. She closed the laptop.

"Okay," she said.

She picked up her chemistry book and set the laptop on top of it and went out and up the back stairs. He heard her door close. June had never slammed a door in her life. She closed them precisely, all the way, until the latch clicked.

Vivienne didn't move. She sat with her hands folded on the table and looked at him.

"Archibald."

"Don't."

"That child is not a volunteer from the audience," his mother said. "You don't get to call her up onstage when it suits the act and then thank her very much and send her back to her seat." She stood, took the cold soup, and poured it back into the pot. "Stop. For one night. Sit in this kitchen with the people who live in it. It will all still be wrong in the morning."

"I can't, Mother."

"I know you can't," said Vivienne. "That's why I asked."

---

The turret was cold, and he didn't turn on the lamp. The light from the landing was enough to show him the trunk under the north window, with its faded gold stars.

He sat down on the floor in front of it. He hadn't been up here in three nights.

*The trunk, Sparrow.*

Seven letters. He had known since Saturday night, in the dark, with the green bands floating over the table.

He put his fingers on the first dial and turned it to S.

The brass was cold. The dial clicked softly into place. He turned the second to P, and the third to A, and his fingers went to the fourth.

He knew where Lorelei had found the word. He'd seen it on Sunday, in Ivy's binder, behind the tab with his name on it: a photocopied page of the *Merrow Packet* from November 1988, with one line highlighted in yellow. *His grandson, Archie, whom he called his "little sparrow."* A library, a laptop, a newspaper nobody had read in thirty-seven years — and then a tin horn, and his mother gone rigid in the dark, on camera, for money.

He took his fingers off the dial.

He was not going to let a dead woman's research open Gus's trunk. He spun the three dials back to nonsense and sat with his hands on his knees.

When he looked up, Vivienne was in the doorway with a fresh bowl of soup, and she had been there long enough. She didn't say anything about the dials. She came in and set the bowl on the rolltop desk, steaming, and touched his hair on her way past, and went back down the stairs.

His phone buzzed.

*Teddy Marchand posted a picture of his boarding pass an hour ago,* June had written. *Cape Air, Owls Head to Boston, 9:05 tomorrow morning. I'm not volunteering. I'm just telling you.*


---

# Chapter 25

## Owls Head

Knox County Regional Airport was the smallest airport Archie had ever loved on sight.

It sat on a flat point of land in Owls Head, south of Rockland, with the bay on two sides and a windsock standing straight out over the runway. The terminal was one story of brown clapboard with a flagpole and a bench. Inside, a woman in a fleece weighed bags on a scale that might have come from a feed store, one officer staffed a security lane consisting of a folding table, and a vending machine had run out of everything but peanut-butter crackers. Out on the apron, a white twin-engine plane about the size of a pickup truck with ambitions sat in the gray morning while a boy in ear protectors fueled it. Nine seats. Archie had counted the windows.

It was ten minutes to eight on Wednesday. The fog was coming off the water in rags, and the sky was low and white, the kind that meant rain by noon. He'd parked the hearse across two spaces in the short-term lot, where a family from Connecticut was now photographing it, and come in with nothing in his pockets but a deck of cards, a pen, and the watch.

He stood at the glass and wound the watch. It had been Gus's. It lost four minutes a day no matter what anyone did to it, and he loved it more than anything he owned that wasn't alive. Twelve turns. In the glass he could see Teddy Marchand at the end of the second row of chairs, with a roller bag between his white sneakers, a phone in each hand, and an unlit vape between two fingers that he kept raising to his mouth and lowering again with a look of real grief.

Teddy saw him in the glass.

"Archie!" He was on his feet before Archie had turned around. "Oh, wow. I was literally about to call you. This is — honestly, this is a sign. Sit. Sit, sit." He patted the chair beside his.

Archie sat.

"Documentary," said Teddy. "Ninety minutes. Prestige. Not the show — the show's done, God rest her. The *real* story. Working title, *Crane*. The medium and the magician. The bet, the respect, the night in the dark. You held her while she—" He touched his chest with a phone. "I've got a call with a streamer at two. If I can tell them you're in, it's not a pitch anymore. It's a deal."

"I'm in," said Archie.

Teddy's mouth stayed open.

"An exclusive," said Archie. "My first sit-down since Las Vegas. You can even ask me about the Glass Box. I won't answer, but you can ask." He smiled. "In exchange for Marco's card."

Teddy's face went still for half a second, and then went pleasant. Archie had watched four hundred mediums do that in the half second after the right question.

"What card?"

"The handheld. Saturday night. Marco shot the table for the whole quarter of an hour before the séance. I watched him do it."

"Exteriors." Teddy shook his head, sorry for him. "Harbor, lighthouse, the drive in on Route 1, the town. I told the lieutenant. That card's in Los Angeles, Archie. It went out with the drives on Monday."

"I was there, Teddy. He shot the candles."

"He shot the candles and then he went outside and shot the harbor."

"You told a lieutenant of the Maine State Police, in a homicide, that a piece of evidence was in California." Archie said it pleasantly. "I don't know what they call that up here. I'd bet Lieutenant Brannock knows. I'd bet he can spell it."

"Are you threatening me?"

"I'm describing your weekend."

Teddy looked out at the plane. The boy in the ear protectors was coiling the fuel hose. "Get a lawyer to tell you what you'd be describing," he said. "I've got three."

"Friday night," said Archie, "I was walking out the back of the Spindrift past a half-open door, and I heard you on the phone. The key-person policy. Runs through the end of the season. That's the point."

For a second Teddy looked genuinely lost. Then he laughed, short and not happily.

"You think I — oh, Archie. Archie, Archie." He leaned back in the chair. "Okay. Here's the policy. It's on the company's books, and every dollar it pays goes straight back to the network, for the episodes I now can't deliver. It makes *them* whole. It makes me nothing. You know what her dying got me?" He counted on the edge of his phone. "A twelve-city live tour in March — arenas — canceled. A spinoff with a dead star. Seasons nine and ten in a drawer, waiting on a signature I'm never getting. I'm not a suspect, buddy. I'm a casualty."

"She was quitting," said Archie.

"She quit every August. I talked her back every September. Eight years." Teddy shrugged. "It's the job. I talk people out of things."

"Then why lie about the card?"

Teddy was quiet. He turned the unlit vape over in his fingers.

"Because I've watched it," he said. "Sunday morning, in my room, with the door locked. At seven forty-four the daughter comes back up the stairs on her own and slides a stick under the tablecloth. A telescoping rod, with a glove on the end. So she can touch Warren Gilley on the ear in the dark." He looked at Archie. "You hand that to the police, it's evidence. It's in a courtroom. It's on the news, frame by frame, with a little red circle around her hands. Eight seasons, a hundred and four episodes, eleven international territories, and the whole library turns into a girl with a stick. That's not a show anymore. That's a class action."

"And Ivy sits in a cell."

"Ivy wiped the thing with her scarf in front of you. That's not on me."

They sat. A man in a Patriots jacket came in out of the cold and asked the woman at the counter if the Boston flight was going, and she said it was, and he went and sat by the vending machine.

"I found her, you know," said Teddy. He wasn't looking at Archie. He was looking at the little plane. "Worcester. A Knights of Columbus hall. Folding chairs, a raffle table. She did forty minutes for maybe sixty people, and then she sat with every one of them after, and she remembered all their names. I'd driven out from Boston to laugh at her." He turned the vape over again. "Eleven years. I liked her, okay? That's the stupid part. I really liked her."

Archie had liked her too, and he'd known her two days.

Teddy put the vape in one vest pocket and unzipped the other. He took out a little hard plastic case, the size of a matchbook, and opened it on his knee. Inside, on a bed of gray foam, sat a black memory card.

"Here's what happened," he said, in a flatter voice, one that had spent a lot of time on the phone with lawyers. "I was packing this morning, and I found this in Marco's kit. I'm a private citizen about to get on a plane, handing it to a witness. What you do with it is between you and the State of Maine." He closed the case. He didn't hand it over. "You never got it from me. You got it from Marco's kit."

"Fine."

"And I want something."

"The documentary. I said yes."

"There's no documentary. Not after this." Teddy held the case up between two fingers. "How'd you do the Glass Box?"

Archie had seen it coming since he'd made the joke himself, ten minutes earlier. You didn't mention the thing a man wanted most unless you were ready to be asked for it. He had seen it coming, and it went through him anyway.

The Glass Box was the last thing he'd done in Las Vegas: a clear case on a bare, bright stage, three thousand people watching him step into it, and then a clear case with nobody in it, and Archie on a plane to Boston to sit in the third row of his daughter's debate final. Four people on earth knew how it worked — Lenny, who had built it; two stagehands who had signed papers thicker than the box; and Archie. That night in Boston, an hour before he promised her a real year, June had looked up from her room-service fries and asked how he'd gotten out, and he'd said, *Magic, Junebug,* and thought it was charming.

"I know what I'm asking," said Teddy. "I'm not stupid. I've watched that tape three hundred times. My kid's got a theory with mirrors."

"It's not mirrors."

"Don't tell me what it isn't. Tell me what it is."

Archie looked at the case between Teddy's fingers. He thought about Ivy in the back seat of the Interceptor with her hands behind her, sitting up straight. He thought about her bare throat.

He got up and went to the coffee counter by the security lane and came back with a paper napkin. He clicked his pen and wrote on it in capitals, pressing hard so it wouldn't tear:

*I, THEODORE MARCHAND, WILL NEVER TELL, SELL, FILM, SKETCH, IMPLY, HINT AT OR HUM THE METHOD OF THE GLASS BOX TO ANY LIVING PERSON, IN ANY MEDIUM NOW KNOWN OR HEREAFTER DEVISED.*

"'Hereafter devised,'" said Teddy. "Who's your lawyer?"

"The best one in New England. I'm paying her a great deal of money as of last night." He held out the pen. "Sign it."

Teddy signed it. Archie signed under him, folded the napkin twice, and put it in his breast pocket. Then he leaned in close, so that the man in the Patriots jacket couldn't have heard if he'd tried, and told Teddy Marchand how the Glass Box worked.

It took about ninety seconds.

He watched Teddy's face while he talked. He'd seen it before, on the very few people he'd ever told anything: first the hunger, then the attention, the eyes narrowing, following it, keeping up — and then, right at the end, when the last piece went in, something that fell. Every method ever invented was smaller than its trick. It had never once stopped hurting to watch.

"That's it?" said Teddy.

"That's it."

"Huh." Teddy sat back. He looked out at the plane, and at Archie, and at the plane again. "Huh."

He held out the little case. Archie took it.

Over the speaker, the woman in the fleece announced that Boston was boarding. Teddy gathered his phones and his roller bag and went through security at the folding table. At the door onto the apron he turned around, and for a second Archie thought he might say something else.

"Huh," said Teddy, and went out across the wet tarmac to the plane.

Archie stood at the glass and watched the little white plane run, lift off over the bay, and go up into the low cloud.

The card was in his breast pocket, behind the napkin. He could feel the corner of the case through his shirt. Lieutenant Brannock was going to kill him.

Archie walked back out to the hearse past the family from Connecticut, who asked if they could get one with him in it, and he said yes, and stood by the gold lettering and smiled for them while the first rain came in off the bay.


---

# Chapter 26

## Forty Seconds

At eleven on Wednesday morning the opera house was still dressed for a funeral.

Marchand Media's blackout drapes hung over every tall window; Hollis had volunteers coming at four to take them down. The police had released the hall on Tuesday morning and taken the props to the lab. What was left stood center stage under two work lights on stands: the round oak table and its seven chairs, where the crew had left them, with torn corners of evidence tape still stuck to the boards around the legs. His buffalo-check coat hung over the back of Lorelei's chair, where he'd dropped it yesterday. He put it on. Rain ticked on the windows behind the drapes.

Lenny had brought a laptop up from the workshop, and a card reader, and fifty feet of orange extension cord, which he'd run from the prop room in the firm belief that electricity was out to get him. He set the laptop on the table in front of Lorelei's chair and held out his hand.

Archie put the little plastic case in it.

Lenny opened it, looked at the card, and didn't plug it in.

"What did it cost?"

There was no point lying to Lenny. "The Glass Box," Archie said.

Lenny brought his reading glasses down off his forehead, looked at him through them, and pushed them back up.

"You told him."

"On a napkin. He signed an agreement."

"He's a television producer."

"It was a very good napkin, Lenny."

"Eleven months," said Lenny. "In a garage. In Henderson. In August."

"I know."

"A hundred and nineteen degrees. The resin wouldn't set."

"I know, Lenny."

Lenny slid the card into the reader and plugged the reader into the laptop with more force than any connection has ever needed.

There were footsteps on the stairs. June came up into the hall at ten past eleven in her Doc Martens and a brown thrift-store cardigan, with her laptop bag on one shoulder and her face set. She should have been in third-period chemistry. She had let the bus go by at the end of Shore Road that morning. She stopped at the top of the center aisle among the stacked chairs, ready to be sent home.

Archie looked at her down the length of the hall. Then he went to the table and pulled out a chair — the one at the right of Lorelei's place — and stood behind it.

"That's Shannon's," he said. "You can be Shannon."

June came down the aisle and up the steps and sat in it. She took the pencil out of her bun and a legal pad out of her bag and laid them on the table, squared to the edge.

"Okay," she said. It wasn't last night's okay. He'd take it.

"What did you give Teddy?" she said. "For the card."

Lenny, at the laptop, didn't look up.

"The Glass Box," said Archie.

June wrote the date at the top of her legal pad and underlined it twice.

The footage came up.

It was a low angle from somewhere near the stage-left wing, handheld but steady, the picture warm and grainy under the work light, with a timecode running in the corner: 19:36:02. The first thing on the screen was Archie's own elbow.

He watched himself pour from Bev's thermos and hold a cup out across the table to a woman in black velvet, who laughed and moved her daughter's hand aside and took it. There was no sound for the toast; Marco had been too far away. Lorelei drank half and set the cup down. Archie watched her set it down, and for a second he couldn't watch anything else.

"Seven forty-one," said June, writing. "Cut."

On the screen Lorelei rose and went out of the frame toward the stage-right stairs, and Ivy rose and followed with the water bottle and the lozenges. The work light went down. The camera stayed on the table. On the audio, faint, came Teddy's voice from the wing: *Marco, stay on the table for me. Give me the table breathing. That's the cold open.*

The table breathed. Empty chairs; the bell, the tambourine, the slate; the trumpet in the center, its three pale bands catching what light there was.

At 19:43:50, Ivy Crane came back up the stairs alone.

She went straight to her own chair, the one at her mother's left. She didn't sit. She bent, half turned from the camera, and her hands went under the overhang of the tablecloth at her own place and slid something in — long and thin and black — and smoothed the cloth down over it. It took about ten seconds. Then she went back out of the frame the way she'd come.

The trumpet sat in the middle of the table the whole time. Her hands never came nearer to it than the table's edge.

"That's the rod," said Archie. "That's how Warren got kissed on the ear in the dark."

Lenny took a tape measure out of one of his sweaters and ran it from the edge of the real table, at Ivy's place, to the center.

"Twenty-three inches."

"Run it back," said June. Lenny ran it back. Ivy's hands went under the cloth and came out empty and went away. "She never reaches," June said, and wrote it down.

Something was rising in Archie that he didn't trust yet. He sat on it.

Seven forty-six: Kev Duchesne leaned into the frame with his phone held out in both hands, photographing the table like a man photographing a celebrity's lunch, and Teddy's voice came sharp off camera — *Phones away, please, sir* — and Kev jumped and backed off toward the radiators on the stage-left wall, where he stayed, a dark hoodie at the edge of the picture. Seven forty-seven: Warren Gilley crossed behind the chairs with one hand on his stomach and was gone. Seven forty-eight: Hollis with a long match, lighting the tall pillar candle in the middle of the table and then the small ones around the edge; then a tray, and a glass of water at every place. Vivienne came into the frame and fussed with a black shawl over the back of her chair until it hung exactly so. Shannon came up the steps and sat down at her place and put her hands in her lap.

"Does he ever point it somewhere else?" said June. "Ever?"

"Find out," said Archie.

She took the trackpad and went forward ten seconds at a time, writing down what was in the frame at each jump — *table, table, table* — all the way down the page. Lenny watched her with his arms folded. Archie watched the candles.

At 19:51:10 the picture went white.

It bloomed out from the center — the tall flame and its reflection in the polished curve of the trumpet right beside it, flaring together in the lens until the whole frame was a smear of gold — and the camera swung away fast, to the right and up, and settled on the one bare window at the stage-left end of the hall. Out in the dark harbor the riding lights of the moorings were coming on, one by one.

On the audio, Teddy: *Hollis — sorry — can you slide the horn left, it's fighting the candle.*

The harbor. The moorings. A little bob as Marco adjusted something. The harbor.

At 19:51:50 the camera swung back to the table.

The trumpet sat a few inches to the left of where it had been, out of the candle's way. Hollis was walking off toward the stage-right wing with her empty tray under her arm. Behind her a young man from the crew knelt to tape down a cable, and at the edge of the frame Marco's own hand came up and did something to the lens and went away again.

"Forty seconds," said June.

She ran it again. Then she went through the rest of the card to the end, and put her pencil down.

"That's the only time," she said. "From thirty-six to fifty-nine he never leaves the table, except for those forty seconds."

"So who's there?" said Archie. "In the forty seconds."

June read it off her list. "Mrs. Tibbetts, with the tray. Grandma, at her chair. Shannon, at her place. Kev, by the radiator." She looked up. "And the crew, going back and forth. Two of them at least."

Archie got up and went around the table to the chair beside his own, the one with the shawl in the footage, and stood behind it. He put his hand out over the tabletop. The center was right there. Anyone at that table could have touched it without leaning. That was what a round table was for.

His mother had been standing exactly here.

He took his hand back.

"Walk it," he said.

They walked it. June sat in Shannon's place with her phone's stopwatch running and reached for the center: three seconds. Lenny stood at the radiator for Kev, walked to the table, touched the middle of it, and walked back: twelve seconds. Archie stood where Hollis had stood and slid an invisible horn; then he stood in Vivienne's place and reached. Every one of them fit, with time left over to stop and say good evening.

At 19:55 on the laptop, Archie walked into the frame himself. He watched himself cross to his mother's chair and sit beside her and put his hand on the table, and turn it over when she laid hers on top. On the screen, in the candlelight, she looked small.

"You're not in the forty seconds, Dad," said June. "You're not on camera at all until fifty-five."

"I was at the back with Dale."

"Dale was with him," said Lenny.

"I know," said June. "I'm saying what the lieutenant's going to say."

Archie was looking at the frozen frame on the laptop — the little flames, the empty chairs, the horn moved a few inches to the left — and he said the thing he'd been sitting on since seven forty-four.

"She never touched it. Ivy. Not after I put it down. She was at her place for ten seconds with her hands under the cloth, twenty-three inches away, and then she was downstairs until seven fifty-eight, and then she sat down, and then Kev's camera has it lying there in the dark until Lorelei picks it up herself. The rod's a fraud, fine. The scarf's a reset. The whole act's a fraud. But she never laid a finger on that horn after I had it at my mouth."

"Then it's one of four people," said June. "Or the crew."

"Ivy didn't do it," said Archie. "That's what matters."

June opened her mouth, and then shut it again and wrote something on her legal pad that he didn't read.

"Kev," she said finally.

Archie was already looking at the radiator.

"He lied to Tess for three days," June said, ticking it down her pad. "He was in the prop room Friday night with the horn in his hands."

"Before the toot."

"I know. I'm saying he sneaks. And he's ten feet away." She turned her phone around. "Spectral Kev. Eleven thousand four hundred subscribers on Friday. Six hundred and four thousand this morning. He turned the ads on Sunday night. He's probably made more since Saturday than in five years."

"Twelve seconds from the radiator," said Lenny, to nobody.

Archie thought of Kev in the crawlspace with his EMF meter, believing in everything. Then he thought about what a dead medium was worth to a man with eleven thousand subscribers, and didn't like how easily the number came.

"I'm going to ask him," he said.

Lenny ejected the card, put it back in its little case, and held it out. He didn't let go of it when Archie took hold.

"It puts your mother at the table," Lenny said.

"I know."

"Where are you taking it?"

"Tess," said Archie. Lenny let go, and Archie was halfway down the aisle before Lenny had finished winding up the cord.


---

# Chapter 27

## Narrowed Down

By two o'clock it was raining sideways off the harbor, cold enough to sting.

Lieutenant Neil Brannock's car was a dark gray Explorer with state plates, parked nose-in at the back of the town hall lot under a bare maple. The driver's door stood open. Brannock sat sideways in the driver's seat with his feet out on the running board and Tess's laptop on his knees, and his reading glasses on, which he wore with the resignation other men bring to a cast. Rain was coming in on his left shoulder. He didn't seem to have noticed. Beside the open door, in her charcoal coat with the hood up and her braid gone dark with wet, stood Tess Calloway, holding a clear evidence bag with the little black card case inside it. Archie stood beside her in his buffalo-check coat, which had begun to smell like a sheep.

He had called her at half past twelve from the hearse. She had listened without a word for so long that he checked the phone, and then said: *Parking lot. Two o'clock. You get ten minutes.* She had carried it to Brannock herself.

Brannock watched seven forty-four once more — Ivy's hands under the tablecloth, twenty-three inches from the horn — and then the forty seconds, and then sat back and took the glasses off.

"Where did you get this?"

"It was found in the cameraman's kit," said Archie. "By Mr. Marchand. Who gave it to me. As a witness."

"When?"

"This morning."

"Where?"

There was no point. "The airport in Owls Head. Around eight-thirty."

Tess turned her head and looked at him. He felt it on the side of his face like a draft from an open door.

"On Sunday Mr. Marchand told me that card was exteriors and had gone to Los Angeles," said Brannock. "I'll be having a conversation with his lawyers about the word *exteriors*." He closed the laptop and handed it out to Tess, who tucked it under her coat. "So the only footage that clears my suspect has been hand-delivered by a man who fools cameras for a living, who got it in an airport from a man who lied to me about it. What did you give him?"

"An interview. And something else. It isn't relevant."

"Everything's relevant until it isn't. This goes to our video people in Augusta. If it's clean, it's clean."

"It's clean."

"I hope so. I really do." He meant it. "Now. You want me to call the jail and open the door."

"She never touched the horn after I put it down. You just watched it."

"I watched twenty minutes of a tablecloth. The tampering stands either way — she wiped it, she lied about the hands, she ditched the remote. That's what put her in a cell, and nothing on this card changes it." Brannock looked out at the rain. "On the murder, your card clears her on one condition, and the condition is seven thirty-five. Did your lips touch the metal, Mr. Fairweather? Or did you make a noise near it and let a roomful of people and two cameras believe they did?"

"I put my mouth on it."

"I'm sure you did. You're very convincing. It's the job."

"Then swab it. You'll find me."

"We did. It's a low-template mixture after that scarf went over it — the victim, and possibly somebody else. The tox came back fast because the sergeant asked for one compound by name. DNA's a different line, and it's a long one. I'll know about a third person in six weeks, maybe." He let that sit. "So say it was real. Say you tooted, honest as the day. Then the horn's clean at seven thirty-five, and I've got your forty seconds. I've got the house manager, who moved it when the producer asked her to. I've got a fan by the radiator with six hundred thousand new reasons. I've got a widow who'd paid the victim a thousand and fifty dollars." He paused. "And I've got your mother."

"My mother."

"Your mother, who cut monkshood out of your garden for the kitchen table — fresh on Saturday morning, she told us, very helpfully. Your mother, who's in that frame at her chair, two feet from the horn, with her hands on the back of it. Who's told two of my troopers, at length and without being asked, that she's despised fake mediums since she was twelve years old. And a fake medium had been in town three days promising to bring her father to that table." He said it plainly, with no pleasure in it. "I don't think your mother poisoned anybody. I'm telling you what that tape says to a jury that's never met her. A mother and her son. He does the talking; she's at the table."

Rain drummed on the roof of the Explorer.

"And if the toot was a trick," said Brannock, "then I'm back where I was on Monday. Ms. Crane at seven-twenty, alone with the props. Or the one man in that building with two million reasons, and poison in his own dooryard, and the best pair of hands in the country." He swung his feet in out of the rain. "Your card doesn't open a door, Mr. Fairweather. It opens one I'd nearly finished with, back onto you and her. If you've got a better name than your mother's for those forty seconds, now's the time."

Archie thought of the radiator, and twelve seconds, and six hundred thousand subscribers. He thought of a man in a bow tie in the Galley on Tuesday morning, with forty people watching him find out what Archie thought of him.

"No," he said.

Brannock looked at him through the open door.

"I did that once this week," said Archie. "In a diner. I'm not doing it again to get my mother off a list."

For a second Brannock looked almost sorry. Then he put the key in the ignition.

"One more thing. Since Saturday you've been to see the victim's daughter, the widow, the producer, the fan, and the chair of the select board. Every person on that tape is a witness in a homicide. If you go near any of them again about this case, I'll treat it as tampering with a witness, and I'll charge it. I won't enjoy it. I'll do it anyway." He looked at Tess for the first time. "Sergeant Calloway won't be in contact with you about this case from here on. Anything you have, you bring to me. In daylight. Through the front door."

"Yes, sir," said Tess.

Brannock pulled the door shut. The Explorer backed out of its space, crossed the empty lot, signaled — at nobody — and turned onto Main Street toward Route 1, wipers going, and was gone.

Tess stood in the rain with the evidence bag in her hand and the laptop under her coat.

"You went to the airport," she said.

"Yes."

"At eight in the morning. Alone. To a witness who'd lied to us." She was looking at the empty space where the Explorer had been. "And then you called me at half past twelve and let me carry it to him. And let him ask you where it came from with me standing here."

"I didn't think he'd—"

"He thinks I sent you." She turned. Her face was wet, and her gray eyes didn't move from his. "He's thought I was running you since the walkthrough on Sunday. He was nearly done thinking it. Now he isn't." She held the evidence bag up between them. "If you'd called me at seven instead of twelve-thirty, I'd have driven. I'd have walked in with a badge and had Marchand sign for it in front of a witness, with a time on it. It'd be worth something."

"He'd never have handed it to a badge."

"Then we'd have gotten a warrant."

"And he'd have been in Boston, and that card would have been in a trash can at Logan."

"Maybe." She didn't give him an inch. "Why didn't you call me?"

He thought about lying to her, and knew he wouldn't manage it. He never had, with anyone he cared about.

"Because you'd have told me not to go," he said.

"Yes," said Tess. "I would have." The rain ran off the edge of her hood. "That's what calling me is for, Archie."

She turned and walked away across the lot to the back door of the town hall, with the bag and the laptop held against her, not hurrying, through the rain. The door shut behind her, and he stood in the empty lot in a wet coat that smelled like a sheep.


---

# Chapter 28

## Paint Day

At four o'clock on Wednesday afternoon Archie climbed the opera house stairs with a square of cardboard under his arm that said, in black marker, SHOW CANCELLED — WITH APOLOGIES — A.F., and found the top of the stairs blocked by Bev Thibodeau.

She was in a barn coat and rubber boots, with a clipboard held against her chest, and behind her the hall was full of noise.

"What's that," she said.

He showed her. She read it. She took it out of his hands, folded it once, and put it in the pocket of her barn coat.

"No," said Bev.

"Bev—"

"Sit down. No — don't sit down. Go see Hollis. She's got you on a list."

He went past her into the hall and stopped.

There were fifty-one people in the Merrow Opera House. Ozzie Lund and Dale Pinkham were up on orchard ladders at the harbor-side windows, unhooking the blackout drapes. The liars' table — Elwin, Dickie and Pickle — had set a plank across a pair of sawhorses by the radiators and were stirring five-gallon buckets of paint with the slow seriousness of men baiting traps. Mattie Pinkham was at the top of the scaffolding along the north wall in a Red Sox cap, shouting down about rollers. Deb Coombs from the library was running blue tape along the window trim with the face of a woman asked to alphabetize the ocean. There were drop cloths everywhere, a radio playing country music, and a folding table by the door crowded with casseroles under foil. Somebody had brought a crockpot of baked beans. Somebody had brought three.

Hollis Tibbetts stood at the front of the stage with a clipboard, pointing.

"There you are," she called down. "You're late. You're on the scaffold."

"Hollis, I came up here to cancel—"

"I know what you came up here for. Bev called me from the landing." She was in her painting clothes: a man's flannel shirt with the sleeves rolled, old corduroys, the gray fingerless gloves. "Listen to me, Archie. I know about that girl, and I know about the lieutenant, and you can't fix either one from a stepladder. But you bought this building with a deed that says it's a theater forever, and it's a theater on Friday night whether you're in a mood or not." She pointed her pencil at the north wall. "Window trim. Cream, not white. You like heights."

"I don't like—"

"You hung upside down from my flies for a month. Up."

He went up.

He was on the second tier of the scaffold with a brush and a can of trim paint the color of heavy cream when Ozzie freed the last hook on the first harbor window and let the drape fall.

It came down in one long black slither and piled on the drop cloth, and the light came in.

The rain had blown through an hour before. Behind it, low over the far shore of the bay, the sun had come out under the back edge of the cloud, and it poured in through the tall arched window at a slant, gold and enormous, across the drop cloths and up the far wall to the pressed-tin ceiling, which lit up square by square. Fifty-one people stopped what they were doing. Ozzie stood at the top of his ladder with the hook in his hand and looked at what he'd done.

"Well," he said, after a while. "Next one."

They took down the next one, and the next, until the hall was so full of light that Archie could see the dust going up through the gold air.

He lasted about twenty minutes on the scaffold before he had to give somebody something.

He climbed down and found Bev on the landing with her clipboard.

"Lobster rolls," said Archie. "For sixty. People will keep coming."

"Galley's closed," said Bev. "Closes at two."

"I'll buy every lobster in the co-op tank. I'll pay you double. I'll wash the pans. Badly, but I'll wash them."

Bev looked at him over her reading glasses. Then she took out her phone and dialed a man twenty feet away.

"Dickie," she said into it. "Get down off that sawhorse. We're opening."

---

At a quarter past six, with the work lamps coming up on their stands and the harbor going dark in the windows, Bev and Dickie Thibodeau came up the stairs with a hotel pan between them, and went down and came back with another, and another, and the opera house ate.

There were sixty-two lobster rolls in split-top buns toasted in butter on the Galley's flattop, the meat in big pink-and-white pieces with nothing on it but a little mayonnaise. People sat along the edge of the stage with their legs hanging over and their paint-spattered hands full. Dale Pinkham ate his with his eyes closed. Mattie ate three and argued that the third didn't count because it had been small. Ozzie carried one to Vivienne on a paper plate with both hands, as if it were a corsage, and she accepted it the same way. Lenny had heard the word *lobster* at five o'clock and gone home to eat a cheese sandwich on principle.

Over by the radiators, Elwin Coombs took his roll from Archie's own hands. He looked at it, and at Archie, and nodded once, and said nothing whatsoever.

Sixty-four days. Archie didn't mind. He had never had a nod before.

He sat on the edge of the stage beside his mother and ate his own lobster roll, and it was without question the best thing he had eaten since August. She and Ozzie had their heads together over something on Ozzie's phone; when Archie leaned over to look, his mother put the phone face down on Ozzie's knee and asked him whether he'd tried the beans.

Shannon Mayo had come in at five with her own roller and gone straight to Hollis, who put her on the long south wall beside her. They'd worked side by side ever since, Hollis talking and Shannon not. Shannon didn't come for a lobster roll. Hollis brought her one. Shannon ate it facing the wall she'd painted, and when Archie came past carrying an empty hotel pan, she moved her paint tray out of his way without looking up.

He took it. It was more than he'd had on Sunday.

---

Kayla Bissonnette came up the stairs at a quarter to seven with a liquor-store carton under her arm and an errand she'd rather not run in front of fifty people who had known her since diapers.

"Sorry, everybody," she said. "The lieutenant wants every glove in the building."

"Every glove," said Dale Pinkham.

"Work gloves, rubber gloves, stage gloves, Chief." She looked wretched. "He said every."

So the town of Merrow gave up its gloves. Pinkham went first, peeling off the yellow rubber dish gloves he'd been rolling paint in and laying them in Kayla's carton with tremendous dignity. Ozzie dropped in his work gloves. Hollis came down from the stage with the shoebox of crew gloves from the light booth, set it on top of the pile, and pulled off her own gray fingerless pair and dropped them in after it.

"Those haven't got fingers, Kayla Bissonnette, so tell the lieutenant I'll want them back by Friday. My hands get cold."

"Yes, Mrs. Tibbetts."

The liars' table, after a consultation, surrendered six canvas gloves stiff enough to stand up on their own. Then Vivienne drew off a pair of long black kid gloves she had not been wearing to paint in, and had produced from somewhere for the occasion, and laid them in the box one at a time.

"I wore those to be sawn in half in Atlantic City in 1966," she told Kayla. "I'll want a receipt."

Half the hall laughed. Kayla wrote her a receipt on the back of a paint-store flyer and carried the town's gloves down the stairs with her shoulders back.

Archie watched his mother sit back down beside Ozzie with her bare hands in her lap, and in his head Brannock was saying it again in the rain: *two feet from the horn, with her hands on the back of it.* He'd handed over the card at two o'clock; it was not yet seven. The lieutenant was very good at his job. Archie would have liked that better in somebody else's case.

---

June found him on the scaffold at a quarter past seven.

She came up the ladder with a can of trim paint hooked over her wrist as a pretext, and sat on the plank beside him with her Doc Martens hanging over the edge. They were at the stage-left end now, at the window that had been bare on Saturday night. Out past it the harbor was black, and the Light on the point came and went.

June watched it go around twice.

"I want to do the Chair," she said. "Friday. With you."

He laid his brush across the top of the can.

"Not come out at the end and wave. Not the part you'd show a volunteer. I want you to teach me the method. All of it." She was looking at the window, not at him. "You told Teddy Marchand how the Glass Box works. In an airport. On a napkin."

"I know."

"So." It was the voice she used to close an argument. "Me too."

Archie looked down at the hall. Hollis was making Dale Pinkham move a ladder six inches to the left. His mother sat on the stage edge beside Ozzie with her shoes off, laughing at something he'd said in about four words. Fifty-odd people were painting his grandfather's theater for nothing, with lobster on their breath.

"It's Gus's," he said. "The Chair. It isn't mine. He showed me the summer I was nine, under this stage, and before he'd show me one thing he made me swear. Lenny builds it. Your grandmother comes up through it. Nobody else alive. There's an oath."

"I know there's an oath."

"You'd have to swear it and keep it. For the rest of your life. Not Mattie, not your mother, not some boy at Yale, not the Supreme Court—"

"Dad." She turned and looked at him then. "I know what a secret is. I kept one from you for two months." She put out her right hand between them on the plank, palm down. "Okay. What do I swear on?"

He reached over and took the jack of clubs out of the air beside her left ear.

June jumped. Then she saw which card it was — the one that had landed face up by his shoe in the library the day he caught her — and her face went red to the pencil in her bun. And then, before she could stop it, it lit up all at once, the whole of it, the way his own face must have lit on a kitchen floor in 1982 with a silver dollar coming out of his ear.

She shut it off almost at once. But he'd seen it.

He laid the card face down on the plank between them. She put her hand flat on it, and he put his on top of hers, and up on the scaffold in the smell of paint and lobster and wet wool, Archie Fairweather said the words his grandfather had said to him on the boards beneath their feet when he was nine years old, and his daughter said them back, every one, without a mistake.

"Yes," he said, when she'd finished. "Yes. Tonight. Lenny's got the practice trap in the workshop."

"Tonight," June agreed. She put the jack of clubs in her cardigan pocket and climbed down without another word, and he watched her cross the floor to Mattie and say something that made Mattie shriek and get paint on Deb Coombs.

---

By eight o'clock the north wall was done and the south wall nearly; the tin ceiling would be another day and another argument. People were finding their coats and disputing whose crockpot was whose. Behind the stage the painted drape glowed under the work lamps — gulls and schooners and a sky far too blue for Maine.

Hollis stood at the center of the stage with her clipboard under her arm and her hands pushed into the pockets of her corduroys. She looked out at the half-painted hall, and at Archie coming down off the scaffold with cream paint to his elbows, and gave her short bark of a laugh.

"Your grandfather would have loved this," she said. "Every bucket of it."


---

# Chapter 29

## The Method

The carriage house had been built for horses, then for the hearse, and since September it had belonged to Lenny, who had filled it with a drill press, a band saw, two welding tanks chained to the wall, and a workbench with every tool hung inside its own painted outline, so you could tell at a glance which one had been borrowed. At the far end, under two hanging work lamps, was the platform.

Lenny had built it the first week, out of plywood and four-by-fours, exactly as high as the stage of the Merrow Opera House. In the middle he had cut a trap the same size as Gus's — two feet by four, two leaves hinged at the center — and underneath it, where no audience would ever see, he had put three wooden steps, a counterweight on a rope, and a sandbag to sit on.

At ten o'clock on Wednesday night it was cold enough in there to see your breath, in spite of the space heater ticking by the bench. Archie still had paint under his fingernails and, he had been told twice, a stripe of it in his hair. He had set a ladder-back chair from the kitchen in the middle of the platform, folded a white sheet over its back, and swung one of the lamps down so it fell on the chair in a hard, bright circle.

It looked, he thought, terrific.

June sat on an upturned bucket in front of the platform with a notebook on her knee. She had already ruled the page into columns. She was wearing her oldest cardigan and a pair of Lenny's work gloves, because Lenny had told her nobody touched his trap with bare hands, and she had taken him at his word.

"Ladies and gentlemen," said Archie.

"Dad."

"I'm setting the scene."

"I've seen the scene. There's a video of Gus doing it in 1979, shot from the balcony by somebody's uncle. You can hear a man unwrapping a sandwich." She clicked her pen. "You said all of it. That's what I'm writing down. All of it."

She had asked that evening, on the scaffold, with the jack of clubs under her hand, and he'd said yes. Now, with the sheet in his hands, meaning it and doing it turned out to be two separate tricks, and he had only ever practiced one of them.

"All right," he said. "The count."

"The count is everything. One, two, three — that's what they hear. But Gus's count took eleven seconds. You say *one*, and then you tell them something. He always started by saying every trick was a promise." June wrote that down. "Then you tell them about the chair, or who used to sit in it, or what it's like to wait for somebody. You make them lean in. Then *two*—"

"What holds the sheet up?"

"In the video," June said. "When he puts it on, it doesn't fall flat. It's wide at the top. Like there's already somebody under it." She looked at him over the notebook. "You skipped that."

At the workbench, Lenny, who had been filing something small, stopped filing.

"I skipped that," Archie admitted.

"Why?"

He had a dozen reasons. He'd been giving them to reporters for thirty years, and they were all charming, and they all meant *no*.

"Habit," he said.

He turned the sheet inside out and showed her.

Sewn into the muslin, about two feet below the top, was a channel of doubled cloth, and inside the channel was a long oval of spring steel no thicker than a coat hanger. He dropped the sheet over the chair. The steel caught on the top of the ladder-back and held the cloth out at the width of a man's shoulders, and the rest fell away in folds to the floor, pooling at the back. Under the lamp it looked like someone sitting under a sheet.

"That's the secret," he said. "From the second it goes on, the chair looks full. They think they're watching an empty chair. They've been watching a shape, and when somebody comes up into it, the shape doesn't change."

June stared at it.

"It's a coat hanger," she said.

"It's a very good coat hanger."

"Spring steel," said Lenny.

"The chair stands on the front leaf of the trap. The back leaf drops. Whoever's under there comes up the steps behind the chair, inside the sheet — that's why it's long at the back — and sits. Eight seconds if you're quick. Lenny holds the counterweight so the leaf doesn't bang, and there's felt on every edge. On three you pull up and back, and the hoop goes with it, and you throw it behind you and never look at it again. Nobody looks at a sheet once there's a person."

"And the person is Grandma."

"The person is Grandma." That had been settled over lobster rolls, with Vivienne holding court from a stepladder. "She comes up. I stand by the chair and put the sheet on and hold the corner so it doesn't move. You do the patter. You do the count. You pull."

"Me," said June.

"You."

She wrote it down, pressing hard.

"She's seventy-three," said Lenny. "She comes up slow."

"Then I count slow," said June.

Lenny looked at her for a moment over his reading glasses. Then he went back to filing.

---

"Tonight," said Archie, "your grandmother will be played by me."

It was dark under the platform and smelled of sawdust and cold concrete, and he had to fold himself onto the steps like a deck chair. Lenny lowered himself onto the sandbag, put one hand on the rope, and took out his stopwatch.

"Don't step on the front leaf," he told June, through the boards. "You'll go through."

"I wasn't going to."

"Everybody isn't going to. Then they do."

Overhead, the sheet went over the chair. June took a breath.

"This is a chair," she said. "It's — an ordinary chair. One."

Lenny pulled. The back leaf swung down without a whisper, and Archie went up into the muffled white of the sheet and got one knee onto the platform, and had his other foot still on the top step when June said, briskly, "Two, three," and the sheet went away and the lamp hit him in the face.

He was on his hands and knees behind the chair with one leg in a hole in the floor. He looked up at his daughter. She looked down at her father.

"Ankle," said Lenny, from below. "That's his ankle."

June put both hands over her face.

"Again," said Archie, backing down the steps. "You counted it like a fire drill. And you were looking at the chair."

"How do you know?"

"I could hear it. Your voice was pointed at the chair, and if your voice is pointed at the chair, so is everybody's attention. Where you look, they look. You have to look at them, Junebug. The whole count. You can't check."

"I *know*."

They did it twice more, and the second time it worked, more or less. Neither of them said so.

---

Afterward she sat on the edge of the platform with her boots hanging over, and he sat beside her. Underneath them Lenny was doing something to the counterweight that involved a wrench and a good deal of breathing.

"The last time he did it," June said. "Did you know it was the last time?"

"No."

"What do you remember?"

On Monday, on Dale Pinkham's boat, he had said it in one sentence: ten years old, second row. It came out differently now.

"I had a seat on the aisle," he said. "Second row, on the left. Your grandmother wasn't next to me, because she was under the stage. I had a bag of peppermints somebody's husband had bought me at the Galley, and I ate every one before the intermission. The radiators were going. The whole hall smelled like wet coats." He looked at the chair. "He did the Chair last. He always did. And during the count, the whole eleven seconds, he looked straight at me. Straight down into the second row. I thought it was because I was his grandson. I thought it was a secret between us."

"It was the misdirection."

"It was the misdirection," he agreed. "It was probably also because I was his grandson. Both things can be true. Then the sheet came off, and it was your grandmother in the chair, in her old sequins, and the whole place stood up. They hadn't seen her on that stage since she was twenty-four. He kissed her hand. The curtain came down. And I went down to the green room to wait for him, because he'd said he'd walk me home, and I fell asleep on a pile of coats."

June looked at her hands.

"Do you think he drowned?" she said.

The heater ticked. Under the platform, the wrench had stopped.

"I think he did the trick," Archie said.

"Then where did he go?"

"I don't know." He held his hands out and looked at the paint under the nails. "I really don't know, June."

She sat beside him for a while. Then she got up, pulled the work gloves back on, and picked the sheet up off the boards.

"Lenny," she said. "Will you go under?"

"No," said Lenny.

"Please."

A long silence from beneath the platform. Then the sandbag creaked.

"I'm going to watch from the house," Archie said, and went and sat on the bucket.

---

She began with Gus's line, the one about promises, and then she left it behind. She told the bucket, the hearse and her father that this chair had come out of a kitchen in this house, and that a great many bad breakfasts had been eaten on it, most of them cooked by her father. She put the sheet on in one clean movement, and the hoop caught, and the shape sat there.

"One," June said.

She looked at him.

He could not afterward have repeated a word of what she said next, because she was looking at him the whole time — not at the sheet, not at the trap, not at the place where every instinct she had must have been telling her to check — straight down at the bucket in the cold, for all eleven seconds. Behind her the back leaf dropped without a sound. The sheet never moved.

"Three," said June, and pulled it up and back and threw it behind her without a glance.

Lenny Szabo sat in the chair in all three of his sweaters, with his reading glasses pushed up on his forehead and his arms folded, wearing the expression of a man waiting for a bus he did not expect to enjoy.

It was perfect.

Archie stood up. He couldn't say anything. June's face changed and started to ask, and he held up one hand.

"I'll be right back," he said, and went out.

---

The dooryard was dark and cold. The fog hadn't come in yet, and the sky over the harbor was full of stars. He stood on the frozen gravel in his shirtsleeves with his breath smoking and let the cold have his face for a minute.

Across the dooryard the kitchen light was on, and his mother was at the window with the phone at her ear and her back to the glass. She had taken two calls into the pantry at supper and come out both times saying it was nobody. His mother had never in her life called anyone nobody. He thought of a black shawl on the back of a chair two feet from the horn, and made himself stop.

Through the carriage-house window he could see the lamp, and the platform, and his daughter shaking out the sheet to do it again. Lenny's bald head rose out of the trap. He said something. June laughed — short and surprised, the kind she usually caught before it got out — and didn't catch it. She put the sheet over the chair, and the hoop caught, and the shape sat under the lamp, waiting for somebody to come up into it.

His phone buzzed. TESS CALLOWAY.

"Sergeant."

"This call isn't happening." Her voice was low, with an engine under it; she was parked somewhere. "The AG's office is filing on Ivy Crane in the morning. Murder."

Archie closed his eyes.

"Okafor's put you on the jail's visitor list for ten tomorrow. She cleared it with the AG this afternoon. Don't be late."

"I won't."

The engine idled. He waited for the rest of it, the voice from the parking lot, and it didn't come.

"I watched your toot forty times," Tess said. "I believe you."

Then she hung up.


---

# Chapter 30

## Visiting Hours

The Knox County Jail was a low brick building on the edge of Rockland with a flagpole, a lot full of pickup trucks, and a lobby that smelled of floor wax and burnt coffee. At five minutes to ten on Thursday morning Archie stood in it with his pockets on a plastic tray — keys, wallet, a deck of cards, two half-dollars, a roll of peppermints and a lock pick shaped like a hairpin — while a corrections officer named Doucette looked at the lock pick and then at him.

"It's a hairpin," said Archie.

"It's not a hairpin."

"It's a very ambitious hairpin."

Doucette put it in an envelope and wrote on the envelope.

"He's with me," said Margaret Okafor, behind him. "God help me."

She was in her fifties, compact, in a black wool coat and flat shoes, with close-cropped hair gone silver at the temples and a leather briefcase older than her client. The wedding cake, he had learned afterward, had been lemon; the hotel kitchen staff had eaten it, and she'd sent him a thank-you note on letterhead.

She walked him down a corridor of painted cinderblock to a door with a narrow window in it, and stopped.

"Ground rules," she said. "You are a witness for the State. You do not discuss your statement or the scarf. If she starts to, you stop her, and if you don't, I will, and I'm better at it." She looked at him over a pair of reading glasses she hadn't put on. "You're here because the State has a theory, my client has a confession that isn't one, and the most famous debunker in the world is paying my invoices. I want a third party, Mr. Fairweather. Somebody with a better reason than an inheritance. Find me one."

"You think there is one."

"I think the Attorney General filed a murder charge this morning on a scarf and a bad feeling." She opened the door. "Twenty minutes."

---

The attorney visiting room was the green of a hospital corridor, with a steel table bolted to the floor and three plastic chairs. Ivy Crane was already sitting in one of them, in an orange jail sweatshirt, with her hands folded on the table. Her hair was still scraped back. Somebody had taken her hair elastic and given her a rubber band instead.

She looked at him, and then at her lawyer.

"I said I didn't want to see him."

"And I said I decide who's useful," said Okafor, and sat down and uncapped a fountain pen.

Archie sat across from Ivy. He had driven twenty minutes down Route 1 rehearsing an opening, and when he opened his mouth it was gone.

"They charged you," he said.

"I know. I was there."

"With murder, Ivy."

"I know what they charged me with." Her voice was perfectly flat. "I told them it was my fault. It was."

"You told them that because you set out the horn."

She didn't answer.

"Every night," he said. "You lay it out. You put it in the middle of the table. And you think if you hadn't—"

"If there's no horn, there's nothing to put anything on." She said it to the table. "If I'd quit at twenty. If I'd quit at twelve. That's not a feeling, Mr. Fairweather. That's arithmetic."

"It's arithmetic with a hole in it."

"Then find the hole." She looked up. "Isn't that why she brought you?"

He wanted to argue. He had the argument ready; he could feel it arranging itself, patter and all. He looked at her folded hands and put it away.

"I taught my daughter a trick last night," he said.

Okafor's pen paused.

"Gus's finale. The Empty Chair. She asked me yesterday to teach her all of it, so I did. In the carriage house, with a coat hanger and a sheet and my engineer sitting under a trapdoor on a sandbag." He shrugged. "She was terrible the first time. She nearly broke my ankle."

"All of it?" said Ivy.

"All of it."

"Including the part you didn't want to show her."

He looked at her. "Including that."

"How old?"

"Sixteen."

Ivy unfolded her hands and laid them flat on the steel.

"I was twelve," she said. "A motel in Nashua. She turned off the lights and held a hairbrush out in the dark and said, *Take my hand, baby.* And I took it, and then I learned to let go of it." She said it like a recipe. "You know the rest. It's on the fan's video."

"Ivy," said Okafor, not looking up.

"It isn't a secret anymore." Ivy didn't take her eyes off Archie. "When it's over and the lights come up, before anybody else can touch it, you wipe the horn. There's lipstick on the small end. Every séance since I was twelve. You don't think about it. You just do it."

"It's a reset," said Archie.

"It's a reset." For the first time something moved in her face. "I wasn't hiding anything on Saturday night except her."

Okafor wrote that down, word for word, and underlined it.

Archie sat back in the plastic chair. In his head, Kev Duchesne was crouched in the dark under the opera house stage with an EMF meter, telling him about the Trumpet Hiatus. *Sister Lori wouldn't use the trumpet in private sittings, Mr. Fairweather. For years. The forums can't even agree when it started. Most people think Brother Amos told her to.*

"Your mother stopped using the horn," he said. "In the private sittings. For years."

Ivy's eyes narrowed. "Kev Duchesne."

"Kev Duchesne."

"Brother Amos told her to put it away. That's the story."

"Brother Amos was your mother in a deeper voice."

"Brother Amos was a bathrobe and an accent she got off a record of Welsh hymns," said Ivy. "Yes."

"Then here's what I know about performers." He leaned forward. "You don't drop the best routine you've got for years because a spirit guide says so. You drop a routine for two reasons. It's stopped working, or it hurt somebody. That horn never stopped working. It's the best trumpet I ever sat with, and I've sat with forty." He kept his voice low. "When did she stop?"

Somewhere down the corridor a door buzzed and banged.

"Two thousand three," Ivy said. "November. I was seven."

Okafor's pen stopped.

"I don't remember much. I remember it was raining. We were parked outside a church. Here — in Rockland. She'd gone inside, which she never did unless she was working a church, and I was in the back seat with a coloring book. She came out with a newspaper under her coat and got into the driver's seat and didn't start the car." Ivy looked at a point on the green wall above his shoulder. "She just sat there with the wipers off and cried until the windows were all rain and you couldn't see out. I'd never seen her cry when it wasn't for a sitter."

"Did she say why?"

"She said a man had died." A small shrug. "I was seven. I wanted to know if we could still get McDonald's."

"Years later," said Ivy, "in a hotel bar in Phoenix, after three of something, she told me she couldn't do the horn on a private sitting for years after the man in Maine. That's how she said it. *The man in Maine.* As if that was his name." She looked back at Archie. "That's all I know. I don't know who he was."

"Would she have written him down?"

"She wrote everybody down."

"Ivy," said Okafor again, differently.

"She kept appointment books," Ivy said. "Thirty years of them. Every sitter since the church halls — names, towns, what they wanted, what she charged them, what worked. In her own shorthand. Nobody can read it but her." She stopped. "Nobody could."

"Where are they?"

She didn't answer right away. He watched her decide.

"In a hatbox," she said. "In Pru Haskell's office safe at the inn. I moved them on Sunday. I told Pru they were Mother's private things." Her chin came up a fraction. "They are."

"Why move them?"

"Because those books are thirty years of people crying in rented rooms," said Ivy, "and I wasn't going to have them read out on a folding table by a lieutenant who hates his reading glasses."

Okafor had stopped writing. She looked at her client, and then at Archie, with the expression of a woman who has just been handed a third party with a ribbon on it.

"The estate isn't probated," she said, half to herself. "The papers belong to the estate. My client is the sole heir. And Mrs. Haskell is an innkeeper, not a probate judge." She tore a fresh sheet from her legal pad and began to write in a fast, upright hand. "Release of personal property. To Archibald Fairweather, one hatbox, contents, from the custody of Prudence Haskell. Ivy, read it before you sign it. Always read it."

Ivy read it and signed it. Okafor signed under her, added her bar number, tore the sheet off and folded it once, and held it out across the table.

Archie took it.

"Read them," Ivy said. She was looking at the paper in his hand, not at him. "Then you'll know what she was. All of it."

"I already liked her," Archie said. "That's the problem."

Ivy looked at him then. There was a knock on the door window — Doucette, holding up five fingers — and she stood, and put her hands behind her back because that was what you did here, and went out without looking back.

---

In the corridor, Okafor shook his hand and kept hold of it a second too long.

"Third party, Mr. Fairweather," she said. "Bring me one. And don't make me read about it in the *Press Herald* first."

She went off toward the interview rooms. Archie walked the other way, toward the lobby and his envelope with the hairpin in it, with Ivy Crane's signature folded in his hand.

Halfway down the corridor a door opened, and Tess Calloway came out of it with a trooper Archie didn't know and a thick file under her arm.

She was in the charcoal coat. Her braid was wet; it must have started raining again. She saw him, and her stride didn't change, and her eyes went once — quick, gray, exact — to the paper in his hand, where the fold had come open enough to show two signatures, and then back to his face.

He didn't say *Sergeant*. She didn't say anything.

They passed each other in the green corridor, close enough to touch, and kept walking, and behind him the door at the far end buzzed and opened and closed.


---

# Chapter 31

## Dress Rehearsal

Pru Haskell was in Bangor for her daughter's birthday, and she was the only person alive with the combination to the safe in the Spindrift Inn's office. She'd be back at nine. She had told him so twice on the phone that morning, kindly, and a third time at noon, less kindly.

By six o'clock on Thursday evening Archie had looked at his grandfather's watch eleven times.

The opera house didn't care. It was full of light and noise and fresh paint, the radiators knocking under the black windows. Ozzie and three of the Pinkham cousins had set out the folding chairs in rows that afternoon. The painted harbor drape was flown out, and the stage was cluttered with everything the show needed and some things it didn't: Lenny's cabinet, a rack of costumes, a stepladder, a bentwood chair with a white sheet folded over its back, and Chief Dale Pinkham, in the center aisle, holding a flashlight and a list.

"Archie," the chief called. "When folks come in. Do I tear the tickets or just look at 'em?"

"There aren't any tickets, Dale. It's free."

The chief considered this. "Then what do I tear?"

"Nothing. You smile and you point at chairs."

"Ayuh." Dale wrote something on the list. "Smile. Point."

They were running Act One in order, which meant they were on the Sparrows, which meant Lenny had flatly refused to release the doves. "They don't rehearse," he'd said. "They know it." So Archie stood alone in the gold wash at center stage producing four imaginary birds from an empty silk, and on the fourth he lifted his empty hands and said, "And then the stage goes blue and—"

The stage went blue. It went blue about two seconds after he'd stopped needing it to, and he stood there in it with his arms up, looking foolish.

"You said *blue* in the middle of a sentence," Hollis's voice came down from the booth at the back of the balcony. "Cue lines go at the *end* of the sentence, Archie. Like a period. I taught English."

"You taught *drama*."

"I taught it to people who could finish a sentence."

At the stage-right end of the second row, Shannon Mayo was folding programs on her knee from a stack the height of a lobster crate. She didn't laugh. Beside her, Liam did, loudly, and was elbowed.

---

At twenty past six the hinge on Lenny's cabinet began to scream. Lenny oiled it and swung the door, and it screamed. He got down on his knees with a flashlight, said, "Different hinge," and disappeared behind the cabinet.

In the hold, Archie sat down on the lip of the stage with his legs over the edge, and Liam Mayo came straight out of the second row.

"Do the coin," said Liam. "The walking one. Mattie says you do a walking one."

Archie took a half-dollar out of his waistcoat pocket and let it walk across his knuckles, forefinger to little finger and back by the thumb, without looking at it. Liam stood with his chin on the edge of the boards and watched it go.

"You're not even looking," Liam said.

"That's the whole trick. If I look, you look."

Liam dug a nickel out of his jeans and tried. It went over his first knuckle, and sideways, and dropped through a gap between two stage boards with a small, final *tink*.

From somewhere behind the cabinet, Lenny said, "That's gone."

Liam looked at the gap. Then he looked at his mother. Shannon looked at Archie for the first time that evening, a long, level look with nothing in it he could name.

"Liam," she said. "Leave Mr. Fairweather alone."

But she didn't come and get him, and Archie gave Liam the half-dollar, and Liam spent the rest of the hold dropping it on his own knee.

---

After that things went wrong in the ordinary way, which Archie had always loved about rehearsals. At a quarter to seven Dale came up the side steps holding a sheet of paper at arm's length.

"Fire marshal's fella was in at noon," he said, to the stage generally and the booth in particular. "That door at the end of the balcony. The fire escape. Says it's got to be unlocked during the show. Code. Second way out of the balcony."

"That door," Hollis called down, "lets in the entire North Atlantic."

"I just read 'em, Hollis."

"I know you do, Dale." There was a great sigh from the booth, a clatter of keys, and Archie watched her come out along the balcony in her cardigan to the iron door at the far end, unlock it, push it open on a wedge of cold black air, and jam it a few inches ajar with a folded program. "There. Unlocked."

She went back into the booth. A moment later her voice came down again, pitched for teaching. "Now, Mattie. Look here. The cue book lives in this drawer. *This* drawer, not that one, that one's full of my cough drops. Every cue's in pencil. If I get the flu, you open the drawer, you read the pencil, you push the slider when it says GO. That's all theater is."

"What if you get the flu in the *middle*?" said Mattie Pinkham, loudly and with great interest.

"Then you'll be very popular," said Hollis.

---

They did the Chair at seven, out of order, because Vivienne said that if she had to sit in a crawlspace in sequins she'd do it while her knees still worked.

Lenny took her down through the stage-right stairs. Archie heard them go along under the boards — his mother's voice saying *Leonard, if there is a mouse down here, I will know* and Lenny not answering — and then the sound of Lenny settling at the trap. Out in the house Hollis brought everything down but a single hard spot on the bentwood chair, standing on its leaf of the trap at center stage.

June came out into the light in her school cardigan, because the costume wasn't finished, and Archie came out beside her and stood by the chair and put the sheet on it. The hoop caught. The shape sat there.

He didn't look at it. He looked at the hall — Dale in the aisle with his flashlight off, Shannon with a program half-folded on her knee, Liam with the half-dollar in his fist — and listened to his daughter.

She started with Gus's line and then left it behind, and told the house a little about the chair, and about waiting. She looked at them the whole time. He could feel her not looking at the sheet; it was a kind of heat. The count went long and slow and nobody in the hall noticed it was long. Under his hand on the corner of the sheet, nothing moved.

"Three," said June, and pulled.

Vivienne Fairweather sat in the bentwood chair in the black sequined gown she had last worn on this stage in 1988, with her hands folded in her lap and her chin up, and looked out at the house with the expression of a woman who had been sitting there all evening and couldn't think what the fuss was about.

Liam stood straight up on his chair and shouted, "*HOW.*" In the aisle, Dale Pinkham sat down on the floor. Shannon's program slid off her knee. And from the booth, high at the back, came Hollis's short, unmistakable bark of a laugh, and then the spot went soft and wide and warm, exactly on time.

June didn't look at her father. She looked at the floor, with her mouth pressed tight, and he could see every bit of what it cost her not to grin.

---

At the break he went up to the booth with a question about that last cue. He wanted the spot to hold hard a half-second longer after the sheet came off, so the room could see Vivienne before it saw anything else.
