The booth sat at the back of the balcony up three steep wooden steps. He was on the second step when he heard Rosie.

"Mumma says Daddy said it's warm," Rosie said, to nobody in particular. "Where he is. In the water. He said it's warm now."

Hollis's voice came back low.

"Oh, sweetheart. The water's never warm here." A rustle; somebody being shifted on a lap. "That's why your daddy had those big orange gloves. Now. Which one's the blue button? Show me."

Through the doorway he could see the back of Hollis's white head bent over the board, and a small hand on a slider, and Mattie at her elbow with the cue book. The cue would keep. Archie went back down the steps as quietly as the old treads allowed.

His mother was in the stage-left wing in her coat, with the sequins showing at the hem, checking her lipstick in a compact.

"You were magnificent," he said.

"I know, darling. Thank you." She snapped the compact shut. "Don't wait supper."

"Where are you going?"

"Out."

"Out where?"

"Archibald, I am seventy-three years old, and out is a complete sentence." She kissed the air beside his cheek and went out by the stage door, and he stood in the wing and heard Brannock in the rain again, *two feet from the horn*, and told himself it was nothing.

---

He asked about the cue at a quarter past eight, from the doorway of the booth, after Shannon had carried Rosie out asleep on her shoulder.

"Cue," said Hollis, without turning around. "Which one, and what's wrong with it."

"The Chair. After the sheet. Can you hold the spot a half-second longer before you warm it?"

"I can hold it till Christmas." She took a pencil from behind her ear and wrote in the cue book. "Half a second. There. Anything else?"

"That's all."

She went on writing. He left her to it.

---

At twenty minutes to nine he was sitting in the hearse in the dark lot behind the Spindrift Inn with the engine off and the release folded on the dashboard, watching the back door, and at four minutes past nine a pair of headlights swung in off Water Street, and Pru Haskell's little blue Subaru pulled into the space by the kitchen and stopped.


---

# Chapter 32

## The Hatbox

The hatbox had come from a milliner on Newbury Street in Boston, a long time ago. It was round and deep, covered in green-striped paper gone soft at the edges, with a cord handle and a lid that fit too tightly, and at half past nine on Thursday night it sat in the middle of the kitchen table at Fairweather House where the monkshood had been, with Archie in front of it and nobody else awake.

His mother's coat was on its hook by the back door, smelling of cold air and fried clams, and her bedroom door was shut. Lenny's light was out in the carriage house. The fog had come in after supper, and every so often, from out past the point, the bell buoy said something low and irregular about the swell.

Archie took the lid off.

Inside, standing on end like files, were small notebooks: thirty of them, the cheap drugstore kind with cardboard covers, in faded reds and blues and blacks, each with a rubber band around it. Most of the rubber bands had died and stuck. He picked one at random and peeled it open.

The pages were full of tiny, cramped, slanting marks in blue ballpoint. Not words. Clusters of consonants, numbers, dashes, small circles, a little wavy line, a sort of T with a bar through it, check marks. Every page, top to bottom, both sides.

He opened two more, and sat back.

Thirty years of a woman's working life, in a language nobody could read but her.

He was still sitting there at ten when he heard June on the back stairs. She came into the kitchen in her pajama pants and his old Harvard Square sweatshirt, with her hair down, and stopped.

"I couldn't sleep," she said. "The count keeps going in my head." She looked at the hatbox. "Is that it?"

He thought about telling her to go to bed. She had school in the morning and a show at night.

He pulled out a chair for her instead.

---

"It's code," June said, after a minute with one of the books. "Isn't it."

"It's shorthand. Her own." Archie had the third book open in front of him, and he was looking at a cluster of letters near the top of a page — *Lwstn* — and something at the back of his mind had gone quiet and attentive. "Lewiston," he said slowly. "That's Lewiston. No vowels."

He turned a page. A small circle beside a number. A wavy line. The T with the bar.

And then he knew where he'd seen it, and it was so obvious that he laughed out loud.

"It's Gus's," he said.

"What?"

"Chapter nine of *The Dead Don't Whisper*. 'The Billet and the Blue Book.' The old mediums passed notes on their sitters from town to town in a shorthand the Spiritualist camps had used since the eighteen-nineties, and Gus printed the whole table. Consonants only. Numbers for relations. A circle means passed. The wavy line is water. The T is the trumpet. A check means it landed." He could see the fold-out table on the cheap paper, and his own nine-year-old finger moving down the columns on the turret floor. "She told me herself, by the fire at the inn. Every medium of her generation learned the trade out of his book."

"You memorized a table out of a book when you were *nine*?"

"I memorized everything in that book when I was nine. I thought there'd be a test." He pulled the nearest notebook toward him. "Find me 2003."

"Which one's 2003?"

He looked at the covers. There were no years on them. He opened one and looked at the top of the first page — *Th 3/9* — and opened another — *Su 11/2* — and understood.

"She didn't write the year," June said, reading his face. "Just the day and the date."

"Then we guess."

"We don't *guess*." She was already pulling the hatbox toward her side of the table. "If she wrote the day of the week, the date only lands on that day in certain years. March ninth is a Thursday in—" she had her phone out, scrolling a calendar "—1995. 2000. 2006. 2017. 2023. And the towns change, and the prices go up. You read me what she wrote. I'll tell you when she wrote it."

So that was how they did it. June laid the notebooks across the farm table in a long row and worked down it with her phone, a pencil and the back of an envelope. Archie read her the top of each book — first date, first town, first price — and she wrote a year on a yellow sticky note and stuck it on the cover. She wouldn't let him skip ahead, and twice she was right not to.

At eleven she stopped with her pencil in the air.

"Read that one again."

"*Mrs. Albert P., Biddeford. Four, passed. Wants to know did he suffer.*"

"Four is what?"

"Wife."

"Mrs. Albert Pomeroy's wife died."

"Apparently."

"And the man in Saco two pages back — you said his husband."

He looked. He turned back. He turned forward again, and read three more, and sat with his finger on the page.

"She moved everything over one," June said. "Every relation. One place."

"Of course she did." He grinned. "Gus published the table. Any sitter who'd read the book could have read her notes over her shoulder. So she shifted it." He looked at his daughter across a table covered in a dead woman's notebooks. "That's very good, June."

"It's arithmetic," said June, and wrote on the next sticky note.

---

After that he could read it, and he read it to her.

He'd expected a ledger, and they were a ledger, but Lorelei had written down more than the money. *Mrs. Albert P., Biddeford. Husband passed. Wants to know did he suffer. Told her no. Cried — check. Forty dollars.* *Old man, Saco. Wife passed. Wants to know is she still mad about the dog. Told him she loves the dog. Twenty-five dollars. Gave him back ten. He paid in quarters.* *Man, Bangor. Mother passed. Wants the will. Mother says look in the freezer. (It's always the freezer.)*

June laughed at that one, and then looked as though she wished she hadn't.

On a page in what she'd marked 2000, between two sittings in Augusta, there was a single short line by itself.

"*I., four today,*" Archie read. "*Pancakes. No sittings.*"

June took the notebook from him and looked at the line for a while, and gave it back.

By half past twelve the row of notebooks had sticky notes from 1995 to last year, and June had moved one stack apart from the rest. Three books. The towns in those three changed less than in any of the others.

"Rockland," she said. "Thursdays. Over and over, for two years, starting in the fall of 2001. Always the same initials first." She counted, touching each entry with the pencil. It took her a while. "Forty-one times."

Archie took the first of the three books and found the first Thursday.

*Th 10/4. Rcklnd, Pk St. — P.L., Mrrw. Lbstr. 6○ D. 15. ~ qry, 8/01. W. H. dsnt kno — NO calls hse. $350.*

He read it twice in his head before he read it aloud.

"P.L. Merrow."

June looked up.

"Lobsterman. Son passed. D. Fifteen. Water — the quarry. August of 2001." He kept his voice level. "Wife, H. Doesn't know. Don't call the house. Three hundred and fifty dollars."

The refrigerator hummed.

"There's a quarry," June said. "Two miles inland. Mattie says everybody swims there in August."

"Keep going," said Archie, and turned the page.

He read her the two years. He tried to read them quickly, and couldn't. Thursday after Thursday, the same initials. The first time the T with the bar appeared, in December: *Son spoke through T. "Dad." Cried — check.* The price going up in the spring to something she called *special sittings*, two thousand five hundred dollars each. June added it all on the back of the envelope without being asked, and turned the envelope around so he could see the number, and he looked at it and turned it face down.

The lines that worked had check marks.

*"It's warm here" — check.*

*"I'm not cold anymore" — check.*

In October of 2003, near the bottom of a page, in the same tiny hand: *"Come see me on the water" — check.*

Archie stopped reading aloud.

June leaned over and read it herself, and read the mark beside it, and he watched her remember what the mark meant.

He turned the page. There were two more Thursdays, and then a gap. Then, in late November, one entry, without a day of the week.

*11/20. P.L. lost at sea. Saw it in the paper.*

And under that — not in shorthand, not in any code at all, in plain, ordinary, slanting longhand, the only ordinary words in thirty years of notebooks:

*God forgive me.*

And under that, back in the code: *No more T for privates.*

---

"Lost at sea," June said finally. "In November 2003."

"Peter Langley," said Archie.

She looked up.

"It's on the memorial. The granite at the end of the wharf." He could see the stone the way he saw everything he'd ever read once — the cold pink morning, Shannon in his coat, the names cut in rows with their years beside them. "*Arthur Coombs, 1978. Robert and James Haskell, 1991. Peter Langley, 2003.*"

June's face went still. Then it went white, starting at the mouth.

"Langley," she said. "H. Langley."

"June—"

"On the card. In Gus's book. The sign-out card." She was looking at nothing. "H. Langley, twelve, oh-three. The last name before mine. I've signed under it three times." Her voice had gone small. "December 2003. A month after. Somebody named H. Langley went to the library and took out Gus's book about how mediums fool people."

The wife who didn't know. Who found out.

Archie didn't answer. He was somewhere else: in a small warm house on Pudding Lane on Monday night, with a fat gray cat on his knees and fish chowder in his stomach, looking at a piano crowded with photographs. A boy on a dock, holding up a mackerel. *Danny. My son.* And she had handed him a velvet waistcoat over the cat's head and told him to try it on.

He didn't say it.

June was watching his face. He had never in his life been able to hide anything from her that mattered, and he watched her see it, and he watched her refuse it.

"You're thinking about Mrs. Tibbetts," she said.

"I didn't say anything."

"You didn't have to." She put the pencil down. "She's Tibbetts. Not Langley. There must be fifty women in this town whose name starts with H. Helen. Heather. Half of them are probably Coombses. And lots of people are named Danny, Dad." She was talking fast now, in her debate voice, building it. "Lots. It's a coincidence. It's a coincidence until it isn't, and you check it. The *Packet*'s on microfilm at the library. Deb opens at nine. There'll be an obituary. There'll be a — something. We check it."

He looked at his daughter across the table: sixteen, white to the lips, holding a dead woman's notebook in both hands.

"Lots of people are named Danny," he said.

"Right." She let out a breath. "Right."

"Go to bed, Junebug. You've got school. You've got a show."

She didn't move for a moment. Then she squared the three notebooks with a sharp tap against the table, set them by his elbow, and stood.

At the foot of the back stairs she stopped.

"We'll check it," she said. "In the morning. Both of us."

"Goodnight, June."

She looked at him a second longer than she needed to. Then she went up, and he heard her door close, and the house was quiet.

---

He didn't go up.

He sat at the farm table with the hatbox and the long row of notebooks with their yellow years, and the one book open at November. After a while he took off his grandfather's watch and wound it, slowly, all the way, the little crown clicking under his thumb, and put it back on. It was two o'clock. Then it was three.

The fog pressed white against the windows.

He kept his hand flat on the page, over the only plain words in thirty years, and sat there until it was time for the Galley to open.


---

# Chapter 33

## Blueberry or Apple

At ten minutes to five on Halloween morning the town wharf was black and wet and empty, and the fog was so thick that the lights of the Galley seemed to hang in it by themselves, with no building around them.

Archie knocked on the glass.

Bev Thibodeau was behind the counter with her back to him, sliding pies into the case. She turned, looked at him through the door, and pointed at the clock over the coffee urn. Then she went back to the pies.

He stood on the step in his buffalo-check coat with his hands in his pockets and waited. Out in the harbor, somewhere beyond the moorings, the bell buoy was ringing, slow and uneven, and a boat engine he couldn't see turned over and caught and went away toward it. At five o'clock exactly the bolt went back.

"Sit down," said Bev. "You're letting the fog in."

He sat on the stool at the end of the counter by the register. The Galley at five in the morning was a different room from the Galley at seven — chairs still upside down on the tables, the booths empty, the windows gray, the grill only beginning to tick as it heated. It smelled of coffee and hot sugar and the bleach she'd used on the floor last night. The radio on the shelf by the kitchen door was reading the marine forecast: *fog, visibility under one mile, seas two to four feet.*

Bev took the chairs down off the liars' table, one at a time, and set them around it at the angles they liked. She filled the urn. She poured a mug of coffee and put it in front of him without being asked, and then she looked at him properly for the first time, over her reading glasses, with the pot still in her hand.

"You've been up all night."

"Yes."

"You look it." She put the pot back on the warmer. "Drink that."

He drank some of it. It was too hot and too strong and exactly what he needed, and it didn't help.

"Your mother was out half the night herself," said Bev. "Ozzie Lund had her at the Lobster Pound in Lincolnville till all hours. Fried clams. Two forks on one whoopie pie." She set out the sugar. "Whole wharf'll know by six."

Archie looked at her.

"Ozzie," he said.

"Ozzie," said Bev. "Drink that."

He had driven down Shore Road in the dark with the whole thing laid out in his head, and somewhere on the way he had decided what he wanted. He wanted Bev to laugh at him. He wanted her to say *No, that's some other Langley, there were Langleys all up and down this coast,* and put a piece of pie in front of him and tell him he looked like something left on a mooring, and send him home to bed. He'd driven down here to be told he was wrong by the one person in Merrow who'd never once pretended he was right.

"Bev," he said. "Settle something for me."

"I'm opening."

"It's a small thing. A name."

She was wiping down the counter with a gray cloth, in long strokes. She didn't stop. "Go on."

"Langley," said Archie. "Peter Langley. He's on the memorial."

The cloth stopped.

Bev stood there with her hand flat on the counter. Then she folded the cloth into a square and set it down by the register.

"Langley?" she said. "That's Hollis."

He didn't say anything.

"Hollis Langley, she was, back when. Married Peter in — eighty-three, it would've been. Roland and I went." Bev picked the cloth up again and found nothing to do with it. "She kept Tibbetts at the school. Said she'd taught two hundred kids to spell it already and she wasn't starting over. Everybody else called her Langley. Then after Peter she went back to Tibbetts for all of it. Mail, bank, everything." She looked at him. "You didn't know that."

"No."

"No reason you would. It's twenty years."

"She had a boy."

"Danny." Bev said it the way you'd set down something heavy you'd carried a long way. "Up the quarry. August, oh-one. He was fifteen. Went off the high ledge with the other boys and didn't come up." She was quiet a moment. "Danny Langley ate a whole blueberry pie on that stool you're sitting on, when he was twelve, on a bet. Roland bet him he couldn't. Roland paid up, too. Five dollars."

Archie put both hands around the mug.

"And Peter—" he said.

"Two years after. Near enough. November." Bev's flat Down East voice didn't change, which was how he knew what it cost. "Took the *Hollis T.* out in a gale warning, middle of the afternoon, all by himself. Nobody knew why. Nobody ever did know why. She fetched up on Shag Ledge. They found him two days after." She looked past Archie, out the gray window, toward the place in the fog where the bell buoy was. "Hollis sat in that back booth every morning that winter. Wouldn't eat. I fed her anyhow."

The urn began to gurgle. Neither of them looked at it.

---

He had thought, at three in the morning at his own kitchen table, that it would feel like a trick coming clear — the moment in the second row when you finally see the thread. It didn't. It felt like a room he'd been living in for a week, filling up slowly with water, and everything in it was something he had already touched.

A letter to a television company in May, before he'd ever come back to Merrow. *Don't cancel her. Beat her. In Gus's house.* Page one-twelve, and three booths in this diner chanting the answer — Mrs. Tibbetts's séance unit, every kid in Merrow. A fire permit in a cardigan pocket, and candles lit by the one pair of hands allowed to strike a match on that stage. Teddy's voice from the wing, *Hollis, sorry, can you slide the horn left*, and a camera swinging away to the window for forty seconds. A boy on a dock with a mackerel. Shannon with a free reading in the summer and three more at three hundred and fifty dollars apiece; *Hollis already gave me the speech.* A four-year-old on a lap in a plywood booth, saying that Daddy said it was warm.

*Oh, sweetheart. The water's never warm here.*

He had heard it. He had stood on the step and heard it, and thought it was the kindest thing anybody had said in that building all week, and gone back down so as not to interrupt.

He looked up. Bev was watching him.

She hadn't asked a single question. She had been watching faces across this counter for forty years, and she had seen his, and she had heard the name he'd put next to it. That was all she had. It was enough. Her square, plain face held, and then, slowly, it gave way, and Archie watched Bev Thibodeau — who had never once sat down behind her own counter in his presence — reach for the stool by the register and sit on it.

"Archie," she said. "What is this."

He couldn't tell her. The words wouldn't come up past his chest.

Bev nodded, once, the way she nodded when somebody told her they were paying cash.

"All right," she said. "I don't want to know. I don't." She took off her reading glasses and pressed her thumb and finger into the corners of her eyes, and put the glasses back on. "But whatever it is, it goes to Tess Calloway. Not to you. Not to Dale. To Tess."

"I know."

"Today."

"I have to talk to Hollis first."

Bev's eyes narrowed. "Why."

"Because I owe her that." He heard how it sounded. He said it anyway. "Because she gave me the keys, Bev. In August. She brought me a pie and the keys to the opera house and told me I looked like I needed both. I'm not going to let her find out from a cruiser in her dooryard."

Out on the wharf, a truck door slammed, and then another.

"Show's tonight," said Bev.

"Seven o'clock."

"Done by ten?"

"About ten."

"Then at ten o'clock," said Bev, "I'm calling Tess myself. I don't care what it is. I'll give Hollis her show, if that's what this is. I'm not carrying it one minute past ten." She stood up off the stool. "You hear me?"

"I hear you."

She went to the pie case and slid the door open.

"Blueberry or apple."

"Bev, I'm not—"

"I didn't ask if you were hungry. There's two. Pick."

He looked at the pies. "Blueberry," he said.

She cut him a slice, a wide one, and put it on a plate in front of him with a fork, and went back to the urn. He sat looking at it. He didn't eat it.

---

The bell over the door rang at a quarter past five, and the cold came in, and the liars' table came in with it — Elwin Coombs first, then Dickie Thibodeau, then the long silent shape of Pickle, in their barn coats and their caps, stamping the fog off their boots. They went past the counter toward their round table by the kitchen door, and Bev was already pouring their coffee.

Elwin slowed beside the end stool. He looked at the man sitting there, at his gray face and his untouched pie, and he stopped.

"Morning, Archie," said Elwin Coombs.

It was the first time in sixty-six days that the liars' table had spoken to him. He had kept count of every one of them.

Archie was looking out the window, at the fog where the bell buoy was ringing, and he didn't hear it.


---

# Chapter 34

## The Trumpet Woman

Her father had kept everything on paper. The night her mother left, when Tess was nine, Walt Calloway had come home from the station and sat at the kitchen table and written it up on a yellow legal pad in his square block capitals — DATE. TIME. WHAT WAS SAID. — and folded it in thirds and put it in the drawer by the stove with the takeout menus. He had run the Merrow Police Department the same way for twenty-nine years, and every call and complaint had gone into the green steel cabinets in the basement of the town hall, one drawer per year, filed by the name of whoever had walked in the door to complain.

Not by the name of whoever they'd complained about. By complainant. Nobody at the State Police knew that. It had taken Tess until Thursday afternoon, driving back from the jail, to remember it herself, and Dale until Friday morning to find the key to the basement.

"Two thousand three," said Dale Pinkham at eleven o'clock on Friday morning, and hauled the drawer open with a shriek of rusted runners.

The basement smelled of damp stone and old paper and the oil furnace roaring on the other side of the wall. A single bulb hung over the cabinets. Dale had brought down two folding chairs from the select board room and a box of doughnuts from the Galley, and he'd eaten two of the doughnuts on the stairs.

"You want to tell me what we're looking for?"

"I'll know it when I see it."

"Ayuh. That's what your dad used to say." He handed her the first fat handful of manila folders. "He'd have been at this an hour ago."

She had believed the magician since Wednesday. She'd told him so on the phone that night, against orders, in a call she would never log. Believing him wasn't evidence. What she had was the lab report on the scarf — aconitine and lipstick together on a single fold, in a smear consistent with wiping something off, not putting something on — and what Margaret Okafor had told her on Thursday in the jail corridor, after Tess had passed Archie coming out with Ivy Crane's signature in his hand. Okafor wanted a third party more than she wanted lunch. *A man in Maine, Sergeant. Two thousand three. My client's mother put the trumpet away after him for years. Find him.*

A man in Maine. Two thousand three. Tess had pulled over on Route 1 with the engine running and thought about a drawer by a stove.

"Abbott," read Dale. "Dog bite. Ayuh, that's the Abbotts' shepherd, that dog bit everybody." He set it on the pile. "Ames. Burglary. Summer place out on the point, they lost a television." Another folder. "Bickford. Noise complaint." He looked up. "Your dad wrote down *noise complaints*?"

"He wrote down everything."

Dale had been a Knox County deputy for twenty years before he came to Merrow in 2010, the year her father retired. He knew the town's people now, but not its paper. He read every name aloud and said who they were, and was right about most of them, and Tess let him, because it helped to have someone talking.

At twenty past noon she found it.

It was a thin folder near the back of the drawer, with a strip of masking tape on the tab and two words in her father's hand: LANGLEY, H.

She sat with it closed on her knee. *Hollis Langley, Peter's wife.* That was what her mother had called her, and the women at church, back when Tess was small and Mrs. Tibbetts was Mrs. Tibbetts only at the high school, where she'd taught Tess to diagram a sentence and told her, in the eleventh grade, that she wrote like a person giving testimony and that it wasn't a compliment. And there had been a boy. Danny Langley, a grade ahead of her, who had played Puck in the Players' summer show the year Tess was twelve, and who drowned at the quarry the summer she turned fourteen.

She opened the folder.

Three sheets of yellow legal paper, written on both sides, and a photocopy. Her father's capitals were pressed so hard into the paper she could feel them from the back.

*DATE: 11/26/03. COMPLAINANT: HOLLIS LANGLEY, 14 PUDDING LANE. RE: HUSBAND PETER LANGLEY (DECEASED 11/18/03, LOST AT SEA, SHAG LEDGE).*

*COMPLAINANT STATES SHE HAS FOUND HUSBAND'S NOTEBOOK AND RECEIPTS SHOWING HE ATTENDED 41 PRIVATE SESSIONS 10/01–11/03 WITH A "SPIRITUALIST MEDIUM" CALLING HERSELF SISTER LORI CRANMER, PARK ST., ROCKLAND. TOTAL PAID $61,500. SECOND MORTGAGE TAKEN W/O COMPLAINANT'S KNOWLEDGE.*

*NOTEBOOK RECORDS "MESSAGES" FROM SON DANIEL (DECEASED 8/01) GIVEN THROUGH A "TRUMPET." INCLUDING: "DAD IT'S WARM HERE," "I'M NOT COLD ANYMORE," AND IN FINAL ENTRIES "COME SEE ME ON THE WATER."*

There was more. A call to the Rockland police: the room over the laundromat already empty, the landlord paid through December in cash. A call to an assistant attorney general in Augusta, whose name her father had underlined twice. And at the bottom of the last page, pressed so hard the pen had nearly gone through:

*NO STATUTE APPLIES PER AAG. SHE NEVER TOLD HIM TO GO OUT IN A STORM. NOT IN SO MANY WORDS. TOLD H.L. I AM SORRY. NOT FILED W/ STATE.*

The photocopy was of a flyer that had been pinned to a corkboard once; there were holes in the corners. MESSAGES FROM LOVED ONES, it said across the top, in a font trying to look like handwriting. SISTER LORI CRANMER, SPIRITUALIST. PRIVATE SITTINGS. Underneath was a woman in her thirties with dark hair and a dark mouth and steady, amused eyes.

There was no white streak yet. It didn't matter. Tess had looked at that face on the autopsy table on Sunday afternoon.

"Tess?" Dale had stopped reading names. "You all right?"

"Peter Langley," she said.

"Langley." He frowned at the furnace wall. "Went onto Shag Ledge in a gale, oh-three. I was on the county then. We got the call when they found him." He shook his head. "Never knew the wife."

"Yes, you do. It's Hollis Tibbetts, Dale."

He looked at her. His big face went slowly slack under the walrus mustache, and he set the folders in his hands down on the pile, very carefully, and didn't pick up another.

"Oh," he said. "Oh, no. Oh, Hollis."

---

She needed one more thing before she called Augusta, because Brannock was going to ask how she knew the woman in a twenty-two-year-old photocopy was the woman in the morgue, and *I looked at her* was not an answer a lieutenant accepted.

Sea View was four minutes up the hill.

"You just missed him," said Marisol at the nurses' station. "The magician. He was here at noon, same as always. He did a trick for Carol. She hasn't let go of it."

Carol Gilley was in her armchair by the window in a cardigan that was, for once, buttoned right, holding a single playing card in both hands. It was the queen of hearts. Across its face, in black marker, in someone else's handwriting, was the word WARREN. Every so often Carol turned it over to check the back, and then turned it face up again, as if to make sure the name was still there.

Tess stood in the doorway and watched that for longer than she meant to.

Her father was by the piano in his wheelchair, humming. He was in a good way today — the clear blue eyes, the big hands quiet on the arms of the chair. "Tessie."

"Hi, Dad." She pulled the footstool over and took the photocopy out of her coat, folded so only the photograph showed. "Do you know this woman?"

He took it. His hands shook and the paper shook with them. He held it a long way from his face and looked at it until she started to think he'd gone back to 1988 and wouldn't be coming out today.

"That's the trumpet woman," said Walt Calloway.

Tess didn't move.

"Hollis's woman." He tapped the photograph with one big finger. "Hollis came to see me about her. Sat in my office and cried. Hollis Langley never cried in her life, Tessie, not even at the boy's funeral. I couldn't do a thing." His face clouded. "Nothing to charge. I told her I was sorry. I told her—"

"I know, Dad. You wrote it down."

"I wrote it down." He nodded, satisfied, and handed the paper back. Then he looked at her properly, and smiled, and she saw him leave 2003 for somewhere softer.

"You made me mail that letter," he said.

"What letter?"

"To the magic fella. On the TV. You were twelve. You wrote him how he did the lighthouse trick and you made me put two stamps on it, so it'd get there faster." He laughed, deep in his chest. "Two stamps. I told you that's not how stamps work."

"Dad."

"He wrote you back, too. You remember? You kept it in your—"

"I remember," said Tess.

She did. A glossy eight-by-ten of a skinny young man in a tuxedo, signed across the bottom, and on the back, in black felt-tip, in a hand that leaned forward as if it were in a hurry: *You're right. Don't tell anyone. — A.F.* It was in a shoebox on the top shelf of her closet on Pound Hill, under her diploma. She had never told anyone.

She kissed the top of his head and went out onto the long front porch, where the rocking chairs sat empty in the cold and the whole bay lay below in the hard afternoon light. She stood at the rail until her face was her own again. Then she called her lieutenant.

Brannock listened to all of it without interrupting, which was how she knew he was taking it seriously.

"A twenty-two-year-old complaint," he said, "a flyer, and an Alzheimer's patient."

"And the forty seconds. She slid it, at the producer's request, in gloves, in the one gap in the footage. She's been on our own list since Sunday. We just didn't have a reason."

She could hear him breathing through his nose.

"Write me the affidavit," he said. "House and outbuildings. I'll walk it to the judge myself. You don't have probable cause to arrest her on this and you know it, so nobody goes near her until we've been through that shed. Where will she be tonight?"

"The opera house. She's running the lights for the show."

"Of course she is." A pause. "Bissonnette in plain clothes in the hall from five. A car on Main Street. If the search gives us anything, we take her at intermission, quietly, out of sight of three hundred people. Not before." Then, in a different voice: "And Calloway. Your magician. He had the daughter sign something. Does he know?"

"I don't know what he knows."

"Find out," said Brannock, and hung up.

She drove down the hill at a quarter past three with the affidavit already half-written in her head. At the bottom, Elm Street went left to the town hall. She went right, onto Pudding Lane.

It was a short street of small old houses with picket fences. Halfway down, in front of a weathered Cape with a blue door and a big front border dug over to bare earth, a long black 1958 Cadillac hearse was parked at the curb with THE GREAT FAIRWEATHER — MARVELS & MYSTERIES in gold letters on its side.

Tess didn't slow down. She didn't look at the house. She drove to the end of Pudding Lane and turned onto Water Street and pulled over by the inn and sat there with both hands on the wheel.

He knew. He'd had Ivy Crane's release in his hand on Thursday morning, and he knew, and he'd gone to her alone.

He hadn't called.

She sat with the engine running and the heater on and the affidavit unwritten, watching the gulls work the pier, until twenty minutes to four, when her phone buzzed on the seat beside her.

It was a text from a number she'd never saved and knew anyway. Two words.

*Intermission. Please.*


---

# Chapter 35

## Pudding Lane

He had not been able to ask her all morning, because all morning she had been surrounded.

By eight o'clock on Halloween the opera house was full of people: Mattie Pinkham and three of her cousins on ladders hanging the new lights, Deb Coombs hemming a curtain, the liars' table in a row of folding chairs at the back offering opinions about the ladders. And in the middle of it, with her clipboard and her ring of keys and her reading glasses swinging on their beaded chain, Hollis Tibbetts, calling cues up to the booth and sending Corey from the Galley to the hardware store for gaffer tape. Archie stood on a ladder beside her for an hour, handing up cable.

"Your tails need pressing," she told him, from the rung below, "and your hair needs cutting. I'll do the first. I wouldn't touch the second for a hundred dollars." She held up her hand for the next coil. "Elwin, if Gus owed your father three dollars, take it up with Gus."

Every time she laughed, it went into him like a splinter.

At noon he went up to Sea View, because it was Friday and he always went on Friday, and did a trick for Carol Gilley, and sat with Walt Calloway through the story about Gus and the police cruiser, and laughed in the right place. At half past two Hollis went home to finish June's costume.

At three o'clock he parked the hearse on Pudding Lane.

---

She was in the garden.

The house was what it always was: a weathered gray Cape with a blue door and a picket fence, books in every window, a dress form wearing half a Victorian bodice behind the glass of the front room. The long border along the fence, where the tall blue flowers had stood in September, had been dug over its whole length to bare brown earth. Only a row of dahlias was left at the end of it, their stalks blackened by frost. Hollis was on her knees beside them on a folded feed sack, in her men's cardigan and an old felt hat, lifting the tubers with a garden fork one clump at a time and laying them on newspaper to dry.

Malvolio sat on the roof of the potting shed in the last of the sun, watching her do it.

She looked up when the gate creaked, and smiled.

"You're early. June's jacket's on the dress form; I've only the hem to finish." She sat back on her heels. "I put the kettle on when I saw that hearse. Did you want tea?"

"No. Thank you."

She looked at him a second longer than she needed to. Then she went back to the dahlias. The fork went in under the next clump, and the tubers came up out of the cold ground in a fat muddy knot, and she shook the dirt off them and laid them on the paper.

"You have to get them up before the ground freezes," she said, "or they rot. Everybody thinks dahlias are tough. They're the most delicate things in the garden." She reached for the next one. "Peter used to say I fussed over them more than over him."

Archie stood at the edge of the dug bed with his hands in the pockets of his buffalo-check coat. Driving over, he had worked out a dozen ways to do this. He could come at it sideways — a little warmth, a little talk, a question she wouldn't see the point of until it had already landed. He'd been doing it to strangers for thirty years.

He didn't want to do it to her.

"Hollis," he said. "What was your husband's name?"

The fork stopped in the ground.

She didn't look up right away. She looked at the dahlias, and at her own gloved hands on the handle, and then she sat back on the feed sack and looked at him the way she might have looked at a student who had finally, at the end of the year, read the book.

"Peter," she said.

"Peter Langley."

"Peter Langley." She nodded. "I was Hollis Langley for twenty years, everywhere but at the school. People forget that."

"Did Peter go to Rockland on Thursday nights, Hollis?"

She didn't answer that.

She pulled the fork out of the ground and laid it across the newspaper so its tines wouldn't touch the tubers. She took off her garden gloves and laid them on the fork. Then she got up off the feed sack, which took her two tries, and Archie didn't help her because she wouldn't have wanted him to.

"How did you know?" she said. She sounded curious.

"The library card. In Gus's book. *H. Langley, December 2003.*"

"Deb and her cards." Hollis shook her head. "Forty years she's been told to throw those out." She looked at the house. "Wait here."

She went in. The blue door closed behind her. Archie stood in the garden with the bare bed and the black dahlia stalks and the cat watching him from the shed roof, and the sun went behind the roofs across the lane, and the cold came up out of the ground all at once.

She came back out with a long white envelope under her arm and a mug of tea in each hand.

"The kettle was on anyway," she said, and held one out to him.

It was chamomile. He could smell it from where he stood: hay and honey, steam going up off it into the cold. The mug said MERROW PLAYERS — *TWELFTH NIGHT* 1997 and had a chip in the rim. Thirty feet behind her, on the roof of the potting shed, Malvolio yawned.

He knew what she was. He had known it at five o'clock that morning on a stool in the Galley. He knew what she had done with her hands, and how little time it had taken her, and that nobody had seen.

He took the mug and drank.

It was too hot, and too sweet. Hollis watched him over the rim of her own. He saw her see it — that he knew exactly what she was, and had taken a cup from her hand anyway — and her mouth moved once at the corner, and was still.

Neither of them said a word about it.

She set her own mug on the fence post and held out the envelope. It was sealed. Across the front, in a teacher's upright, unmistakable hand, it said *Det. Sgt. Theresa Calloway — by hand*.

"I wrote it last night," she said. "When they charged that girl with murder. It's all in there. What I did and how, and where the rest of it is, and that Ivy Crane didn't know a thing about it. I'd have brought it to Tess myself tonight, after." She looked at it. "I'd have liked to tell Vivienne first. I don't suppose that's possible now."

Archie didn't take it.

"Why?" he said. "Hollis. *Why?*"

"It's in there."

"I don't want to read it in a letter."

"No. I don't suppose you do." She was quiet a moment. "You'll get it, Archie. All of it. I'll tell you myself — not like this, in a garden, with the dahlias half up. Afterward."

"After what?"

"After the show."

He stared at her.

"Gus's show. Tonight. His night." Her voice was perfectly steady. "I've run the lights in that building for thirty years. I ran them for him at the end, when old Mr. Thibodeau's hands got too bad for the board — I was twenty-nine, and he let me run the board for the Empty Chair. I've never once missed a show. Not when Danny—" She stopped. "Not ever."

"Hollis—"

"I'm not going anywhere. Where would I go? I'm sixty-six years old and I've got a cat." She was still holding out the envelope. "Take it. If I don't come down out of that booth tonight, it goes straight to Tess. And if I do, it goes to her anyway, and I'll go with it. Either way. Tonight. You have my word."

He thought about Bev at five in the morning, saying *Archie* in that voice. He thought about Ivy Crane in Knox County Jail, who couldn't get out on a Friday night whatever anybody did, and who would walk out tomorrow morning if this envelope said what Hollis said it did. He thought about three hundred people, his grandfather's name on the poster, and two hundred and eighty light cues that lived in this woman's head and in a coffee-stained cue book in a drawer.

And in nine weeks she had never once lied to him. He hated himself for counting that.

He took the envelope.

"Thank you," said Hollis.

"I'm going to tell Tess," he said. "That I know. I'm not going to hide it from her."

"No. You shouldn't." Her face softened. "You're a terrible liar, you know. To people you're fond of. Vivienne says you always have been. It's the only thing about you that isn't a trick."

She went back into the house, and when she came out this time she was carrying a jacket over her arm: dark blue velvet, cut down small, with silver frogging across the front and a high collar. It had been one of Gus's. Archie recognized it from a photograph in the turret.

"For June," said Hollis. "The hem's done. I finished it while you were standing there working out how to ask me." She laid it in his arms on top of the envelope. "She'll look like a queen in it. Tell her to stand up straight."

The jacket was heavy, and warm from being indoors, and smelled faintly of cedar and of Hollis's house.

She picked up her fork and her gloves and looked down at the last of the dahlias still in the ground, and left them.

"Go on," she said. "You've got a show to do, and so have I." She touched his arm once, lightly, and went toward the house with the fork over her shoulder.

At the blue door she looked back.

"Break a leg, Archie," said Hollis Tibbetts, and went inside.

---

He sat in the hearse at the curb with the velvet jacket on the passenger seat and the envelope in the inside pocket of his coat, where he could feel its edge every time he breathed.

He wrote *Tess, I know who* and deleted it. He wrote *Tess, please come to the show* and deleted that too. Across the fence, Malvolio stood up on the shed roof, stretched, and jumped down out of sight.

He typed two words and sent them.

Then he started the hearse and drove to the opera house, where the lights were already on.


---

# Chapter 36

## The Great Fairweather

By a quarter to seven there were three hundred and eleven people in the Merrow Opera House, which was eleven more than the fire marshal allowed and roughly a fifth of everybody who lived in the town.

Archie watched them come in through a gap in the stage-left curtain, which Gus had taught him never to do. The black drapes were gone from the windows now, and the tall arched panes were full of the dark and, far down in it, the lights of the moorings. The hall was warm for once — three hundred bodies and the new radiator valves — and loud, and it smelled of wet wool and popcorn and the cider Bev had brought up in two enormous coffee urns and was selling at the top of the stairs for a dollar a cup, for the roof.

Half the children were in costume: three ghosts, a pirate, a very small hot dog, and Rosie Mayo in the fourth row on her mother's lap, dressed as a lobster in a red felt suit with claws that went past her hands. Beside them Liam wore a black cape and a mustache drawn on with eyeliner, and Archie understood, with a feeling in his chest he didn't have time for, that Liam had come as him.

In the front row, by their own insistence, sat the liars' table — Elwin Coombs, Dickie Thibodeau and Pickle, side by side in their good coats with their arms folded, in the attitude of men who had come to see a boat launched and would believe it floated when it floated. Warren Gilley was in the sixth row on the aisle in his bow tie, and beside him, in a wheelchair with a pass from Sea View pinned to her coat, was Carol, holding a playing card in both hands. Kev Duchesne had a camera on a little tripod in the balcony and a lanyard that said, in Hollis's handwriting, PRESS — APPROVED — BEHAVE.

At the very back of the hall, against the wall by the stairs in a gray parka, stood Kayla Bissonnette — not in uniform, and not watching the stage.

Archie's shoulders came down for the first time since three o'clock. Stepping out the stage door at half past five for air, he'd seen the Merrow PD cruiser parked up on Main Street with its lights off and somebody in it. Now here was Kayla, twenty feet below the booth, with the balcony stairs at her elbow. Tess had read his text. It was Tess's now, and the woman in the booth was watched.

Above Kayla, up in the horseshoe of the balcony, in the little plywood booth with its window full of soft amber light, a small square shape in a cardigan was bent over the dimmer board.

He let the curtain fall closed.

The envelope was in the inside pocket of his tailcoat, under the opera cloak. He could feel its edge when he breathed.

"Places," said Lenny behind him.

Lenny was in a headset over his sweaters, holding a clipboard he didn't need. Behind him in the wing stood June, in Gus's dark blue velvet jacket with the silver frogging, her hair up, her face white. Hollis had been right; she looked like a queen. Beside her, Vivienne, in black with diamonds that might even have been real, was doing breathing exercises from 1962.

"Junebug," Archie said.

"Don't," said June. "I'll throw up."

"You look—"

"*Dad.*"

He went out.

---

The house went dark. The talk died in a wave from the back, people hushing each other, a child saying *is it starting* and being shushed.

Then a single narrow spot from high at the back of the hall, from the booth, found the painted harbor curtain, and the curtain rose, and there, alone on the bare stage in the circle of light, a silver ball the size of a grapefruit was hanging in the empty air.

Nobody had seen it arrive. It was simply there, shining, turning slowly, four feet above the boards.

The hall made the sound Archie had spent his life working for. It isn't a gasp. It's quieter than that — three hundred people breathing in at the same time.

He walked out of the dark at the side of the stage in Gus's cloak, and the spot widened to take him in, and he did not look at the audience. He looked at the ball.

"My grandfather opened every show on this stage with this," he said. "From 1949 to 1988. He called it the Silver Moon."

He held a square of white silk up in front of the ball by two corners, and the ball rose behind the silk and settled against it, glowing through the fabric. When he walked, it came with him. He turned a slow circle and it went around him, under his arm, over his shoulder, along the line of his outstretched hand like a cat along a fence. He went down to the front of the stage and it went ahead of him, out over the footlights, over the front row, and hung there above the liars' table.

Elwin Coombs looked up at it with his mouth slightly open. Dickie Thibodeau leaned back in his chair to get away from it. Pickle, who had not spoken one word in Archie's hearing in nine weeks, slowly raised one finger and pointed at it.

Archie drew the silk away. The ball hung above Pickle's head by itself.

Then it drifted back across the footlights, slowly, like something tired coming home, and settled in his open palm, and was a ball. He tossed it once and caught it.

The applause began at the back and rolled forward and broke over the stage. Archie Fairweather had stood in front of thirty thousand people at a time and heard every kind of applause there was, and this one went straight through him.

For the next forty minutes he forgot about the envelope.

The room took him. Three hundred people from one small harbor town, in their good coats and their children's costumes, wanting to be amazed, and he amazed them, because it was the one thing in the world he was sure he could do.

He did the linking rings with Deb Coombs, who refused to be impressed and then gasped out loud when the ring in her own hands linked through his. He swallowed a packet of needles and a length of thread and drew them out threaded, which made Bev, at the top of the stairs with her cider, say "Oh, for crying out *loud*" clearly enough to be heard on the stage. He borrowed Warren Gilley's wristwatch — asked for it, with a bow; no lifting tonight — and smashed it to pieces in a cloth bag with a hammer, and Warren went red to the tips of his ears, and Carol laughed out loud in her wheelchair, the clear delighted laugh of a girl at a show. Then Archie asked Carol to turn over the card she'd been holding all evening — the queen of hearts with WARREN across it — and Warren's watch was taped to the back of it, ticking.

Warren looked at the watch. Then he looked at his wife, who was holding the card up and showing it to the people around her, beaming. He put his hand over his eyes.

Archie went down to the front row and stood in front of the liars' table.

"Gentlemen," he said. "I'm going to need one of you."

Elwin and Dickie both looked at Pickle. Pickle looked at the ceiling.

"Sir." Archie held out a deck of cards, face down, fanned. "You don't have to touch them. You don't have to take one. Just look at the backs and think of any card in the deck. Don't tell me."

Pickle looked at the cards. Then he looked at Archie. Then, slowly, with the whole hall watching, he nodded.

"Now," said Archie, "for the first time in my presence, and I'm told for the first time in public in living memory — would you please stand up and tell everyone which card you're thinking of?"

The hall went silent. People at the back stood up to see.

Pickle unfolded himself from his chair. He was very tall standing up. He turned around and looked at the three hundred people of Merrow, Maine, every one of whom had known him all his life.

"Seven of hearts," he said, in a deep, rusty voice. And then, after a moment: "And my name's Lionel."

The opera house came apart.

It took Archie a full minute to get them quiet enough to tell them to look under their seats. Every seat in the house — all three hundred and eleven, and the folding chairs in the balcony, and Bev's stool by the cider urns — had a playing card taped underneath it. Every one of them was the seven of hearts.

Lionel looked at the card under his own seat, and at Archie, and sat back down, and folded his arms, and Archie could have sworn that under the gray stubble the man was very slightly smiling.

That was when he saw Tess.

She had come in at some point and was standing at the back by the stair door, a little apart from Kayla, in the charcoal coat with her braid over one shoulder. She wasn't laughing. She wasn't clapping. She was watching him — not the cards, not the crowd, but him — as if she were trying to work out how he did it.

He held her eyes for a second over three hundred heads. She didn't look away.

Then he went back up the steps to the stage, because there was one more number before the intermission, and it was Gus's, and the envelope was back against his chest.

---

The last number of the first act was the Sparrows.

It was the simplest thing in the show. Gus had closed his first half with it for forty years: a bare stage, a single light, the magician in his cloak, and out of the empty air of his hands, one by one, white doves. The cue was in the cue book in Hollis's handwriting, underlined: on the fourth bird, the stage goes to deep blue, and a single pin spot finds the magician's hands, and the doves fly up out of the light into the dark of the hall and settle on the balcony rail.

Archie produced the first dove from an empty silk, and the house sighed. The second from the collar of the cloak. The third from the air, bare-handed, in a flurry of white that drew a cry from somewhere at the back.

He produced the fourth, and lifted his hands, and waited for the blue.

The blue didn't come.

The stage stayed in its warm gold wash. The pin spot didn't find his hands. The light just held — steady, ordinary, wrong — the way stage lights hold when nobody is touching the board.

The doves went up out of his hands anyway, because doves don't wait for cues. They flew out over the heads of the audience in a white clatter of wings, and the people of Merrow turned in their seats to watch them, and three of them landed on the balcony rail as they'd been taught.

The fourth flew higher, and landed on the roof of the light booth.

The booth window was dark.

Archie stood at the center of the stage in the wrong light with his empty hands still raised. Out in the hall they were applauding — they didn't know; it had been beautiful anyway — and he lowered his hands and bowed, and smiled, and said something about a short intermission and Bev's cider and the roof.

In the stage-left wing, Lenny had already pulled off his headset. Archie watched him go through the side door to the balcony stairs faster than he had seen him move in thirty years.

Somebody out front found the house lights and threw them. The hall came up bright and loud and full of people standing and stretching and talking about the seven of hearts.

Archie walked off into the wing. He stood in the dimness by the rope rail in his grandfather's cloak, with the envelope against his chest, and waited.

It was a minute and a half. He counted.

Then Lenny came back through the side door, breathing hard, his reading glasses pushed up on his bald head, and crossed the wing and held out a small folded square of paper torn from the back of a cue book.

"It's for you," said Lenny.


---

# Chapter 37

## Intermission

The note was four lines in pencil, on the back of a page torn from the cue book, in the same upright hand as the envelope in his pocket.

*Archie —*
*Thank you for the lights. I'm sorry about the second half.*
*Ivy Crane didn't do it. You know that.*
*— H.*

He read it twice. Then he turned it over. On the other side, in Hollis's writing from some earlier year, faded, it said *Q.142 — blue to pin on 4th bird — GO*.

"The booth's empty," said Lenny. "Board's on the act-one preset. Her hat's gone. The fire-escape door at the end of the balcony's propped open." He paused. "The one the fire marshal made her unlock."

The stage-left wing was a narrow dim space between the back wall and the edge of the curtain, crowded with Lenny's rope rail and a prop table and the Empty Chair itself — a plain bentwood chair with a white sheet folded over its back, waiting for the finale. Through the curtain came the noise of the hall, three hundred people in the aisles with their cider. Somebody was laughing about Lionel.

"Dad." June was at his elbow in the blue velvet jacket. Her eyes went from his face to the note in his hand. "What's wrong?"

He didn't answer, and he saw her see that he didn't.

The side door to the balcony stairs banged open, and Tess Calloway came through it fast with Kayla Bissonnette behind her, a folded legal document in one hand and her phone in the other.

"Where is she?"

"Gone." He held out the note. "Out the fire escape. I don't know where."

Tess took the note and read it. Kayla had gone white to the lips. "I had the balcony stairs, Sarge. The whole time. She never came down them, I swear—"

"I know. It's not on you." Tess didn't look away from Archie. "The balcony fire door is supposed to be locked."

"Fire marshal made her unlock it on Thursday," said Lenny. "For the show. She grumbled."

Tess closed her eyes for one second.

"We executed the warrant at six-forty," she said, quietly. "Her house and her shed. There's a blue glass jar on the second shelf of that potting shed with a few ounces of something brown in it, and a pair of black cotton gloves rolled up beside it like socks, and the evidence tech wouldn't let any of us through the door until he'd put his own gloves on." Her voice was level. "We were going to take her at intermission. Quietly. Out the back." She looked at the note. "You knew. When you sent me that text, you knew."

"Yes."

"*Intermission. Please.*" She said it flatly. "You gave me a riddle."

"I gave you the time. She asked me for the first half. Gus's half. She said she'd come down out of that booth and go with you after—"

"She *asked* you."

"She gave me this." He took the envelope out of his tailcoat. *Det. Sgt. Theresa Calloway — by hand.* "She wrote it last night, when they charged Ivy. She said it was everything. She said it went to you tonight, either way."

Tess looked at the envelope without taking it. Then she took it, and put it inside her coat unopened.

"Dad," said June. "Who's *she*?"

He looked at his daughter in her great-grandfather's jacket, with her hair up and her face white. At seven that morning, at breakfast, she had asked him, *Did you check the Danny thing?* and he'd said *I'm looking into it*, and handed her a piece of toast, and driven her to school.

"Hollis," he said.

June's face didn't change. She had sat at the kitchen table half the night with him, reading the same tiny handwriting in the same small books. Under *lots of people are named Danny*, he saw now, she had known. She'd let him send her to bed, because he was her father and she'd thought he would tell her.

"You knew this morning," June said.

"Yes."

"And you didn't—" She stopped.

Vivienne's phone rang.

His mother was in the wing behind June, in her black and her diamonds, and she'd heard all of it. She looked at the screen and answered. "Ozzie," she said, and listened, and her free hand went to her throat.

"Ozzie's on the wharf," she said. "For the children and their silly dare. He says somebody just took Elwin's skiff off the town float. No lights. Heading out." She swallowed. "Toward the bell."

Out past the end of the point. Shag Ledge.

Tess already had her phone to her ear. "This is Calloway. I need Marine Patrol and Coast Guard Station Rockland to Merrow Harbor, a fourteen-foot skiff, one person aboard, no lights, heading for the bell off Shag Ledge—" She was moving toward the stairs. At the side door she turned and pointed at Archie.

"You stay here," she said.

"Tess—"

"You *stay here.* This is mine now. It's my job, and it's my town, and she was my teacher, and you've done enough." She looked past him at June, and at the chair with the sheet over its back, and her voice changed slightly. "You've got a show."

She went down the stairs. Kayla went with her.

Archie stood in the wing.

In twelve minutes Lenny would flash the house lights and the second half would start, and at the end of it the stage would go dark and a single chair would stand in a single spotlight, and June would come out in Gus's jacket and say the words he'd taught her on Wednesday night and pull the sheet. He had promised he'd be standing beside that chair when she did. Before that he'd promised her a real year in a real place. Before that, without saying it out loud, he had promised her he would never again walk off a stage and leave her in the audience wondering where he'd gone.

And out there in the fog, a woman he loved was sitting in a skiff with no lights at the place where her husband had drowned.

He couldn't do both. There wasn't a trick for it.

He turned to June, and he didn't put anything over his face. No patter, no grin. He let her watch him choose.

"I'm going," he said. "I'm breaking my promise. I'm sorry, June. I'm going after her."

June stood in front of the Empty Chair with her hands in fists at her sides. Her eyes were wet, and furious, and steady.

"I'm still mad at you," she said. "I'm going to be mad at you for a really long time." Her voice shook. She kept going. "But you know what she's going to do. You know better than anybody. So go."

"The Chair—"

"I know how to do the Chair. You taught me. All of it." She lifted her chin. "Grandma's in the chair anyway. You were only going to stand there and look surprised."

"Darling," said Vivienne, behind her, in her stage voice — and then, in her real one: "I can do the second half in my sleep. I was your grandfather's assistant for twelve years. I know every word of his patter; I wrote half of it. And Lenny built everything else."

"Mattie has the cue book," said Lenny. "Hollis showed her the board. Thursday." He looked at Archie from under his enormous white eyebrows. "Go."

Archie bent and kissed the top of June's head, and she let him. Then he went through the side door and down the back stairs two at a time, with his grandfather's opera cloak flying out behind him over his tails.

Tess was at the bottom of the stairs, at the stage door, on her phone. She looked up at him coming down.

She opened her mouth.

Then she shut it, and turned, and shoved the stage door open into the alley, and held it for him.

The cold came in off the harbor like a wall. The fog had come up while they were inside; it hung in the alley and in the street beyond, thick and gray and glowing under the streetlights, and from somewhere far out past the end of the point came the bell buoy off Shag Ledge, ringing and ringing.

They ran.


---

# Chapter 38

## The Bell Buoy

Ozzie Lund had the engine running.

The harbormaster's boat was a twenty-one-foot aluminum workboat with a little wheelhouse, a blue light on a mast, and HARBORMASTER — MERROW stenciled in white down the side. Ozzie stood at the wheel at the bottom of the fuel-dock ladder in a float coat and a watch cap, with the spotlight already lit and swinging. He was seventy and slow-talking, and he had courted Archie's mother since September with a thermos of coffee and a seriousness Archie had found hilarious right up until this moment, when he came down the ladder behind Tess and Ozzie put out one big hand and steadied him onto the deck without a word.

"Vivienne called," Ozzie said, and shoved the throttle forward.

The boat came off the dock with its bow up and its stern dug in, and the wharf lights went away behind them into the fog in about four seconds.

Then there was nothing.

Archie had been out on this harbor at dawn on Monday in Dale Pinkham's boat, with the light coming up gold and Merrow Light standing white on its point and every mooring visible for a mile. It was a different place now. The spotlight on the wheelhouse roof made a white cone in front of them, full of swirling wet gray, and showed them nothing but more of it. Beyond the cone there was no harbor and no town. There was the throb of the engine and the slap of the hull and, out ahead somewhere, the bell.

*Clang.*

The swell had come up while they were inside the opera house. The boat climbed it and fell off the back, climbed and fell. Archie held the edge of the wheelhouse with both hands and felt the old seasickness rise and told it, firmly, that he did not have time.

Tess was on her radio. "Marine Patrol, Calloway. Outbound past the moorings, heading for the bell off Shag. Visibility maybe fifty feet." She listened. "Copy." She lowered the radio. "Coast Guard's coming out of Rockland. Twenty minutes in this."

"Ayuh," said Ozzie. "Won't need twenty."

*Clang.* Closer.

Archie stood braced in the stern in Gus's opera cloak and his tailcoat and his good shoes, with salt water coming over the side in sheets and soaking him to the knees. He was thinking about an old wooden skiff, thirty-seven years ago, drifting near Merrow Light at dawn with nobody in it and a black opera cloak folded on the seat.

"There," said Ozzie, and swung the spotlight.

The bell buoy loomed out of the fog: a rust-red iron cage on a float, taller than a man, rolling heavily, its bell clanging inside it. Beyond it, so close that Archie's stomach turned over for a different reason, a long black hump of rock rose out of the water with white foam breaking over it and the swell sucking back off it with a sound like a breath. Shag Ledge.

And between the bell and the ledge, rising and falling, a small open skiff with its outboard tipped up out of the water.

Hollis was sitting in the stern.

She was in her men's cardigan and the old felt hat she'd worn in the garden, with no life jacket, sitting upright with her hands folded in her lap, facing the ledge. When the spotlight hit her she didn't turn around. She lifted one hand against the glare and then put it back in her lap.

Ozzie throttled down and brought the workboat around in a wide, careful arc until it lay off the skiff's side, between her and open water, about twenty feet away, rolling. He kept the engine just ticking over. He had been pulling people off this ledge for forty years.

"Hollis," said Archie.

She turned her head then and looked at him across the dark water, and he saw her see the cloak.

"Oh, Archie," she said. "You were supposed to be doing the second half."

"June's doing it."

Her mouth tightened, and then eased. "Is she," said Hollis softly. "Good for June."

The swell lifted the skiff and set it down. Each time, it came down a little closer to the rocks.

"I don't have a trick," Archie said. He had to raise his voice over the bell and the engine and the sea, and he hated that; he wanted to say this quietly. "I want you to know that. I've been trying to think of one the whole way out here and I don't have one. I've just got me."

"I know, dear. You always have." She looked back toward the ledge. "That's the trouble."

"Hollis, come on board."

"Do you know what she said to me?" Hollis was still looking at the rocks. "On Thursday. The first day. I took her around the building — the stairs, the green room, the booth. I showed her Gus's trapdoor. And at the end of it she put her hand on my arm, and she smiled at me, and she said, *Honey, where's the ladies' room?*" Her voice didn't change at all. "She'd sat across a table from my husband forty-one times. She'd talked to him in my son's voice. She'd taken our house. And she stood in Gus's theater and looked me in the face and asked me where the ladies' room was." She was quiet a moment. "She didn't remember the town, Archie. She didn't remember *any* of us."

Archie held on to the wheelhouse. The boat rolled. Behind him Tess was on the radio, low, giving their position.

He thought about a hatbox on a kitchen table at two o'clock in the morning, and thirty small books in cramped private shorthand, and his daughter's finger moving down a page.

"She remembered Peter," he said.

Hollis turned her head.

"She wrote him down," Archie said. "In her book. She kept a book for every year — every sitter, every Thursday, every dollar. Ivy kept them all. I read them last night." He made himself keep his voice steady. "November 2003. There's an entry. *P.L. lost at sea. Saw it in the paper.* And under that, in the same handwriting, it says, *God forgive me.*" He swallowed salt. "And then she put the trumpet away. She didn't use it for a private sitting again for three years. Her fans have a name for it. She remembered him, Hollis. She remembered Peter."

Hollis didn't move.

Then her shoulders came down. Her folded hands came apart in her lap. She looked at him across twenty feet of black water with a face he had never seen on her in nine weeks — a face with no teacher left in it at all.

"Oh," said Hollis Tibbetts. "Oh, Peter."

She stood up in the skiff.

"Hollis, *sit down*—" That was Ozzie, sharp, the first time Archie had ever heard him raise his voice. He had the orange ring buoy off its bracket, and he threw it, a flat hard throw that landed right against the skiff's side with its line paying out from the cleat.

Hollis looked down at it. She didn't reach for it.

And then the swell came.

It was bigger than the others. Archie saw it come out of the fog behind the skiff, a long black shoulder of water, and the skiff rose with it, and rose, with Hollis standing in the stern holding nothing, and at the top of it the skiff swung broadside and the sea dropped out from under it and set it down hard on the edge of the ledge with a crack that carried over the engine.

The skiff went over on its side. Hollis went into the water.

She went under at once. Archie saw the felt hat floating, and the white wash breaking over the rock, and nothing else.

His hands had the silver clasp of the opera cloak open before he knew they'd moved. They had the cloak off his shoulders and folded — lining out, shoulders in, thirds, collar on top, the clasp centered — and laid down on the seat beside the wheel, and he was kicking off one good shoe and then the other.

"Archie—" Tess, behind him. "Don't."

He looked at her. She had the radio in one hand and her other hand out toward him, and she knew exactly what he was going to do, and so did he.

He decided.

"Line," he said to Ozzie. "Around my waist. I don't go in without a line. Never have."

Ozzie looked at him for one second. Then he had the end of a coil of rope around Archie's middle and knotted in a bowline in about the time it took to say it, and he braced his boots against the gunwale and took the strain.

Archie went over the side.

The cold was the worst thing he had ever felt.

He'd known it would be. He had done ice-water tanks for television; he knew that the first thing cold water does is try to make you breathe, and if your head is under when it happens, you breathe in the sea. None of that had been forty-seven years old in fifty-two-degree water at night in a swell.

He came up gasping anyway, the cold like a band of iron around his ribs, and got his breath back by main force — *slow, slow, slow* — and kicked toward the hat.

She came up beside the rock, an arm's length from him, her white hair plastered to her head.

He got hold of her cardigan. He got an arm around her.

And for ten seconds — he counted them; he always counted — she fought him.

It wasn't much. She was sixty-six and the cold had her too. But she twisted in his arm and pushed at him with her hands flat against his chest, pushing him away, toward the boat — and he understood, with the sea in his mouth and the ledge looming, that she was not fighting to stay. She was trying to make *him* go. She was pushing him back to the line, and the boat, and his daughter in a blue velvet jacket on a stage, and she was going to keep pushing until one of them let go.

"*Hollis,*" he said into her ear, with what breath he had. "Hold on. I'm not letting go. So you have to."

The swell lifted them both.

Five more seconds. He felt her stop.

Then her hands, which had been pushing against his chest, closed into fists in the wet front of his tailcoat, and held on.

"Pull!" Archie shouted, to nobody, to the fog, and the line around his waist went bar-tight and dragged them both back across the black water toward the light, and a hand came down out of the glare and closed hard on the collar of his coat, and held, and did not let go — and he looked up through salt and fog and saw Tess Calloway leaning half out over the gunwale with her braid in the sea and her teeth gritted, hauling.


---

# Chapter 39

## Harbor Blanket

By the time Ozzie brought the workboat in against the fuel dock, half of Merrow was on the wharf.

They had come down from the opera house at intermission, because word in Merrow traveled faster than the people carrying it, and they stood under the floodlights at the head of the wharf in their good coats and their children's costumes, three deep along the railing by the Galley: Bev in her apron with a pitcher of cider still in her hand; Elwin and Dickie and Lionel; Warren Gilley without Carol; Deb Coombs; Kev with his camera held down at his side, not filming. Dale Pinkham had strung yellow tape across the top of the fuel-dock ramp and stood in front of it with his arms spread.

The ambulance was already there, its back doors open, its lights turning red and white in the fog.

Then Archie was on the dock, on his knees, with somebody's foil blanket around his shoulders crackling every time he breathed, and his whole body shaking so hard his teeth clacked. He was in his socks. One of them was gone. An EMT, a young man with a beard, was holding his wrist and looking at his watch and saying things Archie didn't listen to.

Hollis was on a stretcher three feet away, wrapped in a gray wool harbor blanket with a foil one over it, her face blue-white under the floodlights and her hair flat and dark with seawater. Two more EMTs were bent over her. Tess crouched at the head of the stretcher, soaked to the shoulder on one side, her braid dripping.

Hollis turned her head and found Archie.

"It was me," she said.

The EMTs went still.

"Not the girl. Not Ivy. Me. I put it on the horn, at the table, when Teddy asked me to move it. Wolfsbane. From my own garden." Her teeth were chattering, and she spoke through them, carefully. "For Peter. And for Danny. And for Shannon, because she'd started on Shannon—"

"Hollis." Tess's hand came down on the blanket over Hollis's shoulder. "Stop. Stop talking." Her voice was rough. "Hollis Tibbetts, you have the right to remain silent. Anything you say can be used against you in a court of law. You have the right to an attorney—"

"I know my rights, Theresa." A ghost of the old classroom voice, through blue lips. "I taught you to spell them."

"—and if you cannot afford an attorney one will be appointed for you. Do you understand these rights as I've read them to you?"

"Yes."

"Then I'm not going to ask you any questions. Not one. Not here." Tess didn't let go of the blanket. "You're going to the hospital, and you're going to get warm, and tomorrow when a doctor says you can, you and I will sit down with a lawyer in a room with the door shut, and you can tell me all of it then. Not on a dock. Do you hear me? Not here, Hollis."

Hollis looked up at her.

"You were always my best one at procedure," she said.

Then her hand came out from under the blankets, shaking, wet. It went into the pocket of the soaked cardigan and came out holding something that clanked: an iron ring the size of a dinner plate, with what looked like a hundred keys on it — brass and steel, long and short, every lock in the Merrow Opera House and half the doors on Main Street. Seawater ran off it onto the dock.

She held it out across the gap. Not to Tess. To Archie.

"Somebody has to run the lights," said Hollis.

He took it. It was so heavy his shaking arm dipped under it.

The EMTs moved then, all at once, and the stretcher came up, and they wheeled her up the ramp past Dale's tape and the people of Merrow standing silent along the railing. Archie saw Bev put her hand over her mouth, and Elwin Coombs take off his cap, and Shannon Mayo at the very end of the railing, with Rosie in her lobster suit asleep on her shoulder, tears running down her face and not wiping them away.

The ambulance doors closed. The lights turned away up Main Street into the fog.

The young EMT was trying to get Archie onto a second stretcher.

"No," said Archie.

"Sir, you were in fifty-degree water for—"

"Three minutes and forty seconds. I know. I counted." He could barely get the words out. "I'm all right. I've done worse." He got one foot under him. "I have a show."

"Sir—"

"I have a *show.*"

Tess was standing over him. She had Hollis's envelope in one hand, dark with seawater at one corner, and her other hand pressed flat against her own chest, and she was looking down at him with an expression he had never seen on her and didn't have time to read.

"You're out of your mind," she said.

"Probably." He held out his hand. "Phone. Please. Mine's in the harbor."

She gave him her phone.

He dialed with a thumb that didn't want to work. Lenny answered on the first ring. Behind him, faint and tinny, Archie could hear an audience laughing, and a piano, and his mother's voice, enormous, doing a bit of Gus's patter he hadn't heard since he was nine.

"She's alive," Archie said. "She's going to the hospital. Lenny, where are we?"

"Your mother's doing the Floating Lady with Corey from the Galley." Lenny's voice was flat. "He keeps waving to his mother."

"How long to the Chair?"

"Seven minutes. Maybe eight. Your mother's milking it."

"Put the chair on the trap. Keep Mother by it. I'm coming in from the alley."

A short silence on the line.

"You'll never make it," said Lenny.

"I've made it from worse. Seven minutes. Don't tell June."

He handed the phone back to Tess. Ozzie was at his elbow, still in his float coat, with his truck keys in his hand and the folded opera cloak over his arm.

"Truck's at the head of the wharf," said Ozzie. "I'll take you to the alley." He held out the cloak. "You'll want this."

Archie looked at it, folded square over Ozzie's arm, and had no time to wonder who had folded it.

"Keep it for me," he said. "Give it to my mother."

Then he got up off the dock in one sock and a foil blanket and a soaking-wet tailcoat, with a ring of a hundred keys clutched against his chest, and he ran — staggered, mostly, but forward — up the ramp past Dale's tape and through the people of Merrow, who stood aside for him without a word and closed in behind him to watch him go.

At the top of the ramp, Bev Thibodeau stepped out in front of him and put the pitcher of hot cider into his free hand.

"Drink that," she said. "Then go."

He drank. It burned all the way down. He gave her back the pitcher, and she pulled his foil blanket closed at the neck with one hard tug, like a mother at a school bus, and stepped aside.

Ozzie had the truck at the curb with the passenger door open. Archie fell into it, and Ozzie pulled out into the fog toward Main Street before the door was shut.


---

# Chapter 40

## The Empty Chair

Gus Fairweather had built the crawlspace in 1951, and he had built it for a man who was five foot eight.

The hatch was in the alley behind the opera house, under the iron back stairs: a square wooden door set into the stone foundation, painted the same gray as the stone, so that nobody had noticed it in seventy years. Archie had found it again in August with a flashlight and Lenny, and they'd crawled the length of it together on their elbows and come up through the trap in the middle of the stage covered in dust, and Archie had laughed so hard he'd had to lie on the boards for a minute, and Lenny had said, *Your grandfather was a short man with no regard for anybody's knees.*

Ozzie's truck stopped at the mouth of the alley. Archie got out.

The hatch was open. Somebody had propped it with a brick and left a flashlight on the ground beside it, switched on, pointing in.

He got down on his knees on the frozen gravel in his one sock and his foil blanket and his ruined tailcoat, with Hollis's keys clutched against his chest, and went in.

It was forty feet. He'd measured it in August. Forty feet of packed dirt and old stone, eighteen inches high in the low places, with water pipes and conduit running overhead and the dust of a hundred years on everything. The flashlight made a yellow tunnel ahead of him full of cobwebs. He went on his elbows and knees, dragging himself, the keys clanking on the stones every time he moved.
