He was so cold. He had stopped feeling his feet somewhere on Main Street. The shaking had gotten into his jaw and his hands and, worst of all, his elbows, which kept going out from under him. Twice he put his face in the dirt.

*Get up, Sparrow,* he thought, in a voice that wasn't his. *Watch my hands.*

He got up.

Over his head, through the floorboards of the stage, he could hear the show. It came down to him in pieces through the old pine: a piano playing something slow and minor — the Empty Chair music, Gus's music, which Lenny had found on a wax cylinder in the turret in September — and under the piano, the enormous waiting silence of three hundred people in the dark. And then, clear and steady and very close, a voice he knew better than his own.

"My great-grandfather," said June, "used to say that every trick is a promise."

He stopped crawling.

"He said that when a magician asks you to watch a chair — an ordinary chair, a chair you could buy at any yard sale in Maine — what he's really asking is for you to believe him. That something that isn't there can be there. That someone who's gone can come back." It was the patter he'd given her on Wednesday night in Lenny's workshop, but she'd changed it. She'd made it hers. "He did this trick on this stage every Halloween for forty years. The last time anyone saw him do it was thirty-seven years ago tonight."

Archie crawled faster. The flashlight beam jerked ahead of him. There was a thin bright square of light outlining the trapdoor overhead, and below it, crouched in the dirt in black velvet and diamonds with a second flashlight in her hand, his mother.

Vivienne turned and saw him coming, and put her hand over her mouth.

Lenny was beside her, on his knees under the trap, one hand on the iron release lever Gus had built in 1951 and Lenny had oiled every week since September. He looked at Archie in the flashlight — the foil blanket, the one sock, the dirt, the keys — and his face didn't change.

"Twelve minutes," said Lenny.

"I said seven."

"You said seven. You took twelve. June's been stalling for five." He jerked his head upward. "She's good at it."

Above them, through the boards: "Would you all help me count?"

Vivienne was already moving. She had been crouched directly under the trap, ready — her place, her cue, her chair; she had been coming up through Gus's trapdoors since she was twelve years old. She shifted aside in the dirt without a word, in her diamonds, and took Archie's face in her two cold hands for one second, and looked at him, and let him go.

"Go on, darling," she whispered. "It's your chair."

"*One,*" said three hundred people, overhead.

Archie got onto the short iron ladder under the back leaf of the trap. There was barely room to crouch; his knees were on the rungs, his head bent against the underside of the stage. The chair stood on the front leaf, a foot from his face. Through the crack along the back one he could see a hard white line of light — the spot, on the sheet.

"*Two.*"

Lenny's hand was on the lever. His other hand came out and gripped Archie's arm above the elbow, hard, through the wet sleeve of the tailcoat, and held it.

"You came back," said Lenny.

It was the only thing he said, and he didn't look at Archie when he said it. He let go and pulled the lever.

The back leaf dropped open on its counterweight without a sound. Above Archie, fallen to the boards behind the chair, was the long tail of the white sheet, glowing with the spotlight beyond it like a lampshade. He went up into it in one smooth motion, hands on the rim, feet on the rungs, and turned inside the cloth, under the hoop, and sat, in the chair, in the hot white light, dripping. Below him the leaf swung shut.

"*Three—*"

Through the sheet he could see June's shape beside the chair. He saw her hand come up and take hold of the cloth.

She pulled.

The sheet went up and back off him in one long white sweep, hoop and all, and the light hit him full in the face, and he sat there in it.

He knew what they were looking at. A tall, wet, filthy man in a dripping tailcoat and one sock, gray dust in his hair and down one side of his face, wrapped in a crackling silver foil blanket that caught the spotlight and threw it back in a hundred pieces like a cloak made of mirrors, with an iron ring of a hundred keys in his lap. They had been promised a grandmother. They didn't know where he'd been. To them it was the best trick anyone in Merrow had seen since 1988.

The sound began somewhere in the back and came forward like weather. Then they were on their feet, the whole hall, the roar of it going up into the pressed-tin ceiling and out through the tall dark windows into the fog.

June didn't move.

She stood beside the chair with the sheet hanging from her hand, in her great-grandfather's jacket, with her hair coming down, and looked at her father. Her face was white, and wet, and absolutely still. She knew where he had been.

"You're late," said June.

Her voice cracked right down the middle.

"I know," said Archie. "I'm sorry. I came the long way."

She dropped the sheet. She put both arms around his neck, foil blanket and seawater and dust and all, and held on — in front of three hundred people, the girl who wouldn't be dropped off at school in the hearse — and Archie Fairweather sat in his grandfather's empty chair in the middle of the Merrow Opera House and held his daughter, and did not vanish.

---

Afterward, when the curtain was down and the house lights were up and the noise out front had gone on so long that Lenny finally turned off the stage lights to make it stop, Archie found his mother in the stage-left wing.

She was standing by the rope rail in her black velvet, with dirt on the knees of it and her diamonds slightly askew. Ozzie Lund stood beside her in his float coat, saying nothing, with one hand at the small of her back.

Over her arm was Gus's opera cloak.

Vivienne held it out to him. She didn't say anything at first. She looked down at it — the black wool and the wine-red silk, folded in thirds with the lining out, the silver clasp precisely centered — and then up at her son.

"Ozzie says you did this," she said. "On the boat. Before you went in."

"I don't remember doing it."

"No," said his mother. "I don't suppose you do." She laid the cloak across his arms, carefully. "It's folded exactly the way my father used to fold it, Archibald. Exactly."


---

# Chapter 41

## Keys

At half past ten on Saturday morning Tess Calloway's gray Interceptor was parked in the dooryard at Fairweather House, exactly between two ruts, with the engine running.

She had come straight from Rockland. "Ivy Crane walked out at ten," she said, when he got in. "I dropped her at the Spindrift for her things." She pulled out onto Shore Road. "Hollis made her statement at eight. She's asking for you. Her lawyer's furious and she doesn't care."

Archie had a fever of a hundred, a cough, and Hollis's ring of keys in the pocket of his buffalo-check coat. Neither of them said anything else all the way to Pen Bay.

There was a state trooper on a chair outside room 214, reading a paperback. He stood up when he saw Tess.

"Twenty minutes," Tess said to Archie, and sat down in the trooper's chair. "And if her lawyer comes up those stairs, I wasn't here."

The room was small and bright, with a window over the parking lot and a long gray stripe of Penobscot Bay beyond it. Hollis was sitting up in bed in a hospital gown and somebody's blue cardigan, with an IV in the back of one hand. Somebody had brushed her hair.

She looked at him over the top of her reading glasses.

"Sit up straight," she said. "You look like you were dragged through a hedge backward."

"I was dragged through a harbor."

"That's no excuse for slouching. Sit. You've got a fever. I can see it from here."

He sat.

"How's June?" said Hollis.

"Mad at me."

"Good. She should be. Was she good, last night? Before you came up through the floor like a drowned rat?"

"She was wonderful."

"I knew she would be. She has your hands and her own head." Hollis looked out at the bay. "I'd have liked to see it." She folded her hands on the blanket. "Go on. Ask me. I've told Tess and a man from Augusta and a lawyer who says I'm making a terrible mistake, and I'll tell you, and then I'm done telling it."

"I know about Peter," Archie said. "I read her books. I know about Danny."

"Then you know the part that matters." She nodded once. "So I'll tell you the rest. I wrote to that producer in May to *expose* her. I want you to know that. In the spring she did a fisherman's widow in Gloucester on the television — the same horn, the same *it's warm here* — and I sat down that night and wrote to Los Angeles about Gus's theater and Gus's anniversary. I was going to stand up on her own camera with Peter's notebook and read it out. Every word."

"And then I came to town."

"And then you came to town, and I thought, oh, *better*. Archie Fairweather will catch her red-handed in front of the whole country." Her mouth tightened. "In July the town signed their contract. Two weeks after that, Shannon came to my kitchen and told me a woman from Lorelei Crane's show had found her on the computer and offered her a free reading. They'd been through the obituaries, looking for sitters." She looked at him. "My letter brought that woman to Shannon's door, Archie. I did that."

Archie didn't say anything.

"In September Shannon came for coffee. She'd had another reading. She sat at my table and told me, so happy, that Tommy had said he missed her on the water." Hollis's voice didn't change. "That's how it starts. That's exactly how it started with Peter. I gave her the whole unit — sixteen years I taught it, and nobody ever asked me why I'd added it. I gave her Gus's book. And she said, *Mrs. Tibbetts, I know it's probably not real, but it's the only time I get to hear his voice*, and she paid that woman another three hundred and fifty dollars." She looked at her hands. "You can't debunk a widow. Exposing her wouldn't have stopped Shannon. It wouldn't have stopped Peter. It would have made her famous for a week."

"So you dug up the garden."

"So I dug up the garden. I thought it would look like her heart. A woman her age, in the dark, at a séance — her fans would have said the spirits took her. I didn't think anybody in Merrow would know what a numb mouth meant. On Sunday morning I was unlocking the radiators and heard Tess say *wolfsbane* to you, and I went down to the green room and was sick in the sink."

"I watched you," Archie said. "From the back of the hall, with Dale, talking about a riverboat. I watched you set out the glasses and slide the horn. I was looking right at you. And I still don't know how you did it."

Hollis reached over with her free hand and took the lunch menu off the tray table.

"Pass me that cup."

He passed her the plastic water cup. She rolled the menu into a cone, quickly, with the narrow end tight, and set it on the tray table, and stood the cup a few inches from it.

"That's the candle," she said, tapping the cup. "I put it a hand from the horn on purpose. Thirty years I've lit that stage; I know what a flame does to polished tin on a camera. On Thursday, at the walkthrough, I watched that boy with the handheld flare on it twice. So on Saturday I set the candle where it would flare, and I waited for somebody to ask the woman with the matches to move the horn." She shrugged. "If Teddy hadn't, I'd have moved it myself. For fire safety. Nobody argues with fire safety."

"The gloves."

"Black cotton, out of the crew's box. And under the right one, a nitrile finger cot from the costume shop. You can't feel it through the cotton." She held up her left hand flat, palm up. "The tray. And under the tray, in the same hand, a tin about the size of a quarter. In the wings, I put one finger in it. Once." She picked up the paper cone with her right hand, the way anybody picks up a funnel, her forefinger hooked into the narrow end, and moved it six inches across the tray table, and set it down. "One second. Less. Then the gloves and the cot went in my cardigan pocket, and I went up to the booth and watched that table until the lights went out. If anybody else had picked it up, I'd have brought the work lights up and called a fire hold."

Archie looked at the paper cone on the tray table.

He had spent thirty years watching hands, and he felt what he always felt at a clean piece of work — the small, helpless lift in the chest — and under it, at the same moment, something cold, because the clean piece of work was a woman who had died a week ago, two floors down in this same building.

"It was the best trick I've ever seen," he said. "And I never saw it."

"Nobody looks at the stagehand, dear. Gus taught me that, in that very building. I was twenty-nine. And everybody said nobody fools Archie Fairweather. It was on the posters for your challenge. I used to like that."

"Well," said Archie. "You did."

"I did." She didn't sound proud. She didn't sound sorry, either. "I'd rather it had been anybody else."

"Why the bell, Hollis?"

She was quiet for a while.

"At a quarter to seven Dale's girl took a phone call at the back of the hall and looked straight up at my window. I taught her to read. I can read her. And there was a cruiser on Main Street with its lights off when I came in." She took off her glasses and folded them. "So they'd been through my house. And I could not stand to be walked out of Gus's theater in front of Shannon and those children. I thought I'd go and tell Peter first." She put the glasses on the blanket. "That's all I thought."

He waited. She didn't say anything else about it.

"Ivy," he said.

Her face changed then, for the first time.

"I told myself it was the scarf. A fine, a night in jail, back in California by the weekend. I told myself that for two days. Then on Thursday they said *murder*, and I came home from your rehearsal and wrote it all out, and I've never in my life been so ashamed of anything as I am of those two days." She looked at him. "Tell her that. Will you? I'd like her to hear it from somebody."

"I'll tell her."

"Would you do it again?" Archie said.

Hollis didn't answer for a long time.

"I'd like to tell you I wouldn't," she said finally. "I'm a schoolteacher. I know what the right answer is. I've graded it." She turned her head on the pillow. "Ask me in a year."

He nodded.

She patted his wrist, twice. Then, in the clipboard voice: "Now listen. I have three things for you, and then you're going to go and take something for that fever. One. The Players. Vivienne runs the costume shop already; she can run the rest. *Our Town* in March, and don't let them talk her into a musical. Two. Rosie. I'm her godmother. Somebody's got to do it properly now, from closer than Knox County. I'd like it to be your mother, if Shannon will have her. Ask Shannon, not Vivienne. Vivienne will say yes before she's finished hearing the question."

"And three?"

"Malvolio. Dale says he went in your front door like he owned it. He'll eat anything except fish. He'll sleep on your chest if you let him and on your face if you don't. Keep him, Archie."

"I'll keep him."

"Good." She let her head go back against the pillow and closed her eyes.

He stood up carefully, so the chair wouldn't scrape.

"Archie."

Her eyes were open again, on the ceiling.

"One more thing. Something I've wondered about for thirty-seven years, and I'd like to give it to somebody who'll wonder about it properly." She turned her head. "Gus's last night. I was in the booth; I ran the board for the Chair. Afterward he went up the center aisle in that cloak, shaking hands, and at the back door Walt Calloway was standing where he always stood, in his uniform, in case. And Gus stopped in front of him, and looked at him, and winked."

Archie didn't move.

"Just winked," said Hollis. "And went out, and walked down to the harbor, and we never saw him again." She closed her eyes. "I always wondered what that was about."

At the door the trooper was standing, and Tess was standing, and the twenty minutes were up.

He walked out to the car in the cold bright sun with Hollis's keys knocking against his hip. Tess started the engine and looked at him.

"Home," she said. It wasn't quite a question.

"The Galley," said Archie. "She gave me some things to deliver."


---

# Chapter 42

## Daylight

The fog had burned off by nine, and at noon, when Tess let him out at the head of the wharf and drove away without a word, the Galley was so full that people were eating standing up at the pie case.

When Archie came in, the bell over the door rang and the room went quiet, heads lifting, forks slowing. Then somebody at the counter started clapping, and somebody else did, and Bev Thibodeau said, "Oh, for heaven's sake, let the man sit down," and it stopped.

The liars' table had a chair pulled out.

Archie looked at it. He looked at Elwin Coombs, and Dickie Thibodeau, and Lionel, who was eating eggs and did not look up. Elwin pushed the chair another three inches out from the table with his boot.

Archie sat in it. Nobody said anything. Bev put a coffee in front of him and a piece of blueberry pie, at noon, without being asked.

"I didn't order pie."

"You're eating pie," said Bev.

He ate the pie.

---

Ivy Crane came in at a quarter past twelve, alone.

She was in the oversized gray sweater she'd been arrested in, two hours out of Knox County Jail, paler than ever, with a manila property envelope under one arm. She didn't look at anybody. She walked the length of the counter to where Shannon Mayo stood with a coffee pot, and the room watched her do it.

"Mrs. Mayo," said Ivy. "I'm Ivy Crane."

Shannon put the coffee pot down on the warmer.

Ivy took a check out of her sweater pocket and laid it on the counter between them. It was for one thousand and fifty dollars. "That's what you paid," she said. "For the readings. I did the research for them. I found Tommy's obituary, and the boat's name, and your Facebook page, and the pictures of your kids." She didn't look away. "Everything my mother told you about your husband, I gave her. None of it came from him. I'm sorry."

Shannon looked at the check. She didn't pick it up.

"I'm going to write to everyone," Ivy said. "Everyone who ever sat with her. There are thirty books. I'm going to give them back their money out of hers and tell them the truth. It'll take a long time." She took a breath. "I wanted you to be first. Because it was here."

When Shannon spoke, it was barely above the noise of the grill.

"Hollis did it for me. That's what they're saying. Because of what that woman told me on the phone."

Ivy didn't answer. There wasn't an answer.

Shannon looked down at the check. Then she folded it in half and put it in the pocket of her apron, and picked up the coffee pot, and went down the counter to refill the man at the end, who didn't need it. Her hand was shaking. She didn't spill a drop.

Ivy turned to go, and saw Archie, and stopped.

He got up. It hurt. He went down the counter to her, and said it low, so that it was hers.

"I saw Hollis this morning. She asked me to tell you something." He gave it to her plainly, all of it: the two days she'd told herself it was only the scarf, the kitchen table on Thursday night, and that she had never in her life been so ashamed of anything. "She wanted you to hear it from somebody."

Ivy didn't move. Her thumb moved once along the edge of the property envelope.

"Tell her I heard it," Ivy said, to the floor. "That's all. I don't have anything else yet."

"I'll tell her."

"Margaret Okafor says you're paying her."

"I am."

"I told you not to help me."

"You did," Archie agreed.

Her face moved toward a smile, and didn't get all the way there, and stayed halfway.

"Vermont," she said. "In January. Vet tech school. I'm going to clean out kennels and get bitten by Labradors." She shrugged. "Nobody's going to hold anybody's hand in the dark."

"Good," said Archie.

She went out. The bell rang behind her.

---

Shannon came to the liars' table ten minutes later with the coffee pot, and refilled his cup, and stood there.

"Liam wants to know if you'll teach him the card one," she said. "The one you did for him on Sunday."

"I will. Any time."

"I said I'd ask. That's all I said." She looked out the window at the moorings, bright in the cold sun. She didn't go.

"Hollis asked me something too," Archie said. "For Rosie. She said somebody ought to do it properly now, from closer than Knox County. She'd like it to be my mother." He kept his eyes on Shannon. "She said to ask you, not my mother. My mother would say yes before she'd finished hearing the question."

Shannon's mouth went tight. For a while she didn't say anything at all.

"Rosie's got a godmother," she said at last. "She's in Rockport with a trooper on her door."

"I know."

"I'm not choosing another one on a Saturday in the Galley." She shifted the pot to her other hand. "Your mother can come Sunday and help with Rosie's hair. Rosie likes her hats. We'll see after that."

She went back to the counter.

---

Warren Gilley came in at half past twelve in his camel coat and his bow tie and stopped in the doorway when he saw Archie.

Archie got up again and went to him before he could leave.

"I'm sorry," he said, low. "For what I said to you in here. There's no excuse, and I'm not going to offer one."

Warren's face stayed set like plaster. "Noted."

Archie held out the cardboard tube he'd had under his arm all morning. Inside, rolled in tissue, were two posters seventy years old: THE GREAT FAIRWEATHER WALKS THROUGH WALLS! and RADIO'S MASTER OF MYSTERY. A man in Philadelphia and a woman in Connecticut had sold them back in the last week, and been very reasonable about it.

"These are from my mother," Archie said. "They were Gus's, and they're hers to give, and she wants Carol to have them. For her room. She says Carol used to sit in the front row every Halloween when she was a girl, with her hair in braids."

"I won't take anything from you," said Warren.

"They're not for you," said Archie. "They're for Carol."

Warren looked at the tube. Then he took it and held it in both hands against the front of his good coat.

"For Carol," he said, and went out without his coffee.

---

At one o'clock the liars' table stood and put on its caps.

Elwin Coombs stopped beside Archie's chair. He looked down at him, and over at Dickie, and at Lionel, who gave the smallest nod.

"That trick," said Elwin. "The chair. Last night."

"Yes?"

"How'd you do it?"

The Galley went quiet again. Archie looked up at the three of them — Elwin's weathered red face, Dickie's suspenders, Lionel, who had stood up in front of the whole town last night and told them his name — and thought about a crawlspace forty feet long, and a girl's voice coming down through the floorboards, and an old man's hand on his arm in the dark.

"I'll never tell you," Archie said. "Not as long as I live."

Elwin considered that. Then he nodded, once, with deep satisfaction.

"Correct answer," said Elwin Coombs, and the liars' table went out into the sunshine.


---

# Chapter 43

## The Memorial

At five o'clock the sun went down behind the town, and the harbor went from gold to pewter to slate, and Archie Fairweather was standing at the end of the town wharf in front of a block of granite with his hands in his pockets.

He had walked home from the Galley and slept for three hours with the cat on his chest, and woken in a house so quiet — his mother asleep upstairs, June out with Mattie, Lenny's door shut — that he'd gotten up and put his coat back on. He'd walked down past the Galley, closed now, and the bait shed and the stacked crates and the fuel dock, where a scrap of yellow tape still fluttered on the ramp rail, and kept walking until there wasn't any more wharf.

The granite was cold. He had put his hand on it without meaning anything by it, and left it there.

*Peter Langley 2003.* It was about two-thirds of the way down the stone, in letters a little worn but still sharp. He had read it twice since August, once with Shannon and once with Tess, and he hadn't seen it either time, because there had been nothing to see. It was a name on a stone, in a town that had been cutting names into this stone since 1847.

At the bottom, alone, the newest name was still pale inside the letters.

He heard her coming before he saw her: boots on the planks, unhurried, all the way down the wharf from the lot. He didn't turn around. She stopped beside him, a little to one side, and looked at the granite, and didn't say anything while the bell buoy rang twice out in the dusk.

"They found Peter's notebook," Tess said. "In her piano bench, under the sheet music. Forty-one Thursdays." She was quiet a moment. "I read it this afternoon. I'm not going to read it again."

Archie nodded.

"I knew Danny," she said. "Danny Langley. He was a grade ahead of me. He played Puck in the summer show at the opera house when I was twelve, and he got stuck on the rope coming down out of the flies, and he just hung there in the air laughing until everybody in the building was laughing with him." She looked at the name. "I'd forgotten that. I'd completely forgotten it until yesterday."

"I'm sorry."

"I'm not telling you so you'll be sorry." She turned and looked at him then. "I'm not going to tell you what you risked, either. Bev's told you. Brannock's going to tell you on Monday, in writing, and you're going to sign it." Her voice was low and level. "I want to tell you what it was like."

"Okay."

"I had your collar in my hand." She looked down at her right hand, and opened it, and closed it. "Ozzie was on the line and the swell had us and you had her, and I had your collar. And after about a minute I couldn't feel my fingers. I couldn't feel anything. I didn't know if I was still holding on. I had to look at my hand to find out." She stopped. "I kept looking. All the way in. I looked at my hand the whole way in."

Archie looked at it now. There was a raw red line across the backs of her fingers where the gunwale had been, and the knuckles were split.

The bell buoy rang. A gull came down onto a piling at the end of the wharf and folded itself up and looked at them.

Archie didn't make a joke. He thought of three. He didn't make any of them.

"I should have called you at five in the morning from the Galley," he said. "Bev told me to. I didn't, because I wanted to hear it from Hollis first, and then because she asked me for the show and I wanted her to have it. I wanted her to have Gus's lights." He looked at the granite. "That was for me. Not for her. I'm sorry, Tess."

She looked at him. Some of the stiffness went out of her shoulders. Not all of it.

"Brannock wants to know if you'd consult," she said.

He stared at her.

"Officially. On what he calls *the weird ones*." The corner of her mouth moved. "He says if this is going to keep happening in his jurisdiction he'd rather have you inside the tent than outside it setting things on fire. He said it this morning at the command post, standing in front of your space heater, which he has not sent back to Portland." She shrugged. "I told him I'd ask."

"What do you think I should say?"

"I think you should think about it for longer than it takes you to say yes."

"Yes," said Archie.

She closed her eyes briefly. When she opened them she wasn't angry anymore.

"Hold out your hand," she said.

"Why?"

"Just hold it out."

He took his left hand out of his coat pocket and held it out. She took it by the wrist and turned it palm down. Then she reached into her own coat pocket and took out a watch.

It was his. It was Gus's — an old steel Hamilton with a cracked crystal and a worn leather strap, which had been on his grandfather's wrist at every show for forty years and on Archie's since he was nineteen. He hadn't noticed it was gone. He looked at his bare wrist, and then at the watch in her hand, and for a second he was eight years old at a birthday party, watching a man pull a dove out of an empty hat.

"You were in the water," said Tess. "When I got hold of you. You had one arm around Hollis and the other on the gunwale, and the strap was half off your wrist. It was going to go to the bottom." She was buckling it onto him as she talked, her cold fingers working the old leather through the buckle. "So I took it."

"You *lifted* it."

"I took it."

"Off a drowning man. In the middle of a rescue. With one hand." He was grinning now. "Sergeant Calloway. That's a *lift*."

"I've watched every one of your specials," said Tess. "Some of them twice." She pulled the strap through the keeper and settled the watch on his wrist and, for a second, didn't let go. Her fingers stayed on the inside of his wrist, cold, over his pulse, which he was fairly sure she could feel. She was close enough that he could see each eyelash, and the gray of her eyes, and the place at the corner of her mouth that had almost smiled.

She let go.

"Don't tell anyone," said Tess.

He had said those words a thousand times, to a thousand volunteers, at the end of a trick. They went into him now and stopped somewhere, like something he had known once and put down and couldn't, for the life of him, remember where.

"Tess—"

But she had already turned. She walked away up the wharf, unhurried, her braid swinging and her hands in her coat pockets, past the fuel dock and the crates and the dark windows of the Galley, toward the lights of the town. She didn't look back. He was almost certain she was smiling.

Archie stood alone at the end of the wharf with his grandfather's watch ticking on his wrist and his own pulse going under it where her fingers had been. Out past the point, Merrow Light flashed green, and went out, and flashed again, and the bell buoy off Shag Ledge rang once, and then again, slow, on a calm sea.


---

# Chapter 44

## Sparrow

Supper on Saturday night was just supper, which at Fairweather House had never once happened before.

Nobody tried to cook anything clever. Vivienne made a pot of the soup she'd been making since 1971, which had no name and no recipe. Ozzie Lund brought bread from the bakery in Camden and stood in the kitchen doorway holding it until somebody told him to sit down. Lenny came in from the carriage house wearing one sweater fewer than usual, which in Lenny was the equivalent of a party hat. And at seven o'clock the back door opened without a knock and Bev Thibodeau came in out of the dark with a pie in each hand and set them on the counter.

"Blueberry," she said, "and apple."

"You brought both," said Archie.

"There's no third pie," said Bev, and sat down, and let June take her coat.

They ate at the scarred farm table with the windows black, and the jug of blue flowers gone from the middle of it, and nobody mentioned why. They talked about the show. They talked about Lionel. Ozzie told a long, slow story about a summer person who had once tried to moor a forty-foot yacht to the bell buoy, and Vivienne laughed until she had to put her spoon down, and Ozzie looked at her laughing as if she'd invented it.

Malvolio sat on the table.

He had arrived at midnight the night before in a cardboard box, and in eighteen hours he had established that the radiator under the kitchen window was his, that Archie's chest was a bed, and that the farm table was a place where a large gray cat might reasonably sit among the bread and the butter dish with his paws folded under him, watching the soup.

"There is a cat," said Lenny, "on the table."

"He's not on the table," said Archie. "He's adjacent to the table."

"He's on the table."

"He's visiting."

"Hollis said he'll eat anything except fish," said June.

"Then he's the only sensible creature in this house," said Lenny, and gave Malvolio a piece of bread without looking at him.

June was sitting across from Archie. She had said almost nothing to him all day. She'd slept until noon and gone out with Mattie and come back at six with her hair blown everywhere and her cheeks red from the wind, and when he'd said *Hi, Junebug* she'd said *Hi* and gone upstairs. He'd let her. He hadn't known what else to do.

But she was watching him now, across the soup, the way she watched an opponent across a debate table in the minute before the round began.

When the pie was gone and Bev had gone and Ozzie was drying dishes he hadn't been asked to dry, June put down her fork.

"I'm still mad at you," she said.

"I know."

"I'm going to be mad at you for a really long time."

"I know. You said."

"Okay." She looked at him a moment longer. Then she stood up. "Can I try something?"

---

The turret stairs were narrow and steep and turned twice, and halfway up, on the landing under the little round window, June stopped and turned around. He was two steps below her, which made them the same height.

"The Glass Box," she said. "In Vegas. How did you get out?"

The answer was right there. It had been there in a hotel room in Boston over a plate of room-service fries, and it was charming, and it was two words long, and it meant *no*.

He sat down on the step.

He told her. It took about four minutes, and he used his hands, and the banister, and a button off his own cuff, and she stood on the landing with her arms folded and didn't interrupt once. When he was finished she was quiet for a while.

"That's it?" she said.

"That's it. And eleven months of Lenny in a garage in Henderson, Nevada."

June nodded slowly, filing it somewhere. Then she turned and went on up the stairs, and he got up and followed her.

---

The turret was cold. It always was. Archie turned on the green-shaded lamp on Gus's rolltop desk, and the round room came up out of the dark around them — the shelves of conjuring books, the framed posters of the Great Fairweather sawing ladies in half and holding doves with tremendous dignity, the black windows on every side with the harbor in them and the town's lights scattered on the water.

And the trunk, under the north window, with its faded gold stars and its seven brass dials and the yellowed card on the lid. *To be opened when I'm gone. Not before.*

June knelt down in front of it.

He knew what she was going to do before her hand went out. He'd known since Tuesday night, when he'd sat on this floor himself and spelled out three letters — S, P, A — and stopped, because he would not let a woman who'd lied to grieving people for thirty years open his grandfather's trunk with a word she'd found in an old newspaper.

"June," he said. "That's her word."

June looked up at him.

"No, it isn't," she said. She took a folded sheet out of her cardigan pocket and held it up: a photocopy, gray and speckled, off the microfilm reader at the library. "Mattie and I went this afternoon. Deb opened up for us."

He didn't need to read it. He had read it upside down, from a wall, behind a pink tab in Ivy Crane's binder. *His grandson, Archie, whom he called his "little sparrow."*

"It was never her word," said June. "A reporter wrote it down in 1988 because Grandpa Gus said it to him." She laid the photocopy on the floor beside her knee. "It was always his. She just said it out loud. That's all she did." She put her fingers on the first brass dial. "Somebody has to say it out loud, Dad. You weren't going to."

He stood in the lamplight and didn't stop her.

She turned the dials one at a time. Each one clicked under her thumb as it went past each letter, a tiny, precise sound.

S.

P.

A.

R.

R.

O.

W.

She took hold of the brass hasp, and looked up at him once, and pulled.

The lock opened.

It opened so easily, with such a small and ordinary *clack*, that for a second neither of them moved. Then the door at the top of the stairs creaked, and his mother was standing in it with one hand on the frame, in her kaftan and her tweed coat.

June lifted the lid.

The trunk smelled of cedar and mothballs and, faintly, impossibly, of pipe tobacco and Brylcreem. Inside lay a pair of white kid opera gloves, yellowed, folded one on the other. Beside them was a deck of cards in a red case, worn soft at the corners, and beside that a square of heavy cream paper folded once, with one word on the outside in a big, looping, theatrical hand that Archie had last seen on a birthday card when he was ten.

*Sparrow.*

His hands weren't steady. He let June unfold it.

*Sparrow —*
*You remembered.*
*That's the whole trick.*
*— G.*

June read it out loud, and then read it again silently, and handed it to him without a word, and he stood holding it in the lamplight and for the moment could not see it very well.

"There's something else," said June.

It had been lying on top of everything, the last thing put in, face down. A postcard. June turned it over.

It was a picture postcard of a sunset, the kind they sell in racks on boardwalks: an orange sun sinking into a flat turquoise sea between two palm trees, and across the top in yellow cartoon letters, GREETINGS FROM KEY WEST. The colors had faded. One corner was soft from somebody's pocket.

June turned it over again.

It had been mailed. There was a thirty-two-cent stamp with a flag on it, and across the stamp a round black postmark, slightly smudged but perfectly legible: KEY WEST FL. MAR 14 1996.

It was addressed in the same big, looping hand: *Master Archie Fairweather, Fairweather House, Gull Point, Merrow, Maine.*

And on the left side, four lines:

*Sparrow —*
*Still working on the trick.*
*Don't let your mother sell the house.*
*— G.*

Nobody in the turret said anything.

Archie read the postmark three times. *1996.* He'd been eighteen, a year from his folding table in Harvard Square. Gus Fairweather had been gone eight years. The whole town had stood on the wharf in the rain for the memorial service. Walt Calloway had closed the case.

And somebody who knew the word had come into this house, sometime after the fourteenth of March, 1996, and opened this trunk, and put a postcard in it, and closed it again.

"That's his hand," said Vivienne, from the doorway. "That's my father's hand."

She came into the room and took the postcard from June and held it under the lamp. Archie had seen her play Lady Macbeth and Mother Courage and, once, unforgettably, a talking teapot, and he had never seen her look at anything the way she looked at that card.

"That old *showman,*" said Vivienne Fairweather.

She sat down very suddenly on the edge of Gus's desk chair.

"I told you," she said, to nobody, to the room. "I told you all. Nobody had opened it. I told you to draw your own conclusions."

Archie looked down at the note in his own hand. *You remembered. That's the whole trick.*

And he did remember — not 1988, not a kitchen with a screen door, but the night before. The fog off Shag Ledge. His own hands, without any instruction from him, unclasping his grandfather's opera cloak and folding it in thirds and laying it on the seat before he went over the side.

Thirty-seven years ago, at dawn, Walt Calloway's men had found Gus's skiff drifting near Merrow Light with nobody in it. And on the seat, in every story anybody ever told about that night, his black opera cloak.

Folded.

His mother was holding the postcard against her chest with both hands. June was kneeling by the open trunk in the lamplight, looking up at him, and her face wasn't mad at him at all just then. It was lit up from the inside like a struck match — the way his own must have been when he was four years old and a big man with silver hair crouched down in front of him in a summer kitchen and said *Watch closely, Sparrow.*

Archie grinned.

"Junebug," he said. "Get your coat."

June was already on her feet.

"Where are we going?"

"Sea View," said Archie, reaching for the opera cloak. "There's a man there I need to see about a boat."
